# pigy implementation plan

Draft for review, 2026-11-14.

pigy is an invite-only web app for writing a post once and publishing it to LinkedIn, Mastodon, Bluesky and Threads. It runs on Cloudflare Workers, installs on phones as a PWA, works offline, and has a REST API with API keys.

## 1. Decisions

### From your answers

| Area | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Publishing | Publish now, save as draft, schedule for an exact time, and a queue with recurring weekly slots |
| Content | Text can be overridden per network. Threads are published as reply chains on Bluesky, Mastodon and Threads, and joined into one post on LinkedIn |
| Media | Images and video |
| API | REST with an OpenAPI spec, using Buffer's resource names, fields and enums |
| Stack | TypeScript backend, local-first frontend that feels like Linear |
| Extras in the first version | Push notifications for failures, share-to-pigy from the phone share sheet, engagement stats |
| Sandbox | Simulated networks that work in the UI, so people can try pigy before connecting accounts. Signed-in users only, no public demo |
| Tests | CI runs against simulated networks and never calls the real services |
| Queue | One posting schedule per user, shared by all accounts. A cross-post goes out everywhere at the same time |
| Domain | Start on the free `workers.dev` address that Cloudflare gives every Worker |
| Later | LinkedIn company pages, a custom domain |

### Made on your behalf

| Area | Decision | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Sign-in | Google only | The allowlist is keyed on a verified email. Bluesky gives no email, and a second provider adds a second identity to reconcile. LinkedIn sign-in can be added later in a few lines |
| Sign-in code | `oauth4webapi` for the OpenID Connect flow, and sessions of our own on D1. Not Better Auth, which an earlier draft of this plan named | Code that talks to external services gets `fetch` and the clock as parameters (section 6), so that tests and local development run against simulators and never reach a real host. Better Auth has no way to pass a `fetch` to the requests it makes to the provider: discovery, token exchange and signing keys. `oauth4webapi` takes one. Sessions are a small, well-documented pattern (section 11) |
| Invite-only | Email allowlist stored in the database, managed from an admin page | No redeploy to invite a friend. Removing an email signs that person out |
| Frontend | React, Vite, TanStack Router, Tailwind, shadcn/ui, and a small client store of our own for the synced data | The speed comes from reading local data, not from the framework. Section 4 says why the store is not a library |
| Data | One Durable Object with SQLite per user and environment, D1 for sign-in and global data | Local-first sync needs ordered, transactional writes per user, which a Durable Object gives for free |
| Publishing | Cloudflare Workflows, started by Durable Object alarms | Publishing is multi-step and must survive retries without double-posting |
| Sandbox shape | A Live/Sandbox switch per user, like Stripe's test mode | Sandbox data, accounts and API keys stay fully separate from real ones |

## 2. Terms

- **Workspace**: one user's data in one environment (live or sandbox).
- **Channel**: a connected social account. This is Buffer's term.
- **Post**: one piece of content, made of one or more parts.
- **Part**: one item of a thread. An ordinary post has a single part.
- **Target**: the delivery of a post to one channel. It has its own status, optional text override, result links and stats. The override is a text of its own, with its own parts.

## 3. Architecture

```
 Browser / installed PWA                      Cloudflare Worker "pigy"
┌──────────────────────────────┐          ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ React UI                     │  HTTPS   │ Hono router                             │
│ Sync store (rows in memory)  │◄────────►│  /api/auth/*   sign-in with Google      │
│ IndexedDB (rows + outbox)    │          │  /api/admin/*  the allowlist            │
│                              │          │  /api/v1/*     public REST API          │
│ Service worker (shell, push) │◄────────►│  /sync/*       WebSocket to WorkspaceDO │
└──────────────────────────────┘ WebSocket│  /connect/*    OAuth to the networks    │
                                          │  /m/*          signed media URLs        │
                                          │  /sim/*        simulated services       │
                                          └────┬─────────────┬──────────────┬───────┘
                                               │             │              │
                                   ┌───────────▼───┐   ┌─────▼──────┐  ┌────▼─────┐
                                   │ WorkspaceDO   │   │ D1         │  │ R2       │
                                   │ per user+env  │   │ users      │  │ media    │
                                   │ posts         │   │ sessions   │  └──────────┘
                                   │ channels      │   │ allowlist  │
                                   │ queue, stats  │   │ API keys   │
                                   │ change log    │   └────────────┘
                                   │ alarms        │
                                   └───────┬───────┘
                                           │ starts
                                   ┌───────▼────────┐  provider adapters  ┌──────────────────┐
                                   │ PublishWorkflow│────────────────────►│ live: real APIs  │
                                   │ durable steps  │   injected fetch    │ sandbox: /sim    │
                                   └────────────────┘                     └──────────────────┘
```

Everything is one Worker deployment. The React app is served as Worker static assets.

A sandbox workspace has a second Durable Object next to its `WorkspaceDO`, the `SimWorldDO`: the simulated networks as that workspace sees them. Section 7 has it.

### Repository layout

```
apps/web          React app, service worker, PWA manifest
apps/worker       Hono routes, Durable Objects, Workflows, D1 migrations
packages/core     Shared domain code: schemas, mutators, validation, text utilities, limits
packages/providers   The contract with a network, and the LinkedIn, Mastodon, Bluesky and Threads adapters
packages/simulators  Simulated versions of the four networks, Google sign-in and web push
e2e               Playwright tests
```

Tooling: pnpm workspaces, strict TypeScript, Biome, Vitest, Playwright, Wrangler, the Cloudflare Vite plugin, Drizzle for both D1 and Durable Object SQLite.

## 4. Local-first sync

The UI never waits for the network. It reads from memory, writes locally first, and syncs in the background. [SYNC.md](SYNC.md) is the protocol between client and server, in enough detail to write a client from.

- **Source of truth**: one `WorkspaceDO` per workspace. All writes go through it in a single order.
- **Client state**: every row of the workspace is held in memory by the sync store and stored in IndexedDB, one database per user and workspace. A cold start renders from IndexedDB before any request finishes: the device remembers who was signed in on it, and the server is asked in the background whether that still holds (section 11).
- **Writes**: named mutators such as `post.create`, `part.setText` and `post.schedule` live in `packages/core`. They are synchronous functions over a small transaction interface: read a row, find rows by a column, write a row, delete a row, the time. The client runs them optimistically. The Durable Object runs the same code as the authority. The REST API calls the same mutators, so an API write shows up in open tabs immediately.
- **Idempotency**: IDs are generated on the client (UUIDv7). Each client numbers its mutations, and the Durable Object remembers the last number applied per client, so a replay is a no-op and a gap is refused.
- **Transport**: one WebSocket per device and workspace, to `/sync/<workspace ID>`, using Durable Object hibernation so idle connections cost nothing. A device syncs the workspace that it shows, live or sandbox, and the other one only once it has been shown, or while its copy holds changes that were not sent: section 7. The tabs of a browser share one copy of a workspace in IndexedDB. The tab that holds a Web Lock holds the socket, and when it is closed the next tab takes over. Every tab writes its own mutations to the shared copy and tells the others over a BroadcastChannel what it wrote. The Durable Object holds a client to one socket: a second socket of the same client closes the first.
- **Catch-up**: every committed write gets an increasing sequence number and an entry in a change log, which names the rows that changed. A reconnecting client sends its last sequence number and receives the current state of the rows that changed since. The log covers the last 1,000 changes: a client that is further behind gets a fresh snapshot.
- **Confirmation**: the message that carries the changes of a client's mutation also says that the mutation was processed, so the client can replace its own version of the change with the server's in one step.
- **Offline**: mutations queue in an IndexedDB outbox and are sent in order on reconnect. Mutations that only repeat one another, such as the text of a part on every keystroke, become one while they wait. A rejected mutation rolls back, and a notice says what was refused and why. The notice is not on the post, as an earlier draft of this plan had it: the post is often gone, which is why the mutation was refused. The Durable Object keeps the reason until the client has seen it, so the notice is shown also when the answer that carried it was lost, and the client keeps it until the person has dismissed it.
- **Conflicts**: the server applies mutations in arrival order, last write wins per field. State guards reject impossible edits, such as changing a post that is already sending.
- **Secrets**: channel credentials are never synced. Each table has an explicit list of synced columns, and a test fails if a credential field appears in any sync message.
- **Mutators of the server's own**: a row that stands for something only the server has is changed by mutators that a client may not send. A channel is one: it is made and removed together with its credentials, over HTTP. What a delivery has done is another: a client asks for a post to be published, and the server says how far each target has come. Clients receive what such a mutator did like any other change.
- **Time**: the `WorkspaceDO` runs every mutation at a later time than the one before it, also the mutations of one message and across restarts, so that the times in the rows say in which order they were changed.
- **Versions**: the protocol and the synced schema each have a version number. A client that says hello with another version is told so, and updates itself. Section 13 says how the new version of the app gets onto a device.

**The client store.** The store is our own code, not a library. `packages/core/src/sync/client` holds everything that needs no browser, and `apps/web/src/sync` what does: IndexedDB, the BroadcastChannel, the Web Lock, the WebSocket and the React hooks.

- **The view** is computed, never stored: the rows of the server with the unconfirmed mutations run on top, by the mutators that the server runs. A patch changes the rows of the server, and the next view has the unconfirmed mutations on the new rows. A mutation that the new rows no longer allow drops out of the view, the way the server will reject it. Each mutation is run at the time it was made by the clock of the device, or one millisecond after the latest time in the rows of the server and the time of the mutation before it, whichever is later: the server will run it after all of those, so a list that is sorted by time keeps its order when the confirmation arrives, also on a device whose clock is behind. A workspace has hundreds to a few thousand rows, so computing the view again on every change costs nothing that anybody notices.
- **Every change of the stored copy is one transaction** of IndexedDB, described by a pure function from what is stored to what to write: applying a patch, adding a mutation and giving it its ID, marking mutations as sent, starting over. The same description of the write goes to the other tabs, which apply it to what they hold in memory. A tab that finds it has missed one reads the copy again. If it finds out by storing a mutation of its own and the read then fails, the mutation is stored all the same: the tab goes on showing it and reads again later, and never stores it a second time, which would make it another mutation under another ID. The messages only make the tabs find out sooner: a tab can be closed after it has stored a mutation and before its message has left, so the tab with the socket also looks at the stored copy with every ping and whenever the browser says that the network or the tab has changed.
- **What is decided in the transaction.** A write waits its turn behind other work on the storage, and the copy can change in that time. So whether a write is still wanted is decided inside its transaction, on what is stored then. A patch is not written for a socket that the tab has given up, or for a copy that has started over under another client ID. And nothing that came from the server is written to a copy whose schema is newer than the tab: a tab that was open before another one loaded the next version of the app says "Reload to update" and gives the socket up. What the person does in such a tab is still stored. Its mutations are of the old schema, like those that the copy held when it was brought to the new one, and section 6 of SYNC.md says what becomes of them. Refusing them would lose typing to the reload that the tab asks for. So one outbox can hold mutations of two schemas, and each mutation in it says which schema it was made under: a mutation takes the place of the one before it only if both are of the same one. A name and its arguments can mean something else in another schema, and the server may reject one where it takes the other.
- **A mutation that cannot be stored**, on a device that is out of space, for example, stays in the memory of its tab and on screen. It is not sent, because the tab with the socket sends what is stored. The app does not call it saved: the indicator says that it is not, which is another matter than the connection, and signing out counts it among the changes that would be lost, in whichever tab it is. Closing the tab would lose it too, and so would loading it again or leaving it for the consent page of a network: while a tab holds such a mutation, of either workspace, the browser asks before the page goes. The question covers a page that the person leaves. A session that ends by itself, because it expired or the invitation was withdrawn, waits for such a mutation. The tab that finds out records on the device at once that the session is over, so that no tab that is opened from then on shows the copies as if it were good, and the other tabs of the person hear of the end in that moment. Each of them then stores what it holds before it leaves for the sign-in page or starts again, and the copy is kept with it, section 16: a write that was under way is over in a moment, and nobody sees a difference. A write that fails is another matter, because the store can try for as long as the device has no room. After two seconds the tab says, in a dialog over the app that cannot be closed, that the person has been signed out, that it holds changes which it has not saved and goes to the sign-in page once they are, and that they are lost if it goes now. It offers that, and nothing else: no time that runs out discards the changes, and the person is the only one who can. Nothing of the workspace behind the dialog can be used meanwhile, and nothing is sent for the session that is over. The server would not see to that: it refuses a new socket, and goes on serving one that was open when the session ended, for up to five minutes (`SYNC.md` section 2). So a tab that learns of the end closes its socket and leaves the line for it, before it waits, and what it stores during the wait stays on the device. A wait can last, and what was true when it began may not be when it ends: the same person can have signed in again in another tab. A tab that waits is not moved by that, because starting again would lose its changes for nothing. When its wait is over it looks at the device again, and only if the device still holds the session that it found over, as ended, does it go on to the sign-in page. Otherwise it ends nothing and starts again as whoever is signed in. A tab that the browser had put aside can hear of the end and of the new session of the same person at once, when it wakes: it has not waited, and it starts again too, but only once it has stored what it holds, because nothing presses and the page load would lose the change without anybody being asked. The wait is not for somebody else who signs in: what a device holds of one person goes when another signs in, stored or not, so the tab starts again at once, as it does when the person signs out or has the kept changes deleted, and it does nothing to the session that the device holds by then. Signing out in one tab makes the others start again, so a tab tells the others how many such mutations it holds. That goes to every tab of the person, whichever of the two workspaces it shows, and to the other workspace that the same tab has open, over a channel of its own: the channel on which the tabs tell each other of their writes is that of one copy, and reaches only the tabs that have that copy open. Before the count is used, the tabs that are in it are asked again, for at most two seconds. A tab says that it holds none when it has stored them, and when it is closed or leaves the app. One that does not answer keeps the count it gave last: a browser freezes tabs in the background, and a frozen tab still holds what it could not store. A tab that died without saying so is therefore counted until the other tabs are loaded again, which costs a question and no change. The store tries again every few seconds and with every new mutation. Mutations that only repeat one another become one in the memory of the tab as they do in the outbox, by the same rule: what is typed into a draft is one change that is not saved, however many keys it took. A mutation is in the memory of its tab and nowhere else from the moment it is made until it is stored, also while it waits its turn to be written or is being written, which the count above leaves out. So a tab can be asked to say when it holds no mutation that is not stored, in either workspace, and whatever loads a tab again by itself, as a new version of the app does (section 13), waits for that.
- **React** reads the store through `useSyncExternalStore`: `useSynced(select)` for rows, `useMutate()` to change them. No screen has a loading state for synced data.

Alternatives considered:

| Option | Verdict |
| --- | --- |
| TanStack DB, with its offline transactions | Tried in a spike at the start of phase 2b, and not used. See below |
| Zero | Needs Postgres and its own server process, so it cannot run on Workers alone |
| LiveStore | Event-sourced. The scheduler and REST API would have to take part in the event log, which is a larger change of model than this app needs |
| Replicache | Fits well, but is in maintenance mode, and it brings a protocol of its own, which the server does not speak |

**Why not TanStack DB.** An earlier draft of this plan named it as the client store, with our own store as the fallback if a spike showed that it did not fit. The spike ran `@tanstack/db` 1.0.0-rc.1.5 and `@tanstack/offline-transactions` 1.2.1 against the model of SYNC.md, and they do not fit it:

- A pending change is kept as a snapshot of the whole row, laid over whatever the server sends. In the spike, a post with a pending edit went on showing as a draft after the server had changed its status, and went on showing at all after the server had deleted it. Our protocol needs the pending mutator to run again on the new rows, where it would have refused. Getting that from the library means computing the view ourselves and feeding it in as if it came from the server, which leaves the library as a container for rows.
- Its outbox holds row changes with idempotency keys, in a storage of its own. SYNC.md needs named mutations with consecutive IDs, dropped in the same transaction that stores the rows of the patch that confirms them. Two storages cannot do that, and the outbox would be a second source of truth for what is pending.
- Only the tab that leads has the durable outbox. The other tabs run without it, so a mutation made offline in a second tab does not survive a reload.
- Its persistence for rows in the browser is SQLite compiled to WebAssembly, over OPFS, per collection: more to load than this store is long, and the cursor would still be stored somewhere else.

## 5. Data model

**D1 (global)**

| Table | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `user` | Google account ID, which is the identity, and the email and name as of the last sign-in |
| `session` | ID, SHA-256 of the session secret, user, expiry |
| `allowed_email` | Email, role (admin or member), who invited them |
| `workspace` | ID, owner, environment (live or sandbox), and since when it is frozen because its owner lost their access, since phase 11b: section 6 |
| `api_key` | Workspace, name, visible prefix, SHA-256 hash of the secret, scopes, last used, expiry, revoked. Since phase 6: section 12 |
| `push_subscription` | The devices that get push messages: the endpoint and the keys that a browser handed out when somebody turned notifications on in it, with the session that did, the public key of pigy that it subscribed with, and the address that pigy was reached at. A row goes with its session, a session has one, and a person ten at most. Section 13 |
| `upload_use` | How many requests of an upload each person has made in the minute that is running, as `preview_use` counts the previews. Section 10 |
| `mastodon_app` | OAuth client per Mastodon instance: one for all users of the live environment, and one for each simulated instance of every sandbox, whose world is its own. Sealed, see section 16. A cache: the adapter replaces an entry that the instance no longer knows, and one that no longer opens |
| `oauth_state` | What an adapter needs to finish a connection that has begun, kept under the `state` of the callback for ten minutes, with the session that began it and the workspace it is for. It holds secrets, a PKCE verifier and for Mastodon the client secret, so it is sealed too |

**WorkspaceDO SQLite (per workspace)**

| Table | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `channel` | Network, account ID, handle, name, avatar, profile link, status, when it was connected, when to refresh the access and when it ends, sealed credentials, and the limits of the account with the time at which its network was asked for them. Not synced: the access that the person was told the end of, so that a reminder is given once per access (section 13) |
| `post` | Status, timestamps, and since phase 5 what says when a scheduled post goes out: the mode (a time that somebody chose, or the queue), the due time, the place in the queue, when the server held it because it found its time too late, and when somebody put it aside, which is the first step of deleting it from the queue (section 9). Source (UI or API) and tags come with the phases that need them |
| `post_part` | Ordered text of each part: of the post's own text, and of the text that is written for one target in its place, which names the target. Section 8 has it |
| `part_media` | The images of a part: which image, its place among them, and its description. Synced, since phase 7. Section 10 |
| `post_target` | Post and channel, status, how often the delivery was begun, error, when it was asked for and when it was sent. Not synced: the progress that the next attempt goes on from, the attempt that has a workflow, the time of the workspace at which the delivery that is under way began, which is part of the ID of its workflow, whether an attempt of the delivery has ended without anybody knowing what became of what it sent (section 8, at the end), and when its stats are next read, for one that is sent (section 14). The text that a target gets in place of the post's is not here but in `post_part`, since phase 4a |
| `target_part_result` | Remote ID and URL of each published part |
| `media` | An image of the workspace: its kind, the name of its file, its type, size and dimensions, whether the server has it, and when the server let go of its bytes. Synced, since phase 7. Not synced: the multipart upload that its bytes arrive through, when that was given up and when the bucket is to be asked again to drop its parts, and since when it has been in no part. Where its bytes are in the bucket is no column: the IDs of the workspace and of the image say it. `duration` comes with video. Section 10 |
| `schedule_slot`, `settings` | Weekly queue slots, each a weekday and a minute of the day. One row of settings: the time zone and whether the queue is paused. Both synced, since phase 5 |
| `metric` | The stats of a delivery that is sent, as the server last read them: the likes, reposts, replies, quotes and views of its first part, when they were read, and whether the network gives any. One row per target, under its ID. Synced. The latest values only, with no history: nothing shows one. Since phase 10: section 14 |
| `notification` | The inbox: what the person has to hear of, at most one row about a post and one about a channel, under the ID of what it is about, with its kind, when it was made and when it was read. Synced. Section 13 |
| `push_outbox` | The push messages that are queued for the devices of the owner and have not been handed to the push services yet. Not synced. Section 13 |
| `change_log`, `client_mutation`, `rejection` | Sync bookkeeping: which rows each of the latest changes touched, the last mutation applied per client, and the rejected mutations that a client has yet to hear of |
| `keyed_call` | The calls of the Worker that came with a key, and how each ended, so that a call which is repeated is not carried out again. A write of the public API that came with an `Idempotency-Key` is kept here with its answer, for 30 days: section 12. For some minutes, the outcome of a call that connected an account again holds the sealed credentials that it replaced: section 6. The notes that a step of a delivery is about to ask a network to publish are kept here too, for a network on which a step must not be run twice: section 8 |
| `workspace` | One row: which workspace the object holds, the sequence number of its last change, the time of its last mutation, and its copy of whether D1 has the workspace frozen (section 6) |

Post status follows Buffer: `draft`, `scheduled`, `sending`, `sent`, `error`. A post is `sending` from the moment its publication is asked for until the last of its targets has ended, and then `sent` if all of them are. A post whose targets ended differently reports `error` and lists each target's own status.

A target has more to say than a post: `pending` while it is only chosen, `queued` while it waits its turn on its channel, `sending`, `sent`, `error`, and `uncertain` for a delivery that ended without anybody knowing how far it got, which a person has to look into. `scheduled` is a status of the post alone: the targets of a scheduled post are `pending` until it is due.

## 6. The four networks

Each adapter in `packages/providers` implements one interface, `Provider` in `src/provider.ts`, and callers know nothing of a network beyond it. An adapter is made by a function of its own, which takes `fetch` and the clock and whatever is the network's own: the registration of the operator's app for LinkedIn and Threads, the address of its client metadata and the keys of the client for Bluesky, a store for its registrations with instances for Mastodon. Adapters never use globals, which is what lets the sandbox and the tests swap in simulators. An adapter keeps nothing between calls: what has to last is handed to the caller as JSON that only the adapter understands, and is checked against the adapter's own schema when it comes back.

| | LinkedIn | Mastodon | Bluesky | Threads |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Developer app | Yes, tied to a LinkedIn Page. Products: Share on LinkedIn, Sign In with OpenID Connect | None. pigy registers itself with each instance on first use | None. pigy hosts its own OAuth client metadata | Yes, a Meta app with the Threads use case |
| Who can connect | Anyone | Anyone | Anyone | Only accounts you add as Threads testers, until Meta approves the app |
| Token life | 60 days, no refresh. The user reconnects | Does not expire | Refreshed automatically | 60 days, refreshed automatically |
| Text limit | 3000 | 500 by default, read from the instance | 300 graphemes | 500 |
| Threads | Parts joined into one post | Reply chain | Reply chain | Reply chain |
| Media | Upload to LinkedIn, then reference | Upload, wait for processing | Upload blob. Video goes through the video service | Meta fetches from a public URL we provide |
| Stats | Not available for personal profiles without LinkedIn's approval | Likes, boosts, replies, and quotes from Mastodon 4.5 on | Likes, reposts, replies and quotes, from the public app view | Views, likes, replies, reposts and quotes, with a permission of their own |

Notes that shape the design:

- **LinkedIn** needs reserved characters escaped in the post text, a dated version header that must be bumped about once a year, and link previews supplied by us (title and thumbnail), because it does not fetch them for API posts. Built in phase 3d, for text: see "LinkedIn" below. Phase 4b added its hashtags and the preview of a link, without the thumbnail.
- **Bluesky** needs links, mentions and hashtags marked up with UTF-8 byte offsets, and link cards built by us: built in phase 4b, "Links, mentions and hashtags" below. Connection uses atproto OAuth as a confidential client, with its keys stored as a Worker secret: built in phase 3c, see "Bluesky" below.
- **Threads** publishes in two steps: create a container, wait for it to be ready, then publish it. A container that is not published within 24 hours expires. Media must be reachable at a public URL, so `/m/*` serves R2 objects through signed, expiring links. Built in phase 3e, for text: see "Threads" below.
- **Mastodon** limits vary per instance. pigy reads them from the instance and validates against the real values.
- **Link previews**: pigy reads the page of a link for its title and its description, and sends them to LinkedIn and Bluesky with the post. Built in phase 4b, without the picture and without a cache: see "Links, mentions and hashtags" below.
- **Token refresh** is one refresh at a time per channel. Bluesky refresh tokens are single-use, so two parallel refreshes would lock the account out. It runs on two occasions. At once, when an adapter says that the network has refused a token before its time: in the step of the publishing workflow that was refused, section 8, which Bluesky takes whenever its access token has ended, every few minutes. And when the time comes that the adapter named, whether or not the channel is in use: by the alarm of the WorkspaceDO, built in phase 3e with Threads, which needs it. A token of Threads is renewed only while it is good, so no step can renew one that Threads refuses, and a channel that nobody renewed for 60 days would have to be connected again. Bluesky's access is renewed by the same timer, a week after its tokens were issued, so a channel that nothing is published to for months keeps its session too. "Renewing an access by the timer" below has how the two take turns.

### The provider interface

| Part | What it is |
| --- | --- |
| Connecting | `beginConnect` takes pigy's callback address and, on networks with no single place to sign in, what the person typed to say where their account lives. It returns the address to send the browser to, the `state` that the callback will carry, and `pending`: what to keep under that state until then. `completeConnect` takes `pending` and the parameters of the callback and returns the account for display (ID, handle, name, avatar, profile link) and the access. It is called once per callback and fails for good whatever went wrong: the code in a callback is good for one exchange, so the person begins again. A failure says whether the person declined, which is no failure to them |
| Access | The credentials, opaque and stored encrypted, with two times: `refreshAt`, when to call `refresh`, and `expiresAt`, when the access ends unless it is refreshed. Either is null where it does not apply. Only adapters of networks that can renew an access have `refresh`. It is called by a timer when `refreshAt` comes, and at once when a method fails with `refresh`, one refresh at a time per channel. `disconnect` gives the access back where the network has a way. `accessPerConnection` says whether that ends one connection or every connection of the account, which decides what a caller does with an access that it could not keep |
| Limits | `limits` asks what the account may post. The answer is data that names its network, defined in `packages/core` with the counting that goes with it: `countCharacters` and `validateContent` are pure and run in the browser too. Adapters do not validate. They send what they are given, and the network is the judge |
| Publishing | `publishStep` takes the content, a key that names the delivery, and the progress that the step before returned. It makes at most one request that changes something and returns the new progress: the parts that are published, each with its ID and link, whether the post is done, how long to wait before the next step, and the adapter's own notes. The progress is stored as it is, so it holds no secret. A step can be run again at any time without publishing a part twice. `tellsDeliveriesApart` says whether what a delivery recorded before it published anything stays good when another delivery has gone to the account since. `repeatsSafely` says whether a step can be run again at all without publishing twice: an adapter that can neither look nor send a request that is carried out once says no, and its caller sees that no step of it is run a second time. Section 8 has the rules |
| Reading and deleting | `readPost` returns the text and what the post replies to, on networks that let pigy read. `deletePost` is fine with a post that is gone |
| Stats | `metrics` returns what people did with one post, the counts that the network has, or null for a post that is gone, on networks that let pigy read them. Since phase 10: section 14 |
| Errors | Every failure that an adapter reports is a `ProviderError` with one of five kinds: `retry` (with the time the network named, if any), `refresh` (renew the credentials now, then try again), `fail` (with a message for the person), `reconnect`, and `uncertain` (a post may or may not be published, and a person has to look). Messages may quote the network, without the secrets of the request. One thing else can come out of any method, as it is: what the `fetch` that the adapter was made with throws that is no failure of a network, or what a store of the adapter's throws. That is a failure of pigy's with none of the kinds. A caller logs it, and knows no more of the call than of one that was cut off |

How the four networks map onto it. All four are built. The column of Threads was the design that the interface was checked against until phase 3e, which built it and corrected the column where it did not hold:

| | Mastodon | Bluesky | LinkedIn | Threads |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| What the adapter is made with | A store for registrations | Its client ID for a callback address, and the keys of the client | Client ID and secret, API version | App ID and secret |
| What the person types | The domain of the instance | The handle | Nothing | Nothing |
| `beginConnect` | Reads the instance, registers pigy there if it has not, PKCE where the instance has it | Resolves the handle to its DID, checks that the DID document claims the handle, finds the PDS and the server that authorizes for it, and pushes the authorization request with PKCE, DPoP and the assertion of the client | Builds the address | Builds the address |
| `pending` holds | State, domain, the registration, PKCE verifier | State, handle, DID, PDS, the authorization server and its endpoints, the client ID and the key it signed with, PKCE verifier, DPoP key | State, callback address | State, callback address |
| `completeConnect` | Code for token, then `verify_credentials` | Checks `iss`, code for tokens, checks that they are of the type DPoP, are the account's that was resolved and have the scopes asked for, reads the profile. A profile that cannot be read leaves the handle for a name and no picture: failing then would leave a session at Bluesky that pigy cannot end | Code for token, then `userinfo` | Code for a short-lived token, that for a long-lived one, then the profile |
| Account ID | Account ID and domain | DID | `sub` | User ID |
| `refreshAt` and `refresh` | None | A week after the tokens were issued. Refresh tokens are single-use | None | A week after issue. Threads renews no token that is less than a day old, and none that has ended |
| `expiresAt` | None | None known | 60 days after issue | 60 days after the last refresh |
| `disconnect` | Revokes the token | Revokes the refresh token, which ends the session | Revokes the token (to verify, section 19) | Nothing to call, and it fails saying so: the person is then told where to end the access themselves |
| `accessPerConnection` | No: every connection of an account gets the token that exists for the registration and the scopes | Yes: every connection is a session of its own | No, until a live run says otherwise: LinkedIn does not document whether a second consent makes a second token, nor what taking one back does to the others | No: every connection has a token of its own as far as is known, but there is nothing to call that would end one |
| `limits` | Read from the instance | Fixed: 300 graphemes and 3000 bytes | Fixed: 3000, for the whole post, so for the parts of a thread together | Fixed: 500, with an emoji counted as the bytes that it takes |
| Steps of `publishStep` | One that notes where the account's statuses stand and who is to see the post, then one per part: look for the status, and post it if it is not there, as a reply to the part before. More steps that only look, where the account has posted more since than one list holds | Two per part: choose the record key and send nothing, then look under the key and write the record there if it is not, with the references to the first and the previous part | One for the whole post: the parts joined | Two per part: make a container of it, as a reply to the part before, then read the status of the container and publish it. More steps that only wait: while Threads is still preparing the container, and while it says that it has published one whose post it does not list yet. A new container, in a step of its own, if the one at hand has expired or is gone and the account has no post of it |
| Adapter's notes between steps | The account's ID, the status after which the next part is looked for, and the visibility of the parts | The account's DID, the record key of the next part, and the reference of each part | None | The container of the next part, the post that was the account's newest when the delivery began, and how often the container was found not ready |
| A step that is run again | Finds the status it posted among the account's own, or posts it | Finds the record under the recorded key, or writes it there | Sends again: it cannot look. So the workflow does not run it again, section 8. When a person asks for another try within the minutes in which LinkedIn refuses a repeated text, the step takes the post that the refusal names | Reads the status of the container first. Published: finds the post among the account's own and publishes nothing. Ready: publishes it, and reads the status once more if Threads refuses. Expired, or no longer known to Threads: looks for the post, and creates a new container only if there is none, which is a recorded step of its own, published in the step after |
| `tellsDeliveriesApart` | No: a status of another delivery that reads the same is among what the step looks through | Yes: no other delivery writes under the recorded key | No: it cannot look | No: the container is the delivery's own and says whether it was published, but which post it became is looked for among what the account has posted since the delivery began |
| `repeatsSafely` | Yes | Yes | No | Yes: a step asks what became of its container before it publishes, and publishes none that was published |
| `readPost` | Yes | Yes | No: reading takes a permission that LinkedIn restricts | Yes |
| `metrics` | The status itself, with the token: `favourites_count`, `reblogs_count`, `replies_count`, and `quotes_count` where the instance has quotes. A 404 is a post that is gone | `app.bsky.feed.getPosts` of the public app view, without a token. A post that the app view leaves out is gone if its PDS has no record of it, and not shown otherwise, yet or any more, which is a `retry` with `notShown`: about that post alone | No: the stats of a member's posts take an approval that LinkedIn restricts, so there is no method | The insights of the post, `views`, `likes`, `replies`, `reposts` and `quotes`, which take `threads_manage_insights`. A token without it, the code 10, is a `fail` that says to connect the account again |
| What asks for `refresh` | Nothing | An access token that the PDS refuses, whatever the reason | Nothing | Nothing: a token that Threads refuses cannot be renewed |
| What asks for `reconnect` | 401, a missing scope | A refresh token that the server refuses, `invalid_grant`, a 403 of the PDS, a key of the client that is gone | 401 | A token that Threads refuses, by the code 190 of the Graph API: it has ended, was taken back, or is none |
| What asks for `uncertain` | Nothing | Nothing | A request to post that ends without a clear answer: none came, LinkedIn failed with a 5xx, or it took the post and did not name it | A container that Threads says it has published and whose post is not among the account's own, also after some more looks: the step never publishes it again. And a look for the post that could not be finished, because there is more to read than a step reads: not having found it is not knowing that it is not there |

The handle of a LinkedIn account is its name: LinkedIn gives pigy no other. How Bluesky tells a token that has expired from one that was revoked is settled: it does not tell, in a way that is documented, and does not have to. Both ask for a refresh, which renews the one and is refused for the other.

What a network makes of a thread, decided in phase 4a:

- **Mastodon, Bluesky and Threads post a thread as a chain of replies**, each part a post of its own that replies to the part before. Each part is counted against the limit of the network by itself.
- **LinkedIn has no threads, and gets the parts as one post**: joined in their order, with an empty line between two parts, and nothing added, no numbers and no "continued". That one text is what LinkedIn's 3000 characters are counted against, and every part gets the same result, the one post. `validateContent` has that rule, with LinkedIn's limits: it says of a thread that is too long as one post that it is, and by how much, and not of a part.
- **Nothing is shortened or split by pigy.** A text that a network would refuse is said before publishing and keeps the post back. Whoever wants a network to get something else than the others writes it: the text of one account, section 8. That is also the answer for a thread that reads badly as one post.
- **The editor says in advance what becomes of a thread.** For a chosen account of a network that joins, it says that the account gets the parts as one post, and counts them as that post: section 8. `networkInfo` in `apps/web/src/lib/networks.ts` has for each network which of the two it does.
- **No network limits how long a chain may be**, as far as their documentation says. pigy does: a text has at most 100 parts, which keeps a row of the list and a message of the sync protocol small.

What was decided along the way, and why:

- **No validation inside adapters.** The browser has to validate while somebody types, and it cannot import the adapters, so the rules live in `packages/core`. Keeping adapters out of it also means that the conformance suite can send what the validation would stop, and see the network refuse it.
- **Credentials carry what revoking them takes.** On Mastodon that is the registration the token was issued to. The stored registration of an instance can then change, or be lost, without breaking a channel.
- **Optional methods for what a network cannot do.** `refresh` and `readPost` are missing from an adapter rather than present and failing, so the type says what a caller can count on.
- **Scopes for media are asked for now.** A registration with a Mastodon instance cannot gain a scope later. Asking for `write:media` in phase 3 spares everybody a second connection in phase 7.
- **A step asks the network what happened, and does not trust a window of time.** Mastodon remembers an idempotency key for an hour, LinkedIn tells a repeated post for about ten minutes. A person presses "Retry" hours after a post failed, so neither is a guarantee. Section 8 has what takes their place.
- **`uncertain` is a kind of its own.** Where a network lets pigy neither look nor repeat a request safely, the truthful answer is that nobody knows whether the post is there. Sending again would be a guess, and so would giving up.
- **`refresh` is a kind of its own.** A token can be refused before the time pigy has for it: the clocks differ, or a refresh for another post replaced it while this step ran. `reconnect` would mark a healthy channel as broken, and `retry` would hand the step the same token until the workflow gave up.
- **A connection is never completed twice.** The code in a callback is good for one exchange. A failure halfway may have spent it, so asking the caller to try again would promise what cannot be kept.

### Bluesky

Built in phase 3c: `packages/providers/src/bluesky` the adapter, `packages/simulators/src/bluesky` the simulator, `apps/worker/src/network/bluesky-client.ts` pigy as a client. Checked against the specifications at https://atproto.com/specs ("OAuth", "Permissions", "Handle", "DID", "TID") and the lexicons of `com.atproto.repo` and `app.bsky.feed.post`. Nothing of it has met the real Bluesky: there is no test account yet, section 19. What was decided, and why:

- **atproto OAuth is written on the Web Crypto API, without a library.** Section 19 listed the risk that the libraries of atproto are written for Node. The parts that pigy needs are small: signing a JWT with ES256, PKCE, posting forms. `oauth4webapi`, which pigy signs in to Google with, has pushed requests, DPoP and `private_key_jwt`, and takes a `fetch`, but reads the time from the global clock, which it lets one shift and not replace, and keeps the nonces of DPoP in memory of its own, where an adapter takes its clock as a parameter and keeps nothing between calls. It also knows nothing of what atproto adds: `sub` in the answer, the nonces of the PDS. The simulator checks every signature with `jose`, which the adapter does not use: a mistake in the one is not repeated in the other.
- **pigy is a confidential client with keys of its own.** Its client ID is the address of its client metadata, `/connect/bluesky/client-metadata.json` of the app, under `/connect/*`, which the Worker answers already. The document holds the public halves of the keys, in `jwks` and not behind an address of their own: one thing less to serve. The keys are the secret `BLUESKY_CLIENT_KEYS`, a list of `<version>:<key>` like `CREDENTIAL_KEYS`, each a private key of P-256 in PKCS #8, which `openssl` makes. The highest version signs the connections that begin. A server binds a session to the key it began with, so the credentials of a channel say which key and which client ID, refreshing and revoking sign with that one, and a key stays in the list for as long as such a session is to last. A channel whose key is gone asks to be connected again. A public client, with no key, would get sessions of two weeks at most.
- **A pigy on somebody's machine connects simulated accounts and no real ones.** Bluesky reads the client metadata from the address of the client, and `http://localhost:5173` is no address that it can read or that atproto takes for a client. atproto has a client for development at `http://localhost`, which is public, has no key, and sends the browser back to `127.0.0.1`: where the cookie of the session that began is not, so the callback would find no connection. pigy does not use it. A live workspace that is reached locally sends its request all the same, and the refusal of Bluesky is shown at the form. In a sandbox the simulated authorization server takes a client at a local address, which a real one does not, and pigy at `pnpm dev` and `pnpm preview:e2e` signs with a key from the repository, under the rules of the development key of the vault: section 16.
- **The account is found the way atproto has it, and checked both ways.** The handle leads to the DID by a TXT record, asked over HTTPS of Cloudflare's resolver, `cloudflare-dns.com`, with JSON, because a Worker cannot ask the DNS another way, and then by the file that the host of the handle serves, which the host may send on to another address: the one redirect that an adapter follows, section 16. A handle that leads nowhere is said to be one that pigy found no account for, whatever the reason, because a handle is what the person typed. The DID leads to its document, at `plc.directory` or the host of a `did:web`, which has to claim the handle in turn and names the PDS. A document claims one handle: the first entry of `alsoKnownAs` that is `at://` and a handle, as the specification "DID" has it. The entries after it are ignored, so an account cannot claim a handle that comes second. The PDS names its authorization server, whose metadata has to name itself as the issuer, offer what pigy needs, and say with `true` what the specification "OAuth" requires of every server: that it names itself in the callback, takes requests only pushed, and reads a client from the address of its metadata. A server that does not is refused before the person is sent to it. That is resolved when the connection begins and not again when it completes: the specification lets a client keep it for an authorization for up to ten minutes, which is how long pigy keeps a connection that has begun. `completeConnect` checks `iss` of the callback, RFC 9207, so that the code of another server is not redeemed here, and that the tokens are of the type DPoP, are the account's that was resolved and have the scopes that pigy asked for. That they are bound to the key of the session is not for pigy to see: an access token is not for a client to read. The PDS checks it, against the proof of that key that comes with every request.
- **What pigy asks for: posts, and media for later.** The scopes are `atproto`, `repo:app.bsky.feed.post?action=create&action=delete`, `blob:image/*` and `blob:video/*`: the permissions of atproto, which are narrower than `transition:generic`, the scope of everything that an app password can do. Reading a post takes no scope: records are public. Neither do its stats, since phase 10: they are read from Bluesky's public app view, `public.api.bsky.app`, which answers anybody. The app view of the account's PDS would take a scope for each method that it is asked through the PDS, which a session cannot gain later. A session cannot gain a scope, so asking for media now spares a second connection when pigy posts media, as for Mastodon.
- **Every call learns the nonces anew.** The authorization server and the PDS each want a nonce of their own in every proof of DPoP, and hand it out with a refusal. An adapter keeps nothing between calls, so the first request of a call to each server is refused once for its nonce, which costs a round trip per step that writes, and nothing else.
- **A part is written under a key that was recorded first, and looked for there.** The first step of a part chooses its record key, a TID, and sends nothing. The second looks for a record under that key, takes it if it is there and is what the step writes, section 8, and writes the record there if not, with `createRecord`. So a step whose answer was lost finds its post when it is run again, however much later, and Bluesky has to remember nothing. Not `putRecord`: it would write over a record, and what it answers for one that is the same is not documented. And not `createRecord` alone: what it answers for a key that has a record is not documented either, and the server that Bluesky runs fails it as an error of its own, which a step would take for trouble to try again later. Looking first leaves neither to chance. The record keys grow within a thread, as the specification wants a TID to, also on a clock that stands still. `createdAt` is the time of the write and is not recorded: a record is never written a second time, so it need not be the same.
- **A refusal of the access token asks for a refresh, whatever its reason.** The PDS refuses an access token that has ended, which is every few minutes, and one that was revoked, without a documented way to tell them apart. A refresh renews the first and is refused for the second, which asks for a new connection. A proof that the PDS refuses is a mistake of pigy's that no refresh cures, and fails. A server that wants another nonce after the one try with the nonce that it handed out has turned to a new one in between: that says nothing of the access, and the step is tried again later. The refreshes are carried out by `accessOf` of the publishing workflow, section 8.
- **Only a refresh token that the server refuses asks for a new connection.** That is `invalid_grant`, RFC 6749 section 5.2: the token is invalid, has expired, was revoked or was issued to another client. Every other refusal leaves the refresh token as it was. A server that cannot read pigy's client metadata, which it could when the session began, is asked again later, and so is one that wants another nonce. A refusal of the proof or of the assertion is a mistake of pigy's, and fails the step. Taking any refusal for the end of the session would mark every channel that renews its access while Bluesky cannot read the metadata as needing a new connection.
- **A refresh whose answer is lost ends the access.** The server has replaced the refresh token, and pigy never learned the new one. The old one is refused from then on, and the server that Bluesky runs ends the session when it comes again. The channel then needs connecting again. Nothing short of a refresh token that is good twice could close this.
- **Disconnecting revokes the refresh token**, at the revocation endpoint of RFC 7009 that the authorization server names, which ends the session with its access tokens. The OAuth of atproto names no way to revoke: a server that names none is told so, and the person is told where to end the access themselves.
- **Without `BLUESKY_CLIENT_KEYS`, a pigy is no client of Bluesky.** Its client metadata is not found, and a connection is refused with a 503, in a sandbox too, whose simulated server checks what pigy signs as a real one does. A channel goes on posting with the access it has, until the access needs renewing, and then needs connecting again.
- **Accounts move between servers in atproto.** pigy keeps the PDS of an account in its credentials, and does not follow an account that moves: its next post fails, and the account has to be connected again.

### LinkedIn

Built in phase 3d: `packages/providers/src/linkedin` the adapter, `packages/simulators/src/linkedin` the simulator, `apps/worker/src/network/linkedin-client.ts` pigy as an application of LinkedIn's. For members, and for text: no pages of organizations, which stay for later as section 1 has it, and no media. Written from LinkedIn's documentation at https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/linkedin ("Authorization Code Flow (3-legged OAuth)", "Sign In with LinkedIn using OpenID Connect", "Posts API", "Little Text Format", "Error Handling", "API Rate Limiting") as its author knew it, without access to the pages while writing, and nothing of it has met the real LinkedIn: section 19 lists what a first run has to settle. What was decided, and why:

- **pigy is an application that its operator registers, and a sandbox has one of its own.** LinkedIn knows an application only as somebody has made it in its developer portal, with the products "Share on LinkedIn" and "Sign In with LinkedIn using OpenID Connect" and the callback address of this pigy. Its ID and secret are the Worker secrets `LINKEDIN_CLIENT_ID` and `LINKEDIN_CLIENT_SECRET`, and without both a live workspace connects no LinkedIn account: the request to begin is refused with a 503. The simulated LinkedIn of a sandbox has an application of the world's own, `sandboxLinkedInClient`, which the world is made with and which is the same in every build. An earlier idea was a stand-in for development builds, as for the keys of Bluesky. It was not taken: the secret of the operator's application has no business in a simulated world, whose simulators refuse a secret that no simulation made (section 7), and with an application of its own a sandbox connects LinkedIn accounts in every pigy, also one in production whose operator has registered nothing. The application of a sandbox is in the repository for everybody to read. It is good in a simulated world and nowhere else.
- **No PKCE.** LinkedIn has PKCE for native applications, at other addresses. An application with a secret, which pigy is, uses the flow without it. The `state` does what it does for every network: section 16.
- **What pigy asks for: who the member is, and to post for them.** The scopes are `openid`, `profile` and `w_member_social`. Not `email`: pigy has no use for it. Reading posts takes `r_member_social`, which LinkedIn restricts, so the adapter has no `readPost`.
- **The account is the member, by `sub`.** The author of a post is `urn:li:person:<sub>`, and the credentials of a channel keep the `sub` next to the token. LinkedIn gives an application no handle and no address of the profile: the handle of the channel is the member's name, and the screens say it once. Where a person has to look at the account, the Sent screen links to the address at which LinkedIn shows everybody their own posts.
- **An access lasts 60 days and is not renewed.** LinkedIn gives refresh tokens to applications that it has approved for them, which nobody who runs pigy can count on. `expiresAt` is what LinkedIn named, the Channels screen says the day, and a post after it fails with `reconnect`. From that day on the row of the channel says that the access has ended and offers to connect the account again, before a post has been refused and the status of the channel has changed. A week before, the inbox and the devices of the person are told: section 13.
- **A thread is one post.** LinkedIn has no threads of an author's own, so the parts are joined, in order, with an empty line between them, and every part is recorded with the ID and the link of the one post. `validateContent` checks that the parts fit together, with what stands between them, and says so where they do not. A post is there whole or not at all, so there is nothing to continue.
- **Every reserved character is escaped.** The text of a post is in LinkedIn's "little text format", which marks mentions and hashtags with characters that it reserves. pigy posts plain text until phase 4, so the adapter writes each of them after a backslash, and the post reads as it was written: a `#` begins no hashtag of LinkedIn's yet, and an `@` mentions nobody. The simulator reads the format and refuses a reserved character that is not escaped, where LinkedIn would publish something other than was meant.
- **Posts are public**, the visibility `PUBLIC`. The live conformance run posts for the connections of its member, the least that LinkedIn has.
- **`uncertain` where pigy cannot tell.** Once the request to post has gone out, whatever leaves it without an answer that says what became of it ends the step as `uncertain`: no answer, a 5xx, a 201 without the ID of the post, an answer that is longer than pigy reads, whatever its status, and one that sends pigy on to another address. Only a refusal that pigy has read, a 4xx, says that nothing was posted: a 429 asks to try again, a 401 for a new connection, and what is wrong with the post fails it. Section 8 has what the pipeline adds for a step that never got to fail.
- **A refusal as a repetition is taken for the post, after an attempt that may have sent.** LinkedIn refuses a post of the same member with the same text as one that it has, for some minutes, with a 422 that names the post that it has (to verify). Whose that post is, the refusal does not say: the member may have written it by hand, or published it from another workspace. So the adapter takes it for the delivery's only where an attempt before this one may have sent, which the workflow tells it with `mayHaveSentBefore` of the publish request. The workflow has that from the workspace, which keeps it with the target, where no client sees it: an attempt of the delivery has ended `uncertain`, by the word of its workflow or because the workflow stopped without one. That is what makes a second try within those minutes harmless, by a person who looked and found nothing, or did not look. An attempt that follows none of that kind has sent nothing that the refusal could mean: within an attempt no step is run twice (section 8), and an attempt that was refused, for a limit or as a repetition, posted nothing. It fails, with a text that says that LinkedIn has a post with this text already and a link to it, however often it is tried. The first form of this took the named post in every attempt, and the second in every attempt after the first: a delivery then said "Sent", with a link, of a post that pigy had not made, when the person pressed "Retry" on a delivery that had been refused as a repetition. An answer from 400 to 499 to the request that posts is a refusal by its status alone, also where it is longer than pigy reads: nothing was posted, and the delivery fails or waits as that status has it, and is not `uncertain`. A refusal is read as a repetition only when its whole message is LinkedIn's, and one that names no post fails the delivery with what LinkedIn said. Section 8 says what is left.
- **A limit is for the day.** LinkedIn limits requests per day, a day begins at midnight UTC, and its refusal names no time. The adapter names the next midnight. That is more than the hour that the workflow waits for at most (section 8), so a delivery that meets the limit fails with a text that says so, unless midnight is less than an hour away: then the workflow waits for it and posts.
- **Disconnecting revokes the token**, at `/oauth/v2/revoke`, as the application that it was issued to (to verify). `accessPerConnection` is no: LinkedIn does not document whether a member who consents a second time is handed a new token or the one that the application has, nor whether taking one back ends the others. So pigy gives back only an access that a channel held to its end. If every connection turns out to have a token of its own, a connection that could not be stored and one that was replaced leave a token that nobody holds, which ends by itself after 60 days. The other way round, giving such a token back could have ended a channel that works.
- **The version of the API is a constant of the adapter**, `linkedInApiVersion`, sent with every request to the versioned API. LinkedIn retires a version about a year after it came out. A request with a retired one is refused with 426, which the adapter says in words: whoever runs this pigy has to update it. Raising it is a change of the code, after reading what LinkedIn changed.

### Threads

Built in phase 3e: `packages/providers/src/threads` the adapter, `packages/simulators/src/threads` the simulator, `apps/worker/src/network/threads-app.ts` pigy as an app of Threads, and `apps/worker/src/workspace/renewal.ts` the timer that renews an access. For text: no media, which is phase 7, and no polls, quotes or links with a preview of pigy's choosing. Written from Meta's documentation of the Threads API at https://developers.facebook.com/docs/threads ("Get Access Tokens and Permissions", "Long-Lived Access Tokens", "Posts", "Reply Management", "Retrieve Threads Media Objects", "Delete Posts", "Rate Limiting", "Troubleshooting") as its author knew it, without access to the pages while writing, and nothing of it has met the real Threads: section 19 lists what a first run has to settle. What was decided, and why:

- **pigy is an app that its operator registers at Meta, and a sandbox has one of its own**, as for LinkedIn and for the same reasons. The Threads use case of a Meta app has an ID and a secret of its own, which are the Worker secrets `THREADS_APP_ID` and `THREADS_APP_SECRET`. Without both, a live workspace connects no Threads account: the request to begin is refused with a 503. A channel that is connected goes on posting and renewing its access without them, which take its token and nothing of the app. The simulated Threads of a sandbox has `sandboxThreadsApp`, which is in the repository, has a secret that is marked as simulated, and is given the callback address of the pigy that a browser is at when the browser asks for its consent page, in full: section 7.
- **What pigy asks for: six permissions.** `threads_basic`, which every request for access has, and `threads_content_publish` to post. `threads_manage_replies` and `threads_read_replies`, because a thread is a chain of replies and Meta documents replying, and reading replies, under permissions of their own: whether a reply to a post of the account's own takes them is not said, so pigy asks (to verify, section 19). And `threads_delete`, without which no post can be deleted. Since phase 10, `threads_manage_insights`, which reads the stats of a post. An access cannot gain a permission later without the person consenting again, so asking now spares a second connection, as the scopes for media did on Mastodon. A channel that was connected before phase 10 lacks the sixth: its stats say that they come once the account is connected again, and nothing else is the matter with it, section 14. Meta reviews each permission before anybody but a tester of the app can grant it: section 18.
- **The token that is kept is the one that lasts.** The code of the callback is exchanged for a token that lasts an hour, and that one for a token that lasts 60 days. The secret of the app and the tokens travel in the address of two of those requests, as Meta documents them: no address is logged, and what an answer of Threads repeats of one is taken out of every message, `quotable`. The account is asked for with the long-lived token and named by the ID that `/me` gives as text: the answer to the code names it as a number, which can be larger than a program reads one exactly.
- **The access is renewed after a week, by the timer, and by nothing else.** Threads renews a long-lived token for 60 days from the renewal, but only one that is at least a day old and has not ended. So unlike on Bluesky, renewing cures nothing that Threads refuses: a token that it refuses, the code 190 of the Graph API, has ended or was taken back, and asks for a new connection at once. The adapter never fails with `refresh`. What keeps a channel is the caller's timer, below. A week leaves more than seven weeks to try again in when Threads is out of reach. Whether a renewal answers with a new token or with the same one and a later end is not documented: the adapter stores what comes, which is right for both (to verify).
- **A part is a container, and then its publishing.** Threads publishes in two requests: one makes a container of the post, which publishes nothing, and one publishes the container. A container says what became of it to whoever asks: it is being prepared, ready, published, or expired after a day. So the first step of a part makes its container, as a reply to the part before it, and the progress records the container before anything is published with it. The second step asks for the status first and goes by it: section 8. A container that was made twice, because the answer to the first request was lost, is one that nobody publishes.
- **A step waits where Threads is not ready, and gives up waiting after a minute.** A container of text is ready at once as far as is known. Where it is not, the step publishes nothing and asks the workflow to come back in ten seconds, six times. After that it fails with `retry`, so the waits of section 8 take over, and the same container is asked about again. A container that Threads could not prepare fails the delivery with what Threads said. That delivery cannot be tried again with success: its progress names the container that failed. It is not known to happen to text, and the way out is to publish the post anew.
- **The post of a published container is looked for, among what is the delivery's own to look at.** The status of a container names no post. So a step that finds its container published, which means that the answer to an earlier run was lost, looks for the post. A later part is among the replies to the part before it, by the account itself, with the text of the part. The first part is among what the account has posted since the delivery began: the step that makes the first container notes before, where the posts of the account stand, and nothing from before is looked at. What it notes is the time of the newest post and the IDs of the posts of that second. Threads says a time to the second, so the time alone does not tell a post from before the delivery from one that came after it began in the same second, and the newest post alone is no boundary once the person has deleted it: the first form noted that post and its time, and a delivery whose boundary was deleted took an older post of the same second for its own, and went on with its thread under it. One list of 100 posts does not always hold that second: where a list ends within it, the step reads the next, three lists at most, and where the second still goes on it notes that it does not know the posts of that second. The first form read one list, and a post of that second on the next one, from before the delivery, passed for one that came after it began. The oldest post that reads like the part is taken: deliveries to a channel run one after the other, so a later one's post is newer. A step that looks for a post reads five lists of 100 posts at most.
- **Only a post of plain text is taken for a part.** pigy publishes text. A photo, a video, a quote or a poll with the same words is something that the person posted in the app of Threads, perhaps between the making of the container and its publishing, and the first form took it by its caption. The step asks for `media_type` and `is_quote_post`, which Meta documents, and for `poll_attachment` (to verify, section 19), and takes a post only if it says `TEXT_POST`, is no quote and has no poll. A text post with a link is still taken: Threads adds the preview by itself.
- **A search that could not be finished is not a post that is not there.** A step that stops after its five lists without having come to the end, or that meets a post without a time that it can read, cannot tell. Neither can one that finds a post which reads like the part in the second in which the delivery began, when the posts of that second could not all be noted: it is from before the delivery, or its own. Where the container says that it was published, that makes no difference to what is sent: the part is not sent again either way. It does to what the person is told: Threads has said that the post is there, so the text says that, and that pigy could not look through enough of the account to find it, and does not say to publish it anew, which would be a second post. The first form said of both that pigy cannot tell whether the post was published. Where a new container would follow, for a container that has expired or that Threads no longer knows, it does: the first form made the new container, and the next step published a second copy of a part that was there. Such a step ends the delivery as `uncertain`, with a text that says that pigy cannot tell.
- **A part that Threads has published and that is not found is never sent again.** Whether the lists of Threads have a post from the moment that it is published is not documented (to verify, section 19), so the step looks three times more, ten seconds apart, and counts that in what it records: a delivery must not end for good because a list was some seconds behind. After that, the person has deleted or changed the post since, or Threads does not list it. Threads has said that it was published, so sending it again would be a second post for certain, where on Mastodon a post that is not found is one that may never have arrived. The delivery ends as `uncertain` with a text that says so, however often it is tried again. To post it once more, the person publishes it anew.
- **A container that Threads no longer knows is looked for too, and so is one that has expired.** Whether Threads forgets a container at some time is not documented. If it does, its status no longer says whether it was published. And whether a container that was published says so for good, or says `EXPIRED` a day later like one that was not, is not documented either. The look is one request. So the step looks as above, takes the post if it is there, and makes a new container if it is not: what Mastodon's adapter does for every part. If it cannot tell, it makes none, as above.
- **A refused publishing is no word on the container.** Meta does not document what publishing a container answers that is published already, or not ready after all. The first form took every refusal for the end of the delivery, which said "failed" of a post that may be online. So a step whose request to publish is refused asks for the status once more and goes by it: published, it looks for the post, still being prepared, it waits, and only a container that still says that it is ready, or that is of no use any more, fails the delivery with what Threads said. A limit and trouble of Threads' own are not refusals in this sense: they ask to be tried again, and the next step asks for the status first as every step does.
- **The error that the Graph API has no better word for is tried again.** Its code 1 is "unknown", possibly temporary. Trying again is safe on Threads, because the step that is tried again looks at its container before it publishes anything. That nothing which pigy asks for is refused for good with that code is assumed (to verify, section 19): if something is, the delivery fails half an hour later instead of at once.
- **`tellsDeliveriesApart` is no.** The container is the delivery's alone, and its status is the only way to know of a part that landed unseen, which speaks for yes. But which post the container became is found by its text among the account's own, and a later delivery with the same first part has a post there that reads the same: when the person has deleted the first one's own, the first would take the second's for it, and the two deliveries would share a post. The contract says no when in doubt. The price is the one of Mastodon, and it is paid by more than a delivery that has recorded nothing. A delivery that has no part with a result, and that another delivery to the channel passes, loses what it recorded: the container of its first part too, also one that was published without pigy seeing the answer. Tried again, it begins anew with a new container, and that first part is posted a second time. This is accepted: the other way was a third kind of progress, one that is kept for its container and still not looked for among the posts, for a case that takes a lost answer, a delivery that fails after it, and another delivery to the same account before the person tries again.
- **`repeatsSafely` is yes.** A step that is run again asks what became of its container before it publishes, and publishes none that was published. That rests on Threads saying `PUBLISHED` from the moment it has taken the request, and for as long as the delivery may be tried again (to verify).
- **Nothing gives an access back.** Meta documents no way for an app to end a token of Threads or to remove itself from an account. `disconnect` sends nothing and fails, saying so. The channel is removed all the same, and the route answers that the access was not given back, so the person is told where they end it themselves: in the settings of their account. Until then the token stands, for 60 days at most, with nobody holding it. `accessPerConnection` is no for the same reason: a caller has nothing to gain from asking.
- **The limit is 500 characters, and an emoji counts as the bytes that it takes**, as Meta documents for the text of a post. What a character is, and what an emoji, it does not say. pigy counts a code point as one and every code point that a pictograph is made of as its bytes in UTF-8, which is too much rather than too little where it is wrong (to verify). The simulator counts the same way in code of its own.
- **A limit of Threads names no time.** An account may publish 250 posts and 1000 replies in 24 hours, and Meta limits the requests of an app besides. The adapter takes the codes with which the Graph API says so for a `retry` without a time, so the workflow waits as it does for a network that names none, section 8, and gives up after half an hour.
- **Each person is a tester until Meta has reviewed the app.** An app in development is granted its permissions only by the accounts that its operator has added as Threads testers, and that have accepted in their Threads settings. That is Meta's rule and nothing that pigy sees: somebody who is no tester is refused on Meta's page. It is not simulated.

### Links, mentions and hashtags

Built in phase 4b: what is in a text besides its words. `packages/core/src/network/links.ts` and `entities.ts` find them, `packages/providers/src/card.ts` reads the page of a link, `packages/providers/src/bluesky/richtext.ts` and `linkedin/text.ts` mark them up, and each simulator does with them what its network does. What was decided, and why:

- **One detection, in `packages/core`, for the editor, the counters and the adapters.** `detectEntities(network, text)` returns the links, mentions and hashtags of a text with where they stand, as that network takes them, none within another: what stands within a link is part of the link. The editor marks with it while somebody types, the adapters of Bluesky and LinkedIn mark up with it when the post goes out, and `countMastodonCharacters` counts by the same two functions that it finds links and Mastodon's mentions with.
