Mastodon and Threads find these themselves in what they are sent: for them the detection says what pigy expects. Each simulator finds them in code of its own, as section 7 has it, and the conformance suite of the adapters holds the two together: it publishes a text with a link, a mention and hashtags to each network, and what the simulated network shows has to be what `detectEntities` found.
- **A link with its scheme is found the way Mastodon 4.5 finds one**, on every network: a host in any script with a top-level domain, a port, a path and a query, not in the middle of a word, without the punctuation of the sentence after it, and with brackets only in pairs. One difference, as before: any top-level domain of two or more ASCII letters is taken, where Mastodon takes the ones that exist.
- **A link without a scheme is one on Bluesky alone**, found the way Bluesky's own app finds one: in lower case, after whitespace or an opening bracket, with a top-level domain of a list that `links.ts` has. It leads to the same address with `https://`. Bluesky shows as a link what a client marks, so this is pigy's to decide, and it does what people know from Bluesky. Mastodon links no such address. Whether LinkedIn and Threads do is theirs and not documented, so pigy takes it for text there (to verify, section 19): the editor says so, and such an address gets no card from pigy.
- **A hashtag is the same on every network**: `#` or `＃` and then letters, marks, digits and underscores of any script with a letter among them, not in the middle of a word or an address, 64 characters and 640 bytes at most, which is what Bluesky takes, and both hold: a tag of few characters with many marks on each is within the first and over the second, and a PDS refuses the post with its facet. A larger tag is text on every network. The other three document no length of a tag (to verify, section 19), and a tag is the same thing wherever a text goes. Mastodon's own pattern also takes a middle dot inside a tag, which pigy's does not.
- **A mention is written the way its network addresses an account.** `@name` and `@name@server` on Mastodon, by Mastodon's own pattern. `@handle.example.com` on Bluesky, where a handle is a domain name. `@username` on Threads (a guess, section 19). None on LinkedIn, below.
- **Nothing is rewritten in a text.** A long address is not shortened, as Bluesky's app does in what it shows: the text that is counted and the text that a step looks for when it is run again is the text that was written.

Per network:

- **Mastodon gets nothing more than the text.** It finds links, mentions and hashtags itself and makes the card. The counting already matched: a link as 23 characters, a mention without its domain. The adapter's look for a status still compares the source of the status, with its mentions as Mastodon rewrites them: section 8.
- **Bluesky gets facets, with the bytes of the text counted.** A link as `#link` with its address, a hashtag as `#tag` without its sign, a mention as `#mention` with the DID of the account, each over the bytes that it takes in UTF-8, `utf8Range`: an emoji before a link is four bytes there and two code units in a string. The simulated PDS refuses a facet beyond the text, one that begins or ends in the middle of a character, two that overlap, and a mention of an account that its world does not have, which a real PDS lets through and an app then shows wrong.
- **A mention on Bluesky is looked up before the part is written, and what the part is written with is recorded.** The step that chooses the record key of a part, which writes nothing, also finds the account of every handle that the part mentions: the way a connection finds its account, and checked both ways, so that a handle whose host names the account of somebody else mentions nobody. With what it found it makes the facets of the part, and with the card that it was handed the embed, and notes both in the progress: all of it is what the record says in public. The step that writes sends exactly those: run again, days later, it writes the same whatever the handles lead to by then, whatever a page says by then, and whatever the pigy that runs it takes for a link or a tag, and takes the record of the run before for its own. Ten handles of a part are looked up, each once, the first ten in the order of the text, and a mention of any other is text. `lookedUpMentions` of `packages/core` says which they are, to the adapter and to the editor, which asks about the same ten and says of the others that they stay text. The first form had two rules: the editor asked about the first ten of the alphabet, and showed a mention beyond the tenth as understood.
- **A handle that is nobody's is text, and no post fails for it.** Not found, not claimed by the account, or not to be found out because the DNS or a server was out of reach: the mention gets no facet and the post goes out. Trying the step again until the lookup works was the other way, and would have failed a post over a mention when it did not. The editor says in advance which handles are nobody's: it asks the server, which asks as a post would, `POST /api/workspaces/<workspace ID>/mentions`, through `mentionable` of the adapter.
- **A step that finds a record under its key compares it with what was noted, facets and card, and works nothing out anew.** The first form noted the mentions alone and made the facets again in every run, with the detection of the pigy that ran the step. A deploy between two runs of a step that changed what is found in a text then made the step find its own record to be another post, at every try: the part was on Bluesky, the delivery failed for good, and the rest of the thread never went out. The first deploy of phase 4b was such a deploy, and every later change to the detection is one, as the list of top-level domains growing. Progress from before phase 4b has a key and no facets noted: its part is plain text, for the step that finds a record and for the step that writes one, so that every run of the step does the same. One part of a delivery that is under way over that deploy goes out without its marks.
- **LinkedIn gets its hashtags as templates, and its mentions as text.** A hashtag is written `{hashtag|\#|tag}`, which says where the tag ends. A reserved character in the name of a tag, which can only be an underscore, is escaped like everywhere else (to verify, section 19). A mention of LinkedIn names a member by an ID, `@[Name](urn:li:person:…)`, and LinkedIn gives pigy no way to find the ID of a name: `@ada` is escaped and is text, and the editor says so. Everything else is escaped as in phase 3d.
- **The first link of a LinkedIn post is sent as an article**, with the title and the description that pigy read from its page: LinkedIn reads no page for a post of an application, and a post without one shows the address and nothing more. Without a card the post goes out as text with its link. A page that names no title still has a card, headed by its host, so its link is previewed under the name of its host: LinkedIn requires a title of an article. The adapter sends no article for a card whose title is blank, which pigy's own reading never makes: LinkedIn would refuse the whole post for it. The parts of a thread are one post there, so the article is that of the first link of them all, `cardLinks`.
- **Threads gets nothing more than the text.** It makes the preview of the first address in the text itself where a post names no `link_attachment`, takes the first hashtag for the one topic tag that a post has, and links the accounts that are mentioned (all three to verify, section 19). So only the first hashtag of a part is one for pigy there, and the editor says of the others that they are text.

The card of a link:

- **pigy reads the page, in the Worker, with the reach of the workspace.** `fetchLinkCard` of `@pigy/providers/card` is the one function that does. A live workspace reads the internet with the app's `fetch`, a sandbox reads the simulated web of its world, and the end-to-end build reaches nothing for a live workspace: `reachOf` decides, as for an adapter.
- **It is the most guarded request that pigy makes**, because a person names the address and the page is anybody's. Only an HTTPS address at a public host is asked, `publicUrl` of `host.ts`: no IP address, no port, no name of a private network. A page may send the request on three times, each time only to such an address again, and the whole way has five seconds: `sendFollowing`. It is a `GET` that carries no cookie, no credentials and nothing of the post. Only an answer with the status 200 that says that it is HTML in UTF-8 is read, and of it the first 256 kilobytes, where the head of a page is: the rest of a body is not read, however long it is. Whether an answer is read is decided by its status and its headers, before anything of its body is read: the body of an error page, of a file and of an answer that sends the request on is given up, where the first form read up to 256 kilobytes of each, for up to five seconds. A page that names another encoding than UTF-8 for itself, in a `<meta>` tag, has no card unless its answer said UTF-8: read as UTF-8, its letters would come out garbled. The hosts of pigy itself are not asked, where the caller knows them, which the route of the editor does. Section 16 has the same list, and why a host of pigy's own zone is a case of its own.
- **Nothing of a page is run or built.** The title and the description are found by looking through its text: the Open Graph tags, then those of Twitter, then `<title>` and the description. The title is the first `<title>` before the head ends: a picture in the body has titles of its own. A page that names nothing but a description is headed by its host. What stands in a script, a style or a comment is not looked at, and neither is what stands within a tag: every tag is passed over whole, up to the first ">" outside the quotes of its attributes, read as the HTML standard reads them, so `<a title="<title>No title</title>">` holds no title. The name of a tag ends at whitespace, "/" or ">" and at nothing else: `</scripture>` in the text of a script does not close it. The page is gone through once, from its beginning to its end, `readPage`: every search goes forward from where the scan stands, and one that finds nothing ends the scan or is not made again, so the work is bounded by the length of the page, whatever the page is made to look like. That has to hold by itself, because looking through a text is work of the processor that no timer interrupts: the first form searched the rest of the page anew after every comment, and took seven seconds for 256 kilobytes of them. A count of the characters looked at, held to four times the length of the page, and a bound on the tags are the second line of defence. The names of HTML are compared without changing the text: lowering a page changes the length of some letters, and a place found in the lowered text is then another place in the page. What is taken is text: character references resolved as a browser resolves them, with U+FFFD for a reference to no character, such as half a surrogate pair, which is no text that can be sent on, without control characters and without the characters that turn a text around, cut to 200 and 500 characters. Markup in a title is not taken out, which would make another title of it: it is the letters of it, shown as text wherever it goes.
- **No picture.** The picture of a card on Bluesky is a blob that is uploaded, a request that changes something and a step of its own, and on LinkedIn an image that is uploaded before: both are what phase 7 builds for images. The editor shows none either, and must not load one from the page: the browser of whoever writes a post would then tell that site who is looking.
- **A step of its own in the workflow, which does not fail.** `cards` runs after the checks and before the first step of the adapter, for a network whose card pigy makes, `cardMaker` of `packages/core`, and returns what it read. Every step of the adapter in the run is then handed the same. A page that cannot be read leaves its link without a card, and so does a failure of pigy's own in the reading, which is logged where it happens, inside the step. A delivery reads eight pages at most, four at once, so the reading is over in ten seconds whatever the pages are, and a thread with more links than that gets cards for the first of them. A part that an attempt before has published is not read for again. And the reading has a time of its own, fifteen seconds, `cardsDeadlineMs`: whatever hangs that the limits of a page do not end, the step is then over with the cards that it has, at half of the thirty seconds that the platform gives it. That is `readCards` of `apps/worker/src/publish/cards.ts`, and it is what a delivery going on without cards rests on: the step returns, with fewer cards or none. Nothing is caught around the step. The first form caught what `cards` threw, to go on without cards when the platform had given up on the step, and had to tell that from the platform stopping the run, to pause it, to start it again or to end it, which arrives at the same place: by the words of the error, "Aborting engine", which nothing promises. Where those words differ, a run that is paused while a page is read would have gone on, and on LinkedIn the step after `cards` is the one that posts. A pause does not refuse the next step either: the local engine runs a step in full that begins while a pause is on its way, and stops the run after it. So the rule of section 8 has no exception here: what is thrown out of `cards` is the platform's, since nothing else comes out of it, and it is thrown on. If the platform gives up on the step all the same, because it was cut off four times in a row, the run ends as every run does whose step the platform gave up on, and the object of the workspace ends the delivery as `uncertain`, for a person to try again. That run has posted nothing, because `cards` comes before every step that posts, and the retry reads the pages anew. Tests pause a run while a page is being read, on Bluesky and on LinkedIn: nothing is published until it is resumed.
- **Not cached.** The first draft of this plan had a service that reads a page once and keeps it. A post to Bluesky and LinkedIn reads the page twice, once in each delivery, and the editor reads it again. That is accepted until it costs something: a cache would have to say for how long a page is what it was, and for whom.
- **The editor asks for the card with a route**, `POST /api/workspaces/<workspace ID>/link-card`, for whoever is signed in and about a workspace of their own, from pigy's own pages. It makes pigy ask a host of a person's choosing, so it is counted: 60 previews a minute per person, cards and mentions together, in the table `preview_use` of D1, and a 429 after that. Nothing of an answer is kept, on the server or by the service worker, which leaves every path under `/api/` to the network. The editor keeps what it was answered in the memory of the page: the parts of a thread lead to the same pages and mention the same people, and each link and each handle is asked about once, where the first form had every part ask for itself when it was shown, and a long thread used up the minute. Once also means once while the question is on its way: the parts of a draft that is opened ask in the same moment, and a lookup of several handles asks only about those that have neither an answer nor a lookup under way, `Answers.askAll` of `lib/preview.ts`. A card and an account are kept for as long as the editor is open. An answer that says "no", that a page could not be read or that a handle is nobody's, is kept for five minutes and then asked for again, and it is forgotten at once when its link or its handle has left the texts of the editor, so that typing it anew asks anew: the page may be up again, the account may have been made, and the adapter answers a lookup that failed at the network as no account too. Until the new answer is in, the old one stays on screen, so nothing changes for an answer that stays the same.

In the app:

- **The editor tints what will be understood, behind the text.** A field of a browser cannot colour its letters. So behind the field of each part lies a layer with the same text in the same letters, not drawn, and a tint behind each link, mention and hashtag. The field stays a plain field: typing, selecting, undoing, input methods and screen readers are the browser's own, and the layer is hidden from the latter. The other way, a field whose letters are transparent over a layer that draws them, would have left the caret and the selection to line up with letters that the field does not draw. The marks are those of the accounts that get the text that is shown, and a mark that not every one of them takes is drawn dashed.
- **What a network takes for text is said in words**, under the part, and the field is described by it for whoever does not see the tints: that LinkedIn has no mentions of pigy's, that Threads takes the first tag alone, that Mastodon links no address without `https://`, which handle Bluesky has no account for.
- **The link that a network leads with is previewed**, with what its page says now and with who shows it: the networks that get this card from pigy, and the ones that read the page themselves and may show it another way. Which link that is, is asked of `cardLinks`, the function that publishing asks, for each network and over the whole text. So a part can have two previews, where an address without its scheme is a link on Bluesky and text on LinkedIn, and a thread has one preview for LinkedIn, with the part that its first link is in. The first form previewed the first link that any account took for one and said that all of them show it. The preview is asked for half a second after the last key.
- **A question that got no answer is not an answer.** Without a connection, which is when much is drafted, the editor says that there is no preview without one and asks again when the browser says that the connection is back. After a 429 it says that pigy makes so many previews a minute, and asks again by itself: a minute later, and after every further refusal after twice the pause before, up to eight minutes, for as long as the question is there. The first form set its timer when the question first failed and not when it failed again, so a second refusal, as another tab or another device of the person causes it, left the promise on screen for ever. The pause grows because every part that waits asks on its own, and they would use the minute up together each time. Only a page that the server tried and could not read is said to be one that pigy could not read.
- **The editor looks a text through once for each network**, however many of its parts ask about it at a key, `detected` of `lib/markup.ts`, and the detection holds what overlaps against each other in one pass: a text of thousands of mentions took hundreds of milliseconds at every key before.
- **Where a browser does not size a field by its text**, the app does, when the text changes and when the field gets another width: the field does not scroll, so a text that wraps anew would be cut off.
- **The Sent screen tells the marks of a post from its words**, by the same detection, for the networks that got the text. **The feeds of the sandbox show what each simulated network made of a post**: the marks that the network has, found by itself or as the post marked them, and the card. `GET /api/sandbox/feed?marks=1` has both with every post. Without the query the posts come as they were before phase 4b: the app of that time refuses a post with a field that it does not know, and a device runs the app that it has until it is next started there. From phase 4b on, the schemas of the answers that a client fetches leave out a field that they do not know and refuse nothing for it, so the next field needs no such query: `AGENTS.md`, "Add an API route".
- **The Queue screen and the calendar show a text as it was written**, as the list of the drafts does: no mark, no link to press and no card. A scheduled post is edited as a draft, which "Edit" makes of it, and the editor marks and previews it there like any draft.

What is left: the picture of a card, with images in phase 7. A cache of cards. Mentions on LinkedIn, which take a way to find a member that LinkedIn does not give. The topic tag and the `link_attachment` of Threads as parameters, should a live run show that Threads does not take them from the text.

### Images

Since phase 7 a part of a post can have images, and a provider is handed where to read them: `MediaSource` of the contract, `packages/providers/src/provider.ts`. The content names each image and says what it is, and what it says is of the bytes that the source hands out: the workflow has made every image fit the network before a step runs, section 8, so an adapter sends what it reads and makes nothing of it. `read` hands out the bytes, whole, which are no more than the network takes of one image. `link` signs an address for a network that fetches an image itself: section 16.

What holds for every network:

- **An upload is a step of its own**, one image to a step, because it is a request that changes something. What it made at the network, the ID of a file or the reference of a blob, is in the progress before the step that uses it runs. Neither is a secret.
- **A step that uploads may be run again.** It then uploads a second time, on a network that keeps a file which is in no post for a while and then drops it: nobody sees it, and no post is made by it. That is why uploading twice is harmless where posting twice is not.
- **An image is part of what a step looks for.** A post is the delivery's own only if it has the images of its part: the very files that the steps before uploaded, where the network says which files a post has. A post that reads the same and has other images, or none, is somebody else's.
- **What a network dropped is uploaded again, once.** A delivery that is taken up long after its images were uploaded can find them gone. The part then begins anew, with its uploads: nothing was posted with them. A network says so by the name of an error, Bluesky's `BlobNotFound`, which an adapter reads from `code` of a `ProviderError`, and never by words of its message, which can say "blob" of any refusal. A part whose images are gone again after they were uploaded anew fails: the next try would find the same.
- **The conformance suite** holds every adapter to it: a part with its images in their order, each with its description, a part that is only an image, as many images as the limits say, a thread whose later part has images, every step run twice, and a post that reads the same without the images, which must not be taken for the part. Each of them fails for an adapter that is broken in that point, which the pull request has shown for Mastodon's.

**Mastodon.** `POST /api/v2/media` takes a file with its `description`, and answers 200 for a file that the instance has done with and 202 for one that it still works on. An image is worked on before the answer, so an upload is given four times as long as any other request. The status names the files with `media_ids`. The adapter uploads one image in a step and notes the ID. When all of a part are noted, it looks for the status, which is the part's only if its `media_attachments` are exactly those IDs, in their order. Before it posts it asks the instance about each file, `GET /api/v1/media/:id`: one that is not done makes the step wait and ask again, two seconds the first time and longer each time up to a minute, because every look is three steps of the workflow and two requests that the instance counts against the account; after about twelve minutes the step fails with `retry`, and the workflow tries it again later as for an instance that asks for that. One that is gone, because a file that is in no status is deleted after a day, makes the part begin anew. The limits are those of the instance, `configuration.media_attachments` and `statuses.max_media_attachments`, and Mastodon's own where an instance says nothing. A status with a file has no card: Mastodon decides that.

**Bluesky.** `com.atproto.repo.uploadBlob` takes the bytes and answers with the reference of a blob: the hash of the bytes, their type and their size. The same bytes are the same blob however often they are uploaded. The record embeds the images, `app.bsky.embed.images`, each with its `alt` and its `aspectRatio`. The adapter uploads one image in a step and notes the blob, and the step that chooses the key of the record makes the embed of the blobs and notes it, as it notes the facets: the step that writes sends exactly that, and takes a record under its key for its own only if it has exactly that. So a record with other bytes is another post. The lexicon has four images of a million bytes each, which is less than nearly every photo has: the workflow makes a smaller one. A post has one embed, so a part with images has no card. A PDS drops a blob that no record refers to, so a record that names one which is gone is refused, and the part begins anew, with another key.

**LinkedIn and Threads** publish no images yet: phase 7b. What is decided for them:

- LinkedIn has an Images API: `initializeUpload` for the member answers with an address to `PUT` the bytes to and the URN of the image, and the post names the URN in `content.media`, with `altText`, or several in `content.multiImage`. Its adapter can neither look nor repeat a request safely, so every step of it goes through `sendingOnce`, section 8, the uploads too: a step that began is not run a second time, and the delivery ends as `uncertain`. For an upload that is more careful than it has to be, because an image that is uploaded twice is an image that no post names. It is the price of one rule for every step of such an adapter, and the person who looks finds nothing posted.
- Threads fetches an image from an address: `media_type=IMAGE` with `image_url` and `alt_text`, and for several images a container for each, with `is_carousel_item`, and one for the carousel that names them. The address is a signed link, section 16, made in the step that creates the container and put nowhere else. In a sandbox the simulated Threads fetches the link from the bucket of the workspace inside the Worker, with the check that the route makes, and never from the internet. A live workspace takes `APP_URL`, the address that this pigy is reached at, which no step can know otherwise.

### Renewing an access by the timer

Built in phase 3e. `Access.refreshAt` of the contract asks a caller to renew when that time has come, whether or not the credentials are in use. Until Threads, nothing did: Bluesky's sessions last months unused. What was decided, and why:

- **The alarm of the WorkspaceDO renews.** The object holds the sealed credentials and the time, which is in the column `refresh_at` of a channel and not synced. Its alarm, which looked after the deliveries only, now also renews the access of every channel whose time has come, and is set for the next such time when no delivery needs it sooner. The object is built with what the publishing workflow is built with, to make the adapter of a network and to open and seal: `fetch`, the keys of development where a build has them, and `providerOf`. It opens the credentials with `src/channels/credentials.ts`, in the call and for the call, and stores what the refresh returned as a step of a delivery does: in place of the credentials that it started from and of no others, with `channel.renew` for the new end.
- **The two that refresh take turns.** A delivery renews the access of its channel itself when a step is refused, and some networks end the credentials that a refresh starts from. So the alarm renews no channel that a delivery is under way to. And no delivery begins while the alarm renews: deliveries begin in the alarm and nowhere else, the runtime runs one alarm of an object at a time, and within it the deliveries are begun first and the accesses renewed after. A post that is published while an access is being renewed waits for that one renewal to end, half a minute at most: the alarm stops after a channel when a delivery has become ready to begin, begins it as its last step, and comes again at once for the channels that are still due. Since phase 5 a change sets no alarm while the alarm runs: section 9, "The alarm". The first form renewed every channel that was due before it looked at the deliveries again.
- **A renewal that is overdue goes before the next delivery.** A renewal waits for the delivery that is under way to its channel, and a channel that always has a post waiting begins the next delivery when one ends. The first form let it: the access of such a channel was never renewed, and a token of Threads that has ended cannot be renewed any more. So once a renewal has been due for a day, `renewalOverdueMs` of `src/workspace/store.ts`, no delivery to its channel begins: the alarm renews the access first and begins the delivery as its last step, and the end of a delivery sets the alarm for that as it does for a delivery that can begin. Whatever comes of the renewal leaves the channel with a later time or with none, so a delivery waits for one attempt, half a minute at most, and renewals and deliveries to a channel stay one at a time. A day is far longer than a delivery takes and far less than what an access has left when its renewal comes due.
- **What does not work is put off, and never left due.** A network that is out of reach or in trouble is asked again an hour later, or when it says. A network that will not renew, and credentials that no longer open, mark the channel `needs_reconnect`, with the mutator `channel.lapse`, which names the connection that was refused as the end of a delivery does: section 8. A pigy that has lost its keys, or a failure that is its own, is tried again a day later: trying sooner would fail again, and never trying again would lose an access that a deploy could have saved. That goes for the keys that seal, `CREDENTIAL_KEYS`, and for a key that the renewal itself takes at a network, which `cannotRenew` of `src/network/providers.ts` says: without `BLUESKY_CLIENT_KEYS` the adapter would say that the account has to be connected again, as it rightly does of a session whose key was replaced, but nothing was asked of Bluesky and the session is as good as it was when the secret is back. The first form marked every Bluesky channel whose renewal came due in such a deployment. Every outcome leaves the channel with a later time or with none, and the alarm keeps a minute between two looks for renewals whatever the times say.
- **A renewed access that nothing holds is given back.** While the network is asked, the person can disconnect the channel or connect the account again. The channel then no longer holds the credentials that the refresh started from, and what the refresh returned is not stored. On Bluesky that is the token of a session that nobody could end, so the object gives it back, where the adapter says `accessPerConnection`.
- **An alarm that cannot set the next one fails.** Everything else that goes wrong in an alarm is logged and tried again by the next one. If the next one cannot be set, there is none: no access would be renewed and no delivery watched until something else woke the object. So the alarm fails then, and the runtime runs it again, which it does some times with a growing pause.
- **A workspace from before the timer gets its alarm when somebody opens it.** Its channels have a time to renew at and no alarm. The hello of a client sets one, like a connection and a renewal do. Nothing goes through the workspaces to set one, so a workspace that nobody opens, connects to or publishes in for the 60 days of a Threads access loses that access: phase 11 deploys for the first time, so no such workspace exists.
- **The workspaces of somebody who has lost their access are frozen**, since phase 11b. The timer knows the workspace and not whether its owner is still invited: that is decided in D1, at sign-in and on every request, section 11. Until phase 11b the channels of a person who was removed from the allowlist kept an access that the timer renewed for as long as the workspace was there, though nobody could post with it. Two ways to end that were weighed: disconnecting their channels, which gives the access back where the network takes it, or freezing their workspaces. Freezing was chosen because an admin can remove somebody by mistake and invite them again, and a disconnect is undone only by the person, account by account, at each network's consent page. So `frozen_at` of the `workspace` table of D1 is set in every batch that revokes the API keys of an account, for the same accounts, by the statements that end the access of an account together, `endAccessOf` in `apps/worker/src/auth/session.ts`: when an admin removes the address, and where a sign-in finds that an account has lost its address (section 11). A workspace is frozen where the keys that reach it are revoked, and thawed where new keys can be made, which is a sign-in of the account that succeeds: a key that still works reaches a workspace that is not frozen. The thaw asks again whether the address is invited, in its own statement, `thawWorkspacesOf`: an admin can remove the address while a sign-in is on its way, after it has looked, and D1 runs one statement at a time, so the removal either comes before the thaw, which then thaws nothing, or after it, and freezes the workspaces again. Asked in a statement before, the removal could fall in between and leave the workspaces of somebody who is not invited thawed for good. An invitation does not thaw: it says nothing about who has the address by now, the reason why it does not bring the keys back either (section 12).
- **What a frozen workspace does.** It renews no access, takes up no scheduled post and begins no delivery: the store says that nothing of either is due, so the alarm is set for neither, and a mutation takes no due post up either. The access of its channels runs out at the networks by itself: after 60 days on Threads and LinkedIn, and on Bluesky when the session that nobody refreshes ends. A token of Mastodon does not end, and stays sealed and unused until the person disconnects the account or ends the access at the instance. That is accepted as the price of a removal that can be undone. The data stays as it is, the channels with it, and the inbox goes on: it removes what is old, and tells of an access that ends in a week, which the person finds when they come back. The sockets of the workspace are closed whenever it is found frozen, and no socket is opened for it. Their clients connect again and are turned away, because the sessions ended in the same batch, so no change comes in by a socket that was open when the access ended, which would otherwise be served for up to five minutes (section 11). A change that comes in all the same, on a socket that the object has not closed yet, is rejected with `frozen`.
- **What is under way goes to its end, and what waits its turn ends.** A delivery that is `sending` at the freeze is not stopped: a run that is stopped halfway leaves a post that nobody knows the fate of, `uncertain`, which on LinkedIn cannot be tried again safely, and it is a matter of minutes. The deliveries that wait their turn, `queued`, are ended as not sent, through the server's mutator `target.halt`, with an error in words for the person: their access to pigy ended before the post's turn came, and they can try it again now that they are back. Until phase 11b they went out too, on the argument that they were asked for by somebody who had the access then. That holds for a run under way and not for a queue: somebody who is removed for what they post can have hundreds of posts waiting, which with a network that asks to be tried again would go out over hours. The inbox tells of each such post as of every post that failed, `post_failed`, and the record of publishing has its delivery as not sent. Once the person is back, they try it again from the Sent screen, `target.retry`, as any delivery that failed. A delivery that is queued while the workspace is frozen, by a call of the API that was on its way when the key was revoked, say, ends the same way: the object ends the queued deliveries each time that it finds the workspace frozen, and in place of beginning one. No delivery waits for the renewal of an access that is overdue while the workspace is frozen, because none comes: the step that a network refuses renews the access itself, section 8.
- **What happens when it thaws.** The posts whose time came while the workspace was frozen are found late, by the rule of section 9, at once: sent if they are at most an hour late, which is the case of a removal that an admin undid at once, and held otherwise, which the inbox tells the person, `post_held`. A post of the queue takes the next slot. The renewals that came due meanwhile are made, and an access that ended meanwhile asks for the account to be connected again, which the inbox says too.
- **D1 decides, and the object reads it.** After the batch the Worker tells the object of every workspace that it froze or thawed, `reviewFreezeOf` in `apps/worker/src/workspace/workspaces.ts`, through `waitUntil`, and the object reads its mark in D1 itself, `reviewFreeze`: it is not told which. The object learns the mark in one way, `#checkFreeze` of `apps/worker/src/workspace/object.ts`, which numbers its reads and goes by an answer only if no read that began later has been gone by: two reads that cross, as the one that a word asks for and one that the object makes itself, cannot leave it with the older answer. A word that is lost is made up for where it would cost something. The object reads D1 again before every step that would act for the owner: before it opens a socket, which is before any hello, before every mutation that the Worker asks it for, whether for a delivery, a call of the API, a connection or a renewal, because a mutation takes up the scheduled posts that are due, and in its alarm before it takes up a post, before each renewal of an access, the next one too after one that waited for its network, and before it begins a delivery. So a freeze that it was not told of holds from the first thing that it would have done, and a thaw that it was not told of holds from the moment the person opens the app. What is left is a socket that was open before a freeze whose word was lost: for up to five minutes its client's mutations take up a post that has come due, and the delivery of that post is ended at the alarm without having begun. Reading D1 before every message of a client was weighed and not done: it would add a request to every keystroke, and messages that waited for it could overtake each other. A D1 that cannot be read leaves the object as it was, which is logged.
- **Not frozen: an admin who is taken out of `ADMIN_EMAILS`.** No route hears of that, as for the API keys (section 12), and the operator who edits the variable knows whose the address is. Such an admin who tries to sign in with the address that they are stored with is refused, and revokes no key: nothing of their identity is lost, and their keys work again when they are let in again. So their workspaces are not frozen there either.
- **What is left.** A refresh whose answer is lost on a network that ends the credentials it started from costs the access, as for a delivery: section 6, "Bluesky". An object that is stopped between the refresh and the storing loses it the same way. And the reminder before an access ends that the person has to renew, LinkedIn's, is still phase 9.

### Connecting and disconnecting

The Worker's side of the first row of the table above, built in phase 3b-1. `packages/core/src/connect.ts` has the requests and the answers.

| Route | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| `POST /api/workspaces/<workspace ID>/connect` | Takes the network and, where the network asks, what the person typed. Calls `beginConnect`, keeps `pending` in `oauth_state`, and answers with the address to send the browser to. A refusal carries a message for the person, and names the field of the request that it is about: 400 with `field` set to `identifier` for what they typed, 400 with `network` for a network that pigy cannot connect, 502 without a field for a server that is out of reach or in trouble, 503 for a pigy without keys |
| `GET /connect/<network>/callback` | Where the network sends the browser back to. Takes `pending` out of `oauth_state`, calls `completeConnect`, stores the channel with its sealed credentials, and sends the browser on to `/channels?connect=…`. It answers with that redirect whatever fails, pigy's own database included, and never with an error. From `completeConnect` on, the work is handed to `waitUntil` and goes to its end if the client leaves meanwhile. A workspace that fails while it stores the channel is asked a second time, with the key of the first call. If the channel is not stored then, the access that `completeConnect` handed over is given back, where the connection has one of its own. So is the access that the channel held before, when the account was connected already |
| `DELETE /api/workspaces/<workspace ID>/channels/<channel ID>` | Removes the channel, and calls `disconnect` with the credentials that it held. Answers whether the network took the access back. Both steps go to their end if the client leaves meanwhile |

What was decided, and why:

- **A connection ends in the session that began it.** The callback is a request that another site starts, so it is believed only for its `state`: random, made by the adapter, kept on the server, good once, and found only for the session that began. Otherwise somebody could connect an account of their own, keep the answer of the network to themselves, and have another person open it: that person's workspace would then post to the first one's account. A callback in another session finds nothing and uses nothing up.
- **A refusal to begin says where a form should show it.** One status covered what a person typed, a network that pigy cannot connect and a request that is malformed, and only the first belongs next to the field for the server. So an error response that is about one field of the request names it in `field`, here and in every route: section 12. A server that cannot be reached is a 502 with no field, because trying again later can work.
- **In a sandbox, a server that its world does not have is a mistake of the person, not trouble of a server.** To the adapter it is a host that cannot be reached, which on the internet means trying again later. In a sandbox it means never: a world has the servers that it has. Somebody who enters `mastodon.social` there is told so with a 400 about `identifier`, in a message that names the simulated server to enter, and somebody who enters a real handle of Bluesky is told which simulated accounts there are: `sandboxIdentifiers` in `packages/core`. The `fetch` of a world marks the failure for the route to tell: section 7.
- **The address in the answer is one that a browser may be sent to.** A page of the app navigates to it, so it is checked on both sides with the same schema, `connectResponseSchemaFor` in `packages/core`: an HTTPS address, which the consent page of every network has, or an address of the app itself, which serves the simulated consent pages and is reached without HTTPS in development. An address such as `javascript:…` would run in the page.
- **The outcome travels as a word, not as a message.** The callback answers with a redirect to `/channels`, with `connect` set to `connected`, `cancelled`, `expired` or `failed`, and with the network, the workspace and the channel where they are known. What a network said goes to the log, and so does what went wrong in pigy, without the values that a failed query was run with: the `state` of the callback is one. A message in an address would be shown by the page as pigy's own, and anybody can make up an address. Somebody who is no longer signed in when the network sends them back gets `expired` like everybody whose connection is not found: the app has them sign in, brings them back to `/channels`, and says that the connection has to be begun again.
- **Disconnecting always removes the channel, and removes it first.** The `WorkspaceDO` removes the channel and hands over the sealed credentials that it held, in one transaction. Only then is that access given back, where the network has a way. If that fails, because the network is out of reach or the credentials no longer open, the channel is gone all the same, and the answer says that the access may still stand: a channel that could only be removed while its network answers could stay for ever.
- **The access that is given back is the one that was removed.** In the other order, with the network asked first and the channel removed afterwards, three things went wrong. A workspace that failed in between kept a channel that said `connected` with a token that was revoked. An account that was connected again in between had its new credentials stored on the channel, which was then removed with them: a token that pigy held no more and had not revoked. And a channel could outlive the revocation of its own token. Now a channel that cannot be removed keeps its access, and an account that is connected again while it is disconnected gets a channel of its own, which the disconnect does not touch.
- **One case is left, and is accepted.** Mastodon gives a new connection the token that the account already has with the same registration. An account that is connected again while the revocation of that token is on its way gets the same token, so its new channel says `connected` with an access that is about to go. pigy cannot tell, since the token is the same, and the channel turns out to need connecting again when it is next used. It is the same as for two workspaces that share an account, below, and takes two tabs and a moment of bad luck.
- **A disconnect goes to its end when nobody waits for the answer.** The Workers runtime drops what is left of a request when its client goes away. Somebody who clicks to disconnect and closes the tab would leave a channel that is removed and a token that nobody revokes, with nothing left to revoke it with: the credentials went with the channel, and a second request finds no channel. So the route hands the removal and the revocation to `waitUntil`, which keeps them going for up to 30 seconds after the client is gone, and it still answers whether the access was given back. Two cases are left, and are accepted. The answer of the `WorkspaceDO` can be lost on its way after the object has removed the channel. And the revocation can fail, or take longer than those 30 seconds, once the person has left and cannot be told. In both, the token stays good at the network until the person revokes it there, and pigy holds nothing of it: the credentials went with the channel, and with them what the workspace still kept of a connection that this one had replaced, below. Closing them would take a place for credentials that wait to be revoked, outside the channel, and a job that tries again: too much for a token that its owner can take back.
- **A connection that is not stored gives its access back, where the access is its own.** Between `completeConnect` and the channel in the workspace, the callback holds the only copy of an access that stands at the network. When the workspace fails to store the channel, or refuses it, or the credentials cannot be sealed, that copy was thrown away. For Mastodon nothing is lost: the next connection is handed the same token. For Bluesky it left a session with a refresh token that nobody holds and nobody can end, good for months. So the callback calls `disconnect` with the access before it lets go of it, and answers `failed` as before without waiting for it. The whole of it is handed to `waitUntil` before `completeConnect` is called, as the work of a disconnect is: the exchange of the code, the storing and the giving back. Handed over only where the storing had failed, it was dropped for somebody who closed the tab while the code was exchanged or the channel stored, and the session stood. What goes wrong there is logged as for a disconnect, without a secret, and the session then stands until the person ends it at the network. Whether to do so is a property of the adapter, `accessPerConnection`, and not the callback's to decide by network. Mastodon's says no, and must: Mastodon gives a new connection the token that the account has with the same registration already (to verify, section 19), which is the token of the channel that the account may have, here or in another workspace. Revoking it for a second connection that failed would take a working channel's access away, to tidy up something that the next connection is handed again anyway. A workspace that throws has not said whether it stored the channel: its answer can be lost on the way back. Giving the access back then ended the session of a channel that said `connected`, and when the account was connected again, that of a channel that had worked until then. So the call that stores a channel has a key, like every call of the Worker that may be repeated, section 8, and the callback repeats it once when it throws: a call that had arrived is answered as it was, and the person is told `connected`, and one that had not arrived stores the channel now. Only when the repeat fails too is the access given back. What is left, and accepted: the answer of the first call and the repeat are both lost. The channel then says `connected` with a session that was ended, and needs connecting again when it is next used.
- **A connection that replaces another gives the earlier access back, where the access is its own.** An account that is connected again keeps its channel, which gets the credentials of the new connection. On Bluesky the ones before are a session of their own, which nothing holds from then on. The workspace hands them back, sealed, from the transaction that replaced them, and the callback ends that session with `disconnect`, through `waitUntil`, after the channel is stored. Only where the adapter says `accessPerConnection`: on Mastodon the credentials before hold the token that the new ones hold, and the workspace is not asked for them. The workspace keeps what it handed back under the key of the call, in `keyed_call`, sealed as it was, so that a repeat of the call is handed the same. The only repeat is the callback's own, seconds later, and what is kept is the refresh token of a session that may stand, if giving it back fails. So it is kept for five minutes and not for the 30 days of the key: the workspace takes it out of what it keeps of the call with its first change after that, whatever the change, a draft that is typed into included, and at once when the channel of the account is disconnected. A repeat that came later would be answered without it, and that session would stand. Nothing takes it out by a timer: in a workspace that nobody changes again, it stays, sealed, until somebody does. That it could not be given back is logged, without a secret, and the session then stands until the person ends it at the network.
- **The adapter ends a session that it does not hand out.** `completeConnect` of Bluesky's adapter refuses tokens that came with a 200 when they are not bound to its key, are for another account, or lack a scope, and can fail after the tokens came. The callback gets an error and no credentials then, so the adapter revokes the refresh token itself before it fails, as far as that goes, and fails with what was wrong in any case.
- **One account may be connected to several workspaces, and they share its access.** Nothing stops two people from connecting the same account, and Mastodon gives out the same token for the same registration and account. Disconnecting in one workspace revokes that token, so the channel of the other needs connecting again when it is next used. This is accepted. Keeping the token while another workspace uses it would take an index of which workspace holds which account, kept in step with every `WorkspaceDO`, and it would leave pigy with access to an account that somebody has just disconnected. Section 8 has what else such a pair of channels can do.
- **A channel gets only the addresses that a browser can open.** The picture and the page of a simulated account are on a host that exists in its world alone, so a channel of a sandbox has neither.
- **Channels change only through mutators that clients cannot send**, `channel.connect` and `channel.disconnect`. See section 4.

The Channels screen, built in phase 3b-3a, is the app's side of these routes:

- **The list is read from the local copy**, like every screen, so it is there at once and without a connection. Connecting and disconnecting are the two things in the app that a device cannot do by itself: they need the server, and without a connection the screen says so and offers neither.
- **What a network asks is a table, not a screen.** `apps/web/src/lib/networks.ts` says for each network what it is called, whether pigy can connect it, what a person types and where they remove an app at the network. The screen is made from that, so the next network is an entry. A network that cannot be connected yet is listed as coming, and is nothing to press.
- **In a sandbox nobody guesses a server.** The form has the simulated server of the sandbox filled in and says that it is simulated. Somebody who enters another one is told which to enter, next to the field: the route names the field.
- **A refusal is shown where the route says.** With the field `identifier`, next to the field. Otherwise with the form: a 502 with the advice to try again in a moment, a 503 with the plain statement that whoever runs this pigy has not given it the key to store credentials with.
- **The dialog is one step in the history of the browser.** Its address says what it shows, so that the way to a network and back, a link from another screen and the command palette all lead into it. Opening it adds an entry to the history, and Back closes it. The steps inside it, from the choice of a network to the form and back, take the place of that entry: Back never leads through what the dialog has shown. Closing the dialog leaves the history as it was before the dialog. Where the dialog was opened on the Channels screen, by a button of the screen or by the command palette, its entry says in its state that the screen is under it, and closing goes back to that entry: the screen is in the history once, however often the dialog was opened and closed, and Forward opens the dialog again, as Forward does. The state of an entry is kept by the browser, so this holds when the page is loaded again with the dialog open. Where the entry does not say so, what is under it is not known: the dialog was opened by a link from another screen, or by an address from outside the app. There the screen takes the place of the entry, so that Back leads to where the person came from and not into the dialog again. Phase 3b-3a first closed the dialog that way everywhere, which left the screen in the history twice for every dialog that was closed on it, and Back did nothing that often.
- **A connection that is being begun can be called off.** Until the answer of the server has sent the browser to the network, closing the dialog, going back to the choice of a network, changing the workspace or leaving the screen gives the request up, and an answer that comes all the same is not followed. A slow answer would otherwise take the person to the consent page of a real network after they have turned to something else, which can be the sandbox.
- **The outcome is taken out of the address at once**, before the screen is shown, and kept in memory until the screen has said it. Loading the page again does not say it again. The screen has a text of its own for each of the four outcomes and shows nothing else of the address: the account that was connected is named from the row of its channel, when the copy has it.
- **The tab shows the workspace that the connection was for.** The address names it. A connection can end in a tab that shows the other workspace: it was begun in another tab, or the network has sent the browser back in a new one. Showing the outcome under the name of the other workspace would say "connected" over a list without the account. This is the one place where something other than the person changes what a tab shows, and it changes that tab alone: the device goes on remembering what the person chose.
- **Disconnecting asks first** and says what it means. The answer says whether the network took the access back. If not, the screen says so and names the place in the network's settings where the person removes pigy. The channel leaves the list when the server has answered, a moment before the copy hears of it. The keyboard was on the button of its row, which is gone with it, so it goes to the button that connects an account. That button is disabled when the connection was lost while the request was on its way, and a disabled button takes no focus: the keyboard goes to the heading of the screen then. The request is not called off, unlike the one that begins a connection: the server goes through with it whatever becomes of the page. So the question stays open until the answer is in, and the command palette does not open over it, or over any other dialog: a command would take away the screen that the answer is waited for on. The shortcuts of the app do nothing while a dialog is open for the same reason, also when the key press does not come from the dialog: its buttons are disabled while the request is on its way, and a browser takes the focus off a disabled button. The Back of the browser still can. Then a notice says how it went, on whichever screen the person is by then, because what is left to do when the network kept the access is theirs to do. That notice is for the person and not about the workspace on screen: it does not leave when they change workspaces, as section 7 has it, and it names the account and the workspace that the account was in. The warning that the network kept the access stays until the person closes it.

What the publishing pipeline of phase 3b-2 added to channels, and what is left:

- **The limits of an account** are in the row of its channel, with the time at which the network was asked. `limits` is asked when an account is connected, where a network that does not answer costs the limits and not the connection, and again by a delivery that finds them more than a day old or missing. The browser reads them from the row, to count while somebody types.
- **The status `needs_reconnect`** is set by the mutator that ends a delivery, in the same change as the target that failed, when an adapter fails with `reconnect` or the credentials of the channel no longer open. It is set only for the connection that failed, which the delivery names by its `connectedAt`: a channel that was connected again before the delivery said so stays `connected`. Connecting the account again clears it. A delivery to a channel in that status fails at once, without a request to the network.
- **Refreshing credentials.** A step that an adapter fails with `refresh` renews them and stores the new ones in the channel, in place of the ones that it started from and only of those: `renewChannelAccess`, with the mutator `channel.renew` for when the access ends. `refreshAt` is stored with them, and since phase 3e the alarm of the `WorkspaceDO` goes by it: "Renewing an access by the timer" above.
- **Sealing again.** A step of the workflow opens the credentials of its channel each time. Sealing them again there with the newest key, so that an old key can be dropped, is not built: see section 16.

## 7. Sandbox and simulators

### Simulators

`packages/simulators` contains a working imitation of each network's HTTP API, written from the official docs:

- **LinkedIn**: OAuth, user info, posts, image and video upload. Built in phase 3d: OAuth with its consent page, token introspection and revocation, user info, and posting and deleting text. The rest comes with the phases that use it.
- **Mastodon**: app registration, OAuth, instance info, media, statuses. Since phase 10 a status has the counts of what people did with it.
- **Bluesky**: OAuth with DPoP, identity resolution, blob upload, record creation, video service, post stats. Built in phase 3c: identity, OAuth, and writing, reading and deleting posts. Phase 10 added the stats of a post, from the public app view, `app.bsky.feed.getPosts`. The rest comes with the phases that use it.
- **Threads**: OAuth, token exchange and refresh, containers, publish, insights. Built in phase 3e: OAuth with its consent page, the token of an hour, the one of 60 days and its renewal, the profile, containers with their status, publishing, the posts of an account and the replies to a post, deleting, and the quotas of an account. Phase 10 added the insights of a post. The rest comes with the phases that use it.
- **What people do with a post**, since phase 10, is the world's, and every simulator answers with it in the words of its network: what a test says that people did, `engage`, the replies among the posts of the world, and in a sandbox what its audience did, which grows with the clock, section 14.
- **A small web**, built in phase 4b: a handful of pages under `.test` with what a page says of itself in its head, one that has moved, one that writes markup into its title and a file that is no page. pigy reads them for the card of a link in a sandbox, and the simulated Mastodon and Threads make their own cards from them. It keeps nothing and takes no faults.
- **Google sign-in** and a **web push service**, used by development and tests only. The second is built in phase 9, `packages/simulators/src/push`: the push services of the browsers together with the devices behind them. It checks the token of a sender and the headers of a message as the standards have them, and decrypts what it is sent with the keys of the device that it made up, which a push service cannot. Section 13 says where it runs.

Each simulator:

- Validates requests strictly. A missing header, wrong token, bad field or over-limit text is rejected the way the real service rejects it.
- Keeps state: accounts, tokens, uploaded media, published posts and reply chains.
- Supports fault injection: rate limit, expired token, server error, slow media processing, and "succeeded but the response was lost".
- Has a controllable clock.

Adapters keep calling the real hostnames. The injected `fetch` decides whether the request leaves the Worker or is handled in-process by a simulator. The adapter code that runs in tests is the code that runs in production.

What the simulators of the networks share is written once, in `packages/simulators/src/world`:

- **The world**: the accounts, tokens and posts of all networks in one form, behind a storage of keys and values that is read and written at once. It has the shape of the storage of a Durable Object with SQLite, so the `SimWorldDO` passes its own. A simulator keeps nothing outside the world and is made anew for every request. `feed` lists what is published, for the simulated feeds page.
- **The clock**: the one the world is made with, plus what `advance` has added, which is kept in the world.
- **Faults**: armed ahead of time, for a network and optionally for one kind of request, and struck by the next request they apply to. The kinds are `rate-limit`, `server-error`, `lost-response` and `token-revoked`, which Mastodon can suffer from, and `token-expired`, which came with Bluesky: a token that has ended before its time, which a refresh cures. A token of the world can have an end, which the clock of the world reaches. Processing that takes long is added with the first network that has it, together with the simulator that applies it and its tests. A fault strikes a request that the network takes up: the simulated PDS of Bluesky refuses a request for the nonce of its proof, or for its token, before it takes a fault, so that the fault is not spent on a request that nobody carried out.
- **The consent page**: where a person chooses which simulated account to connect, marked as a simulation. `answerConsent` answers it without a browser.

A simulator is written from the network's documentation and shares no code with the adapter or with the validation in `packages/core`: a simulator that is more lenient than the service hides the mistakes it is there to find. Where the documentation is silent and something had to be assumed, the code says GUESS, for a live run to confirm.

The simulators of the networks are part of the production build, because the sandbox runs on them. So they are built to act on a simulated world and on nothing else:

- A simulator makes no requests, and fails for every host that is not its own.
- A simulated Mastodon instance has a domain under `.example`, `.test` or `.invalid`. Such a name cannot exist on the internet, so a simulated instance is never taken for a real one, and a request that was meant for it and left the Worker by mistake reaches nobody. The other three networks have fixed hosts, where this cannot be done. The simulated LinkedIn answers on LinkedIn's two, `www.linkedin.com` and `api.linkedin.com`, and takes an application only with a secret that a simulation made: the application of the operator's is never told to a simulated world, section 6. The simulated Bluesky answers on Bluesky's real hosts, `bsky.social`, `plc.directory` and the resolver `cloudflare-dns.com`, because those are the hosts that the adapter asks. Its PDSes and the handles of its accounts are under `.test`, which atproto sets aside for development, so what is an account's own is never a real host. What keeps a sandbox off the real hosts is the next two points.
- Every secret of a simulated network begins with `pigy-sim-`, and a world finds no token that it did not issue. A real credential that reaches a simulator is refused before it is compared with anything.
- Which `fetch` an adapter gets is decided by the environment of the workspace, live or sandbox, and by nothing in a request. `reachOf` in `apps/worker/src/network/reach.ts` is the one place that decides, and every adapter in the Worker is made through it. A sandbox gets a `fetch` that leads into its world and nowhere else, whatever host a request names. A live workspace gets the `fetch` that the app was built with.

The sandbox gives the Mastodon adapter a store for registrations of its own, per world: the same simulated instance exists in every user's world, each with its own registered clients. The registrations are in the same table as the real ones, `mastodon_app` of D1, under the ID of the sandbox workspace where the real ones are under `live`. They are pigy's own records, not part of a network, so they are not in the world.

### The simulated world of a sandbox

Every sandbox workspace has a `SimWorldDO`, named after the ID of the workspace, which holds its world in the storage of the object. `apps/worker/src/sim` has it.

- A new world starts with one Mastodon instance, `mastodon.example`, and three accounts to connect as. `sandboxMastodonDomain` in `packages/core` is the name, for the app to offer. It also has a simulated Bluesky with one PDS behind the simulated entryway, and three accounts, `ada.bsky.test`, `grace.bsky.test` and `alan.bsky.test`: `sandboxBlueskyHandles`. A world from before Bluesky gets them the first time it is used: each network that came later is seeded under a key of its own. So is the simulated LinkedIn, with pigy as an application of the world's own and three members of the same names, whom LinkedIn addresses by those names. That application has one redirect address, in full, as LinkedIn compares it: the callback address of the pigy at which a browser asks for the consent page. A world cannot know where its pigy is reached, so the Worker says so with every request for a page, from the address of that request, and the object enters it for the application.
- A simulator makes no requests, so the simulated authorization server of Bluesky cannot read the client metadata of pigy, as a real one does. The object of the world knows what pigy serves at its address, and answers for it: the same document, made by the same function, with the keys that the Worker has. It takes a client at a local address, which a real server does not, for a pigy that runs on somebody's machine.
- The Worker is the only way in, and the object makes no requests. A request crosses into it whole, as plain data, and so does its answer: a body that is passed as a stream stays open across the call until somebody reads it.
- The object hands a request to the simulator that owns its host: each simulator says which hosts are its own. A host that none owns does not exist in the world, and the `fetch` of the world fails for it as for a host that cannot be reached, with an error of its own kind, because in a world that is for good. An answer that a fault makes a simulator lose fails the same way without that mark. So does a call that the runtime could not carry into the object or back: a connection to the object that was lost, an object that a deploy has reset, one that has more to do than it can take. The way into its world is to a sandbox what the internet is to a live workspace, and the runtime marks such an error as its own, `retryable` or `overloaded`, which is what `worldFetch` goes by. Whatever else goes wrong in a world, such as a world that turns out to be another workspace's or a bug in a simulator, is a failure of pigy's and is not passed off as the network's.
- Like the `WorkspaceDO`, the object remembers which workspace it was first used for and answers no other. It answers no live workspace at all.
- A world keeps what lasts a while only for as long as it lasts. A simulator remembers proofs, nonces, code challenges, requests that wait for a person, codes and tokens, about one entry for every request that a client makes, and forgets each when its time has passed and it next writes to the same table: a world that is used for months does not grow with every post. A token whose end has passed goes when the network issues another, revoked or not. A code that was exchanged is the exception: it is what tells a code that comes a second time, however late, which ends the session that it was exchanged for, so the simulated Bluesky keeps it for as long as that session lives and forgets it when the session has ended.
- The pages of a simulated network that are there for a person, which are the ones where they consent to a connection, are served by the Worker under `/sim/network/<host>/…`: `/oauth/authorize` on a Mastodon instance and on an authorization server of Bluesky, `/oauth/v2/authorization` on LinkedIn. The address names no world: it is that of the sandbox of whoever is signed in. So no address shows one person the world of another, and the API of a simulated network cannot be reached from a browser at all. The address of such a page is anybody's to write, and a consent page sends the browser on. So a simulator sends it only to an address that was registered in full, and the Worker passes on no redirect of a simulated page that leads away from the origin of the request, whichever simulator gave it: the first form of the simulated LinkedIn took pigy's callback path at any origin, and an address at pigy's own host then sent whoever was signed in to another site, in a production build too.

The Google simulator is the first one, built in phase 1, and differs from the others in two ways:

- It is not part of the production build, because it signs anybody in as anybody. The Worker's entry point builds it in only for development, tests and the build that the end-to-end tests run against. The production build is checked for it, and it answers only requests that name a local host such as `localhost`. That is a property of the request, not of where it comes from, so a build that contains it must not be exposed to a network.
- It keeps almost no state. Sign-in happens before there is a user whose `SimWorldDO` could hold any, so its authorization codes carry what is needed to redeem them, signed. Only which codes were redeemed is remembered, in memory.

Its consent page is served by the Worker under `/sim/google/*`. Without Google credentials, development and the end-to-end build use it and make `admin@example.com` the admin, so that a fresh clone needs no setup.

### Sandbox in the app

- A **Live / Sandbox** switch: at the top of the sidebar, in the header on a phone, and in the command palette. The sandbox has a banner across the top of every screen, also over what covers the app on a phone, such as the editor of a draft, and its name in the title of the tab. The live side has neither.
- New users start in the sandbox, which starts empty: no account is connected and there are no sample drafts. The first steps are made plain instead: the empty Drafts screen says what the sandbox is and leads to connecting a simulated account, which takes the form with the simulated server filled in and the consent page, the editor of a draft leads to choosing the account and publishing, and the empty Sent screen to the simulator. Phase 3b-3b decided it, below.
- Connecting a simulated account goes through a simulated consent screen under `/sim/*`, clearly labelled as fake.
- A **simulated feeds** page shows what was published to each network, including thread chains and media. `GET /api/sandbox/feed` has what it shows: the accounts of the world, each with its posts, a thread as a chain of replies. Built in phase 3b-3b, as the Feeds of the Simulator screen, without media until phase 7.
- A **failure panel** lets the user make the next publish fail, to see error handling, retries and notifications. `GET /api/sandbox/faults` has what it offers, the kinds of fault and of request that each simulated network declares, and what is armed. `POST` arms a fault and `DELETE` disarms them all. A fault that asks to be tried again makes the workflow wait as long as it would for real, a minute at least, and the panel says so. Built in phase 3b-3b, as the Failures of the Simulator screen.
- Both routes name no workspace, like the pages of the simulated networks: they answer for the sandbox of whoever is signed in. `packages/core/src/sandbox.ts` describes them.
- Sandbox API keys (`pigy_test_…`) hit the sandbox, so scripts can be developed without posting for real.
- Simulated Google sign-in is compiled out of production. Real sign-in is always required, including for the sandbox.

How the switch works, and why:

- **The choice belongs to the tab, and the device remembers the last one.** A tab shows the workspace that was chosen in it, over a reload and over the way to a network and back. The device remembers the choice that was made last, and a new tab, or the app when it is next opened, starts with that. Somebody who has never chosen starts in the sandbox. The other way, one choice for the whole device that every tab follows, was not taken: a tab would turn from sandbox to live behind the back of whoever comes back to it, and what they then try there would go out for real. A tab that changes only when somebody changes it cannot do that. It also lets the two be open side by side, which is how something is tried in the sandbox next to the real thing.
- **A link that is opened in a new tab starts with the device's last choice.** The address of a draft or of a channel to connect again does not say which workspace it is of. A tab that follows such a link stays in its workspace. A new tab has no choice of its own yet and takes what the device remembers, which another tab may have set since: so the link can open under the other workspace, where the draft is not found and there is no such channel to connect. That is accepted. The name on screen is right, the screen says that the draft is not there, and nothing is written into the other workspace. The other way would be to put the workspace into every address, for the switch to rewrite and for every screen to check.
- **A choice names its workspace.** What is remembered is the ID of the workspace. A choice that somebody else left on the device names no workspace of whoever is signed in now, and counts as none. Signing out forgets the choice with everything else: the device's, the choice of the tab that signs out, and the choice of every other tab, which forgets its own when it starts again because of the sign-out. Whoever signs in next starts in the sandbox, in whichever tab, also if it is the same person.
- **The name and the data change in one step.** The route that every screen is under hands the workspace and its store to the screens together, and the banner and the switch read the workspace from the same place. So no screen shows the data of one workspace under the name of the other, not between two renders either. Every screen is made anew for the other workspace, so nothing that it held is carried over: an open dialog, a draft that can be brought back. What the address says beyond the screen goes too, such as the draft that is open: a draft is never edited into the other workspace.
- **Notices belong to a workspace.** The offer to undo a deletion leaves the screen with its workspace. The notice of a refused change does too, without being dismissed: it is there again when the workspace is. They leave in the step in which the name changes, like the screens: the list of the notices is made anew for the other workspace. A notice that faded out under the other name could still be pressed there, and its undo would write into the workspace that is not on screen. The exception is a notice that is for the person: how something went that they asked for and have to act on, when they have left the screen that would have said it. So far that is the outcome of a disconnect, section 6. It stays over a change of workspace, because the person still has to hear it and nothing else would tell them. It can then be on screen under the name of the other workspace, so it says which one it is about, and it offers nothing that could be done to the wrong one.
- **Switching does not stop the sync of the workspace that is left.** A tab goes on syncing every workspace that it has shown. Stopping would lose the changes that the tab could not store, which exist in its memory alone, and would keep what was written offline from the server until the person came back to that workspace. It also makes switching back instant. For the same reason the app starts the sync of the workspace that it does not show when the copy of that workspace holds changes that were never sent, for example after a session that ended while the device was offline: the sign-in page promises that they are synced when the same person signs in again.
- **The switch says what the other workspace holds.** The sync indicator is about the workspace on screen, and "Synced" there is not the whole story of a tab that goes on syncing the other one. When the tab holds changes of that one which it could not store, or which wait to be sent, the switch says so: with a dot, in words under it in the sidebar, and in words at that workspace in the choice that it opens, which is where a phone has room. The words are the ones that the indicator would use. It says nothing when there are no such changes, and nothing while they are on their way, which is over in a moment.
