- **Signing out counts both workspaces.** The changes that would be lost are those in the copies of both workspaces and those that a tab could not store, whichever workspace that tab shows: section 4.
- **The first time a workspace is shown on a device, it is empty for a moment.** The screens have no loading state, so until the first answer of the server the lists are empty. After that, switching shows the copy at once.

The Simulator screen, built in phase 3b-3b, and what a new sandbox has:

- **A new sandbox starts empty.** Phase 3b-3a left open whether it should come with accounts already connected and a few sample drafts, to be decided with publishing, which is what they would be for. It does not. Connecting a simulated account is one click on its consent page, and is part of what the sandbox is there to show: how a connection goes, what a network asks, and that pigy never sees a password. Accounts that were there already would skip that, and the first connection that the person makes would be a real one. A channel also stands for the credentials that a network handed out, so a channel that is there from the start would take the server going through a connection on its own: a second way to make channels besides the routes of section 6, for the sandbox alone. Sample drafts would be something to delete before the drafts are one's own. The empty screens say what to do next instead, each where it is needed. And the end-to-end tests stay as they are: every test that is somebody new finds an empty sandbox, and the admin, whom the tests share, has no rows that every test would have to step around.
- **One screen, two sections.** The feeds and the failure panel are the Feeds and the Failures of one screen, the Simulator, which is one item in the navigation: the bar of a phone has room for six. The Sent screen leads to a post in the feeds, and the failure panel to writing a post.
- **It is the sandbox's alone.** The live workspace has no simulated world: no Simulator in its navigation or its command palette, and the address says that there is nothing at it there, with the way to the sandbox. That screen asks nothing of `/api/sandbox`. It is for an address that is typed, kept or opened in a tab that shows live. Whoever is on the Simulator and switches to live asked for live, and not to be told that there is nothing at an address: the switch, which otherwise stays on the screen that is open, goes to the drafts then.
- **The feeds are fetched, and say so.** They are not synced, so they have a loading state and a failure, with a way to try again, which no synced screen has. They are asked for again whenever a delivery of the workspace moves on, which the synced tables say, when the person asks, and when the connection or the window comes back. A delivery does not always say what it has done in the world: a lost answer puts a post there, and a network that asks to be tried again uses up a fault, and the workflow then waits a minute or more without a change of the workspace. So while a delivery is being sent, and only then, the screens of the simulator also ask every few seconds. A refresh that fails leaves what was shown and says that it is from before, with a way to try again.
- **A link names what to bring into view**, a post or an account, in the address: from a delivery on the Sent screen to its post, from a delivery that nobody knows the end of to the account. The feeds bring it into view and give it the focus.
- **The panel offers posting first**, which is what it is there for, and any request. It says in words what each kind of fault does and what pigy does about it, and for the three that make the workflow wait, that the wait is a minute at least. What pigy does depends on the network: on LinkedIn, where it cannot look for a post, a lost answer and a failing server end the delivery as unknown at once, nothing waits, and the panel says that.
- **The list of what is armed is kept true.** A fault is used up by the request that it strikes, which a post from any window makes, so the panel asks for the list again when the feeds are asked for again, which includes every few seconds while a delivery is being sent: a fault that makes the workflow wait has struck long before the delivery moves on. It lists the faults in the order in which they were armed and does not say which strikes next: a fault strikes the first request that it fits, so that is not always the first of the list. Arming and disarming each answer with the list as they left it, and two answers that arrived the other way round would leave the older one on screen: the panel makes one change at a time. The answer of a change and the answer of a read can pass each other in the same way, and a fault that a delivery used up while the answer of "Arm" was on its way would be back on the list. So the list is not read while a change is on its way: a read that is asked for then is made after the answer, and one that the change finds on its way is given up and made again after it.

Simulator state lives in a `SimWorldDO` per sandbox workspace, and so per user: one person's simulated posts are never visible to another.

## 8. Publishing pipeline

1. A post becomes due: the user chose "now", which is the mutator `post.publish`, or from phase 5 the WorkspaceDO alarm fired. The post is `sending` and its targets are `queued`.
2. The WorkspaceDO begins the delivery of each target whose channel is free: the target is `sending`, in a new attempt, and gets one run of `PublishWorkflow`, with an ID that is this delivery's alone so that a second start is a no-op: the workspace, the target and the time at which the workspace began the delivery. A target whose channel has another delivery under way waits for it to end, as described below.
3. The workflow runs durable steps, each with at most one request that changes something on a network: publish part 1, publish part 2 as a reply, and so on, and from phase 7 upload each media item. Each result is written back to the WorkspaceDO as it happens, so the UI shows progress live.
4. Transient failures (rate limit, server error, timeout) retry with backoff. Permanent failures (invalid content, revoked access) stop and mark the target `error` with a readable reason. A failure that leaves open whether a post was published, on a network where pigy cannot find out, marks it `uncertain`: the reason then asks the person to look before they retry. A post that has ended and has not arrived everywhere is told of in the inbox and to the devices of the person: section 13.
5. If a thread fails midway, the sent parts stay recorded. "Retry", the mutator `target.retry`, continues from the failed part and never re-posts a sent one.

Built in phase 3b-2, for text and for Mastodon, the one network that has an adapter. `apps/worker/src/publish` has the workflow, `apps/worker/src/workspace` the queue, and SYNC.md section 4 what a client asks for and sees.

How the workflow and an adapter share the work:

- The content is checked with `validateContent` before the first step, against the limits of the account, which the first step of a run asks of the network again if the channel has had them for more than a day. What fails is not sent: the target ends in `error` with what `validateContent` found, and the other targets of the post go on. Each target is a delivery of its own from beginning to end, so one that fails holds no other back.
- A step of the workflow is one call of `publishStep`. It returns the progress as plain JSON, which is the recorded result of the step and the input of the next. `publish` in `packages/providers` is the loop: it takes the workflow's `step.do` and `step.sleep` and names the steps by their position, the same on a replay.
- The credentials are asked of the WorkspaceDO inside every step, also when a step is run again. A post can take longer than credentials last, and they can be replaced under it: a step that an adapter fails with `refresh` renews them and stores the new ones in the WorkspaceDO, in place of the ones that it started from. The alarm of the WorkspaceDO renews them when `refreshAt` comes, never while a delivery to the channel is under way: section 6, "Renewing an access by the timer". The WorkspaceDO hands the credentials out sealed, as it stores them, and the step opens them where it calls the adapter: section 16.
- A `ProviderError` says what the workflow does:
  - `retry` runs the step again with the same input, after the time the network named if it named one.
  - `refresh` has the step renew the credentials at once, whatever `refreshAt` says, store the new ones in the WorkspaceDO and call the adapter again with them. `publish` does this inside the same workflow step. If the stored credentials are no longer the ones that were refused, another refresh has replaced them, and they are used as they are. A refresh that the network refuses, or new credentials that are refused too, mean `reconnect`.
  - `fail` ends the target with the message.
  - `reconnect` ends it and marks the channel, if the channel still has the connection that was refused. The step says which connection it was refused with, by the `connectedAt` that it read with the credentials, and `target.end` compares: the end of a delivery can be recorded after the person has connected the account again, and marking the channel then would fail every later delivery without trying an access that is good.
  - `uncertain` ends it with the message, which asks the person to look at the account. The workflow does not run the step again by itself. If the person finds nothing and presses "Retry", the step runs again from the recorded progress, and sends.
- A step can also fail with what is no `ProviderError`: a bug in pigy, or what the `fetch` of the adapter threw that is no failure of a network, which an adapter passes on as it is. It has none of the kinds, and the workflow must not read one into it. It logs the failure, and the step is one whose outcome is not known, like a step that was cut off. The target then ends as `uncertain`, with a text that says that pigy cannot tell how far it got and asks the person to look. It is not run again by itself: a bug would be met again, and what it leaves behind is unknown. A person who asks for another try gets one from the recorded progress, where an adapter that can look does.
- Before a step is run again, the run before it has to be over: a request that is still on its way to a network, or still being worked on there, shows nowhere yet. So a target has one run at a time, and the backoff after `retry` starts at a minute, which is longer than an adapter waits for an answer.
- After `retry`, the step is run again when the time has come that the network named, and not before a minute is over. Where the network names none, the waits double from a minute: 1, 2, 4, 8 and 16 minutes. There is an end to it: a step is run six times at most, which gives up on a network that stays out of reach after half an hour, and a network that names a time more than an hour away is not waited for at all. The target then ends in `error`, with what the adapter said and how often it was tried, and the person tries again when they see fit. A delivery that waited for hours would hold up everything behind it on its channel. `apps/worker/src/publish/retry.ts` is the whole policy.
- The deliveries to one channel run one at a time. A target takes its first step only when every target of the same channel that began before it has ended, and until then it waits its turn in the WorkspaceDO, which starts the workflows. An adapter tells the posts of a delivery from everything else on the account by what its first step recorded: Mastodon's by the status that was the account's newest then. Two deliveries to one account that begin together, "Hi" with the reply "a" and "Hi" with the reply "b", record the same status, and the second takes the "Hi" of the first for its own. It replies to it with "b", so one thread holds parts of both, and deleting either post in pigy deletes the first status of the other. No adapter can prevent that. One at a time, each delivery records where the account stands after the one before it has ended. The rule is kept per channel, because that is what a WorkspaceDO sees. An account that is connected as two channels, in two workspaces, is not covered: nothing coordinates the two workspaces, so their deliveries to it can overlap. That goes wrong only if both publish the same first part to the account at the same time.
- A target has ended, for that rule, when it is done, or when it has failed and is not taken up again. A failed target that "Retry" can still continue has not ended: it goes on from what it recorded when it began, and what has gone to the channel since is newer than that. On Mastodon only a target without a published part is concerned, because a later part is found as the reply to the part before it, which no other delivery has.
- Later targets pass a failed one. A target in `error` or `uncertain` does not hold up the queue of its channel, whatever it has published: nobody may ever look at it, and from phase 5 the posts that are scheduled behind it would pile up without a word. The price is paid by a target that has published no part and is not under way: one that failed, and one that was tried again since and waits its turn. On a network whose adapter does not tell the deliveries to an account apart, which is Mastodon of the two that are built, it can no longer go on from what it recorded from the moment that another delivery to its channel has passed its checks and is about to make its first request to the network. The workflow of the other says so to the WorkspaceDO, in a step of its own after `prepare` and before the first step of the adapter, and the WorkspaceDO drops the progress of every other target of the channel that has no recorded part, whatever its status. A delivery that ends before that point drops nothing: its channel needs connecting again, its post does not pass `validateContent`, the limits of the account could not be asked, or pigy has no keys. It has put nothing on the account that another could take for its own. Neither does a delivery that is tried again with nothing left to send, because an attempt before it recorded every part and only its end was lost: it skips the step. A delivery that only has replies left to send does drop the progress of the others, though on Mastodon none of them could take a reply for its first part: the rule does not rest on what the later parts of a post are on a network.
- That price is paid only where it buys something. The rule was written when Mastodon was the only network, whose adapter looks through what the account has posted since the delivery began. Bluesky's looks under a record key that the delivery chose and recorded, where no other delivery writes: a post of another delivery is nothing that it could take for its own, and the recorded key is the only way to a first part that landed unseen. Dropping it made a retry choose a new key, find nothing there and post the part a second time, with nothing gained. So an adapter says which kind it is, with `tellsDeliveriesApart` of the contract, and the workflow of a delivery to a network whose adapter does leaves the step out: nothing is dropped, and a target that others have passed goes on from its progress. The step was also where a delivery found out that it had been ended before it sent anything. Without it, a delivery that is taken up with a key chosen sends in its first step, and still not for a delivery that has been ended: while its run is running, only the disconnect of its channel ends a delivery that has passed its checks, and that removes the credentials, which every step asks the workspace for before it asks the network for anything. It is a property of the adapter and not of each progress, because one channel has one adapter, and what a step looks for does not change within a network. LinkedIn's cannot look and says no, which costs nothing, since it records nothing. Threads' records a container, which is the delivery's own and says whether it was published, but finds the post of a published container among what the account has posted since the delivery began: it says no, section 6. The deliveries to one channel still run one at a time on every network: a refresh is one at a time per channel, and deliveries are what refreshes.
- What "Retry" guarantees follows from that. It does not send again what was recorded. A part that landed unseen, which is one that reached the network without pigy getting the answer, it finds where the adapter can look. On Bluesky that is all: the part is under its recorded key whatever has gone to the account since. On Mastodon it holds unless another delivery to the same account has got as far as asking the network in between, whether or not it posted anything: then the retry starts over as a new delivery, no longer finds a first part that landed unseen, and that part may be posted twice. A delivery can end as `error` with a first part unseen in three ways: the network stayed out of reach through every retry after the request that got through, the look that follows a lost answer was answered with a 429 that names a time more than an hour away, which `retry.ts` does not wait for, or the network refused the credentials on that look. A target that nothing has passed goes on from its progress, and finds such a part. A target that has published a part keeps its progress whatever passes it.
- A step may ask for a wait before the next one, which is how Threads gets the time to prepare a post, and how media will get the time to be processed.
- The progress lists the published parts one by one. "Retry" starts a new run from the progress that was recorded, with the same key. A delivery that starts over has the same key too: it is still the delivery of that target, and a network that remembers the key answers with the post it made for it, which is the one thing that can still spare a second post.
- The key names the delivery: the ID of the target. It is what Mastodon's idempotency keys are made from, so publishing a post a second time on purpose takes a new key. That rests on the ID of a target never being used again, which holds for the app: it makes up a new ID for every target. A client that deletes a post and adds a target under the same ID, to the same account and within the hour that Mastodon remembers a key, is answered with the first post and sees the second as sent. It harms nobody but its own workspace. A key that the server makes for each target, and keeps through its retries, would close that, and belongs with the REST API of phase 6 if its clients are to choose IDs.

How the WorkspaceDO and the workflow share the work. `apps/worker/src/workspace/store.ts` and `object.ts` are the one, `apps/worker/src/publish/workflow.ts` the other:

- **The queue is the targets.** There is no table for it. A target that is `queued` waits, in the order of `queuedAt`, which no two targets of a channel share: the WorkspaceDO runs every mutation at a later time than the one before. `startDeliveries` begins, for every channel without a target that is `sending`, the one that has waited longest. That and the mutator it runs, `target.start`, which refuses while another target of the channel is `sending`, are the only place where a delivery begins.
- **An alarm starts the workflows.** Every change of a workspace ends with a look at whether a delivery can begin, and if so the alarm of the object is set to now, in the same write as the change. The alarm marks the targets `sending`, creates their runs, and notes for each that it has. A run that the platform does not create is logged and left for the next alarm, a minute later: it does not keep the runs of the other deliveries from being created, and no change brings the alarm forward for it. Otherwise every keystroke of a client would cost an alarm, a request to the platform and a line in the log for as long as Workflows is down. If the object stops in between, the alarm runs again and creates what is missing: the platform takes an ID once, and a run that is there under the ID of a delivery is that delivery's own. The ID is made of the workspace, the target and the time at which the workspace began the delivery, which is the time of the mutation that made the target `sending` and so no other delivery's. The target and its attempt would not do: the runs of all workspaces share their IDs, a client makes up the ID of a target, which is unique within its workspace only, and a target that is deleted with its post and added again under the same ID begins at its first attempt again. A delivery would then find the run of another under its ID, take it for its own, and never be carried out.
- **The object watches its runs.** A workflow tells the workspace how a delivery ended, as its last step. A run that cannot, because the platform gave up on a step of it, or somebody terminated it, would leave its target `sending` for good, with every later post to the channel behind it and nothing that a person could do. So while a delivery is under way, the alarm comes back every minute and asks the platform for the status of each run. A run that is over without having ended its delivery has its target ended as `uncertain`.
- **A run reports what it is, and is refused when it is not.** Every report names the target and the attempt. A report on a target that is not `sending` in that attempt is refused, and the run stops: the delivery was ended otherwise, by a disconnect or by the watch above, and may have begun anew. That is the guard in the mutators, `currentTarget` in `packages/core`.
- **A report is carried out once.** A step of a workflow is run again when its answer was lost, and a call that was given up on can arrive after the ones that followed it. A report that was carried out twice in the wrong order would put a delivery back to where it had fewer parts. So every call that changes the workspace comes with a key, and the object carries a call with a key out once: it keeps the outcome under the key, in the transaction of the change, and answers a repeat with it. The key is the ID of the run, what the call is for, and a digest of what it says. The name of a step is not enough: a run that somebody restarts takes its steps again from the first, with other things to say. `mutateWorkspace` takes such a key from any caller, which is what the REST API of phase 6 needs for the `Idempotency-Key` of a request.
- **What goes wrong in a step is its result.** A step that talks to a network catches what the adapter throws and returns it, as a description: the kind, the message, how long to wait. The workflow reads it and decides. An error that is thrown out of a step reaches the workflow without its class, and the platform runs such a step again on its own schedule, which is not the network's. The platform's own retries are left for a step that was cut off: its time was up, the Worker went away under it, or the workspace could not be asked for what the step needs before it does anything.
- **What the platform throws is not caught.** The workflow ends a delivery only for what it has seen fail itself. When the platform stops a run, to pause it, to restart it or because it has given up on a step, that arrives in the workflow as an error like any other, and taking it for a failure of the delivery would end a delivery that is only being moved. It is thrown on, and the watch above ends the delivery if the run is over.
- **Credentials.** A step asks the WorkspaceDO for the sealed credentials of its channel and opens them where it calls the adapter, every time it is run. Nothing of them is in what the run was started with or in what a step returns, which the platform stores and shows. A run is started with the workspace, the target and the attempt, and its first step returns the text of the post, which every step after it is handed, as the contract of an adapter asks.
- **A refresh** is asked for by the step that the network refused, with the credentials that were refused. Bluesky is the first network that takes this path, every time a delivery finds its access token ended: `test/bluesky.test.ts` of the Worker has a post renew the access and a later one go out with what was stored. It is carried out only if those are still the ones that the workspace holds, and what it returns is stored only in their place. One at a time per channel follows from one delivery at a time per channel, and from the one thing besides that refreshes, the alarm of the WorkspaceDO, leaving alone a channel that a delivery is under way to: section 6, "Renewing an access by the timer". On some networks a refresh ends the credentials that it started from, so what it returns must not be lost: when the WorkspaceDO does not take the new credentials, the step hands them over again, the same sealed text, up to four times within half a minute. If the WorkspaceDO still does not take them, the step goes on with them and the log says so: they are good for that step, the next one reads the old ones, and on such a network the account then has to be connected again. Ending the step instead would only bring that forward.

What happens to a delivery when something is taken from under it:

- **The post.** A post that is `sending` is not deleted: `post.delete` is refused until its last target has ended. Deleted in the middle, it would leave posts on the networks that nothing in pigy knows of. It cannot be edited from the moment it is published, so every step is handed the same content.
- **The channel.** Disconnecting removes the channel at once, as before, and ends what was to go to it, in the same change: a target that is `queued` or `sending` ends in `error`, with the parts that it has published. The run finds out at its next step, where the workspace has no credentials for it or refuses its report, and stops. The request that was on its way is refused by the network, which has taken the token back, or carried out: such a part is on the network with no result in pigy. A target that had ended stays, with the ID of a channel that is gone, and cannot be tried again.
- **The WorkspaceDO.** Everything that it knows of a delivery is in its database, so it can leave memory at any time. A run that cannot reach it tries again for hours.
- **The Worker.** A deploy or a move in the middle of a step makes the platform run the step again, a minute later at the earliest, with the results of the steps before. That is the case that an adapter is built for.

An account that is connected as two channels, in two workspaces, stays as it was: nothing coordinates their deliveries, and nothing was added. Keeping them apart takes a lock per account that every WorkspaceDO goes through, for a case that needs two people to publish the same first part to a shared account in the same moment, and whose outcome is a thread that both can see and delete.

Avoiding duplicates when a step is run again. A step may have reached the network with only its answer lost, and it may be run again a moment or days later. So an adapter does not rely on what a network remembers for a while. Before a request that publishes, the recorded progress holds what a later run needs to find out whether the request was carried out, and a step that does not find it there records it first and sends nothing. A step that finds it asks the network:

| Network | Recorded before the request | A step that is run again |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mastodon | The account's ID, its newest status and the visibility that every part is posted with, by a first step that sends nothing. For a later part, the part before it | Looks through the account's statuses that are newer than that for one with the text of the part that replies to the part before it, or to nothing for the first part. In the text, the case of a mention does not count, and neither does its domain if that is one of the instance's own, which the first step also records. The domain of another instance counts. The status also has to have the recorded visibility, and nothing besides its text: no media, poll, quote or content warning. Takes it for the part if it is there, posts if it is not. The `Idempotency-Key`, made from target and part, is sent as well: Mastodon remembers it for an hour |
| Bluesky | The record key of the part, a TID, chosen by a step of its own that sends nothing | Looks for the record under that key and takes it if it is what the step writes: at the address of the key, a post with the text of the part, replying to the first and the previous part by the versions that were published, or to nothing for the first part, and with nothing else, no picture, links, language or labels. Only the time at which it was written may be any. Writes it under that key if it is not there. Section 6 says why it looks first |
| Threads | The container of the part, by the step that makes it, which publishes nothing. For the first part also where the posts of the account stood then: the time of the newest and the IDs of the posts of that second, or that there were more of them than it read. And how often the step has looked for a post that Threads does not list | Asks Threads for the status of the container before it publishes. `FINISHED`: it publishes, and asks for the status once more if Threads refuses. `PUBLISHED`: an earlier run has published it, so the step publishes nothing and looks for the post: a later part among the replies to the part before it, the first part among what the account has posted since the delivery began, in both a post of plain text with the text of the part. A post that is not found is looked for three times more, ten seconds apart, and is not sent again: the step fails with `uncertain`, because Threads has said that it was published. `IN_PROGRESS`: it publishes nothing and comes back. `EXPIRED`, which a container is that was not published within 24 hours, and a container that Threads no longer knows, say nothing certain of whether they were published: the step looks for the post, and makes a new container only if there is none. That is a step of its own, so the new ID is recorded before anything is published with it. Where the step cannot tell whether the post is there, because it could not look through enough of the account, it makes none and fails with `uncertain`. Meta does not document what publishing a published container answers, so a step never does that knowingly (to verify, section 19) |
| LinkedIn | Nothing that would help: LinkedIn lets pigy read no posts and takes no key | Sends. Because that is not safe, a request that ends without a clear answer is not retried: the step fails with `uncertain`, and a person looks. And the workflow does not run a step of this adapter a second time: see below. Inside about ten minutes LinkedIn refuses the same content again and names the post that it has, which the adapter takes for the post of the delivery where an attempt before may have sent (to verify) |

What this guarantees: no part is published twice, however late a step is run again, as long as the run before it is over and what it published is still on the network as it was published. A status that the person has edited or deleted in the meantime is not found, and is posted again. Not on Threads, where the container says that the part was published: there such a part is never posted again, and the delivery says so.

The rule by which Mastodon's adapter finds a status cannot take another post for the one it looks for unless that post says the same in the same place to the same readers, has nothing but its text, and is newer than the delivery: the person posted the text in the meantime, by hand or with another app. The two would have been duplicates. It is never the post of another delivery to the same channel, because those run one at a time. Without that rule the claim would not hold, and for an account that two workspaces have connected it does not: see above.

The text of a status is not the text that was sent. Mastodon drops the whitespace around it, and it writes every mention of an account that it finds the way the account is written: on mastodon.example, `@ADA@mastodon.example` is saved as `@ada`, and `@gargron@mastodon.social` as `@Gargron@mastodon.social`. Which accounts a server finds, only the server knows, so the adapter does not rewrite as Mastodon does. It compares the two texts with every mention in lower case, and without its domain if that is the instance's own, which are the two things Mastodon changes in a mention it has found. A status that differs from a part only in the case of a mention, or in naming the instance where the part does not, is therefore taken for it: both mention the same account. A mention of the same name on another instance is somebody else, and keeps its domain, so such a status is not taken.

The visibility is settled by the first step and not left to Mastodon, so that a step knows what it looks for: the one the adapter was made with, or else the account's own setting, read from the account. A status with the same text for other readers is not the post, and taking a status for the account alone as a public part would leave the post unpublished. Mastodon makes "unlisted" of what an account that its moderators have limited posts as "public", so for such an account the first step records "unlisted".

A step reads one list of 40 statuses. If the account has posted more than that since, the step posts nothing and notes how far it has looked, and the next step goes on from there, so a post is never sent before everything newer has been looked at. A status that is not newer than the recorded one is never looked at, also where a server that does not know `min_id` lists it: it is from before the delivery, however much it reads like a part.

What `uncertain` does not cover is a step that is cut off after its request went out and before any outcome was recorded: its time was up, the Worker went away under it, or somebody restarted the run. A workflow runs such a step again, and an adapter that cannot look sends a second time without ever having failed. Only LinkedIn's is of that kind, and says so with `repeatsSafely`. Phase 3d settled it, with the smallest change that does:

- **A step says that it begins before it sends.** For an adapter that does not repeat safely, the workflow calls the adapter only after the WorkspaceDO has noted that the step begins: under the ID of the run and the name of the step, with a mark that this run of the step has made up for itself. The WorkspaceDO keeps the first mark that it is handed under a key and answers every call with the mark that it keeps, which is what its calls with a key do anyway (`keyed_call`, section 5). A run of the step that is handed its own mark is the first, and calls the adapter. One that is handed another is a second run of a step that began before: it does not call the adapter, and the delivery ends as `uncertain`, with a text that says that pigy was interrupted and cannot find out whether the post arrived.
- **The note is taken last**, when the credentials of the channel are read and opened and nothing is left but to call the adapter. Earlier, a step that failed before it had anything to send would leave a note behind.
- **It is keyed by the run and the step, and by nothing that the step says**, unlike the reports of a run, whose keys hold a digest of what they say. A run that is restarted takes its steps again from the first under the same names, and has to find what the run before it noted. A step that a network asks to have tried again is a step of another name, and "Retry" is another run: both have notes of their own.
- **A note whose answer is lost is asked for again, with the same mark.** The WorkspaceDO can note the step and lose its answer, as when a deploy resets it under the call. A step that failed for that would be run again by the platform with a mark of its own, find the first, and end as `uncertain` with nothing sent. So the step repeats the call a few times before it gives up, as it does for renewed credentials, and is handed its own mark back.
- **A delivery outlasts its notes, and then does not send.** A call with a key is kept for 30 days, and a run can stand for longer: the platform keeps a run that is paused, and what it keeps of one that has ended it keeps from the end on. A step that is cut off with its request out, and whose run is taken up after the note is cleared away, would be handed its own mark and post again, long after LinkedIn has stopped refusing the text. So a step that finds no note for a delivery that began 29 days ago or longer is answered with no mark, and ends the delivery as `uncertain` like a second run. The other way was to keep such notes for as long as their delivery can go on, and to clear them when it ends or begins anew: more code in every place where a delivery ends, for a delivery that waits a month before its first request, which this one fails without need.
- **The price is an `uncertain` that was not needed.** A step that is cut off between the note and its request has sent nothing, and neither has one that could not tell the WorkspaceDO for longer than it tries, nor one whose delivery is older than its notes are kept. All end as `uncertain`. The person who looks finds nothing, and tries again. A run that somebody restarts after a step had ended with a clear answer finds the note of that step too. That is accepted: the other way round is a second post.
- **The workspace remembers that an attempt may have sent.** A target has a column that is not synced, `may_have_sent`, which is set where an attempt of its delivery ends `uncertain`: `endDelivery` of the store, which the end that a workflow reports and the end that the alarm makes of a workflow without a word both go through, in the transaction of `target.end`. It stays set over the attempts after that one, whatever they end with, because an attempt that was refused has learned nothing of the one before: `uncertain`, then a try that is refused for a limit, then another try still takes the post that LinkedIn names. It goes when the delivery is sent, with the target when that is removed, and when the delivery begins anew: `aboutToSend` takes it back together with the progress, for the same reason. Another delivery to the account is about to post, and where the two read the same, the post that LinkedIn names from then on can be the other one's. The delivery whose own post did land unseen then fails when it is tried again within those minutes, with the link to its post, where it could have said "Sent": that is the price of never having two deliveries share a post, and nothing is posted twice for it. The workflow hands it to the adapter as `mayHaveSentBefore`, from what `prepare` returns, which has the field only where it is true: a run that began before a deploy that added it reads as one that has sent nothing. The migration marks the targets that stand as `uncertain` in a workspace from before the column.
- **Nothing else changes.** An adapter that repeats safely is called as before and nothing is noted for it. No table was added, and nothing of the note or of the column is synced. The schema version of sync was raised in phase 3d all the same, to 5, for another reason: a channel can hold the limits of LinkedIn, which an older app would refuse. Phase 3e raised it to 6 for the limits of Threads and for the mutator `channel.lapse`.

What is left open on LinkedIn is what the network leaves open:

- A person who looks, finds nothing because LinkedIn has not shown the post yet, and tries again after the minutes in which LinkedIn refuses a repetition, posts twice.
- A refusal as a repetition names a post and not who made it. A delivery of which no attempt may have sent fails on it, in every attempt. A delivery of which one may have takes the named post for its own, and that is wrong where the attempt had not sent after all and the post is somebody else's: the member posted the text by hand, or from another pigy, after an attempt that ended `uncertain` without a post, and then tried the delivery again within those minutes. The delivery then says "Sent" and links to a post that it did not make, and deleting it would delete that post. A delivery of the same workspace does not get there: it takes the note from the others to its channel before it posts. LinkedIn would not publish the text a second time within those minutes whatever pigy did.
- An attempt ends `uncertain` also where it cannot have sent: a failure of pigy's own before the request, or a workflow that stopped in its first step. The workspace does not tell those from the ones that may have sent, and the note stays for as long as the target does. So the case of the item before is open for longer than it has to be.
- An answer from 400 to 499 to the request that posts which is longer than pigy reads is taken by its status alone. A 429 of that kind waits for LinkedIn's next day and not for the time that the answer may have named, and what LinkedIn said is not in the message.

The app's side of publishing, built in phase 3b-3b: the editor of a draft chooses where it goes and publishes it, and the Sent screen follows each delivery.

- **Where a draft goes is chosen in its editor, and kept like its text.** Under the text, every channel of the workspace is a button. Choosing one is `target.add`, leaving it out `target.remove`: the choice is saved at once, syncs to the other devices, and stays with the draft.
- **What a chosen account would refuse is said before publishing, and keeps the post back.** The editor checks the text with `validateContent` against the limits of each chosen channel, from its row, and so counts the way each network does: a link as 23 characters on Mastodon, graphemes and bytes on Bluesky. It says next to the choice which account refuses what. An empty text is said once, for the draft. A channel whose limits are not known yet is not checked: the server checks before it sends. Phase 4a added the counters while typing and the text per account, below.
- **A channel that needs connecting again cannot be chosen**, and says so with a way to connect it again. One that was chosen before it broke stays chosen and keeps the post back until it is connected again or left out: publishing would only fail it at once.
- **Publishing offline is publishing.** `post.publish` is a mutation like every other: the draft leaves the list at once, and the post is on the Sent screen as waiting. The editor, the notice after publishing and the Sent screen say that it goes out when the device is back online. The device cannot tell whether the server has heard of a post before it went offline, so the Sent screen says that what it shows is what the device knows.
- **After publishing, the editor closes** and a notice leads to the post on the Sent screen. The notice is of the workspace, and leaves with it.
- **The Sent screen lists the posts by when they were first asked to go out**: the earliest `queuedAt` of their targets. The time of the post changes whenever a delivery ends, and the list would reorder itself as the deliveries went on. A target that is tried again is asked for anew, so a post whose every target is tried again moves up, which for a post to one channel is every retry. That is accepted: it is the post that the person is busy with.
- **The statuses are words**: a delivery is waiting, sending, sent, failed, or unknown for `uncertain`. Waiting says that it waits its turn, unknown that pigy cannot tell whether the post arrived. That is said once: an unknown delivery comes with its reason, which says that pigy cannot tell and to look at the account first, and under it stands only what the reason leaves out, that trying again may post twice. A post is sending, sent, failed, or partly sent where some of its deliveries arrived and some did not. A post none of whose deliveries is known to have failed, and one of which is unknown, is unknown too, and not failed. Its time is said as when it was published only if something of it is known to be: otherwise as when it was tried, or started while it is sending.
- **A post of the network is one link.** A delivery links to what it has published, part by part. Where a network has joined the parts of a thread into one post, as LinkedIn does, the parts share its ID, and the delivery has one link, not one per part.
- **"Retry" is offered where it can work**: on a delivery that failed or is unknown, whose channel is there and connected. A channel that needs connecting again offers that instead, and a delivery to a channel that is gone offers nothing, because the mutator would refuse. A delivery that is unknown asks the person to look at the account first, with a link to it: its page at the network, or its feed in the sandbox. LinkedIn gives pigy no address of an account, so there the link is to where LinkedIn shows everybody their own posts, and the text says that LinkedIn gives pigy no way to look. "Retry" stays next to it: the person decides.
- **A post can be deleted once it has ended, and not while it is sending.** The server refuses that, so the screen does not offer it. Deleting asks first and says that the networks keep what was published. There is no undo, unlike for a draft: a post that went out cannot be made again.
- **A published part links to where it is**: to its address at the network in a live workspace, and to its place in the feeds in the sandbox, whose posts have no address that a browser can open.

Threads and text per account, built in phase 4a. `packages/core/src/sync/mutators.ts` has the mutators, SYNC.md section 4 what a client sends, and `apps/web/src/routes/_app/index.tsx` the editor:

- **A draft is a list of parts, and the editor shows them one under the other.** Each part is a field that grows with its text. Under it are its counters and what can be done with it: add a part below, move it up or down, remove it. ⌘⇧↵ adds a part below from the text, ↑ and ↓ at the very edge of a part go to the part next to it, and ⌫ in an empty part removes it. A part that had text and is removed with its button can be brought back by its notice. The notice outlives what was on screen when the part went: the editor may show another text of the draft by then, or another draft, or none. So the Undo opens the draft and the text that the part goes back into before the part gets the keyboard, and the keyboard is never left on the page, where ⌫ deletes a draft. A part that is brought back comes with its text, and so does a text for an account: when the server refuses one, because the account was left out on another device meanwhile, the notice of the refusal offers to keep the text as a new draft, as it does for an edit. Moving is by buttons: dragging is not built. None of these keys is taken while an input method composes a character: ↓ and ↑ go through its candidates then, and ⌫ and Escape take back what it holds.
- **The order of the parts is kept by the server.** A mutation says which part a part goes after, and no position. The mutator puts it there on the rows as they are when it runs and numbers the parts anew. The alternative, positions that a client computes between two neighbours, needs fractions that run out or strings to compare, and a rule for two devices that compute the same one. Here the mutations are run one after the other anyway, by the `WorkspaceDO` and by a client on its own copy, so naming the neighbour is enough: two devices that add a part at the same place both get theirs in, and no two parts ever share a place. What it costs is that a part which is added in the middle rewrites the `position` of the parts behind it, which for a thread is a handful of small rows.
- **Counters are per part and per network, for the accounts that get the text.** With no account chosen, a part says how many characters a person would count. With accounts chosen, it says for each network among them how long the part is the way that network counts, against what the account takes: a warning from nine tenths of the limit, an error over it. Accounts with the same limits share a counter. Two Mastodon servers that take different lengths get one each, named by their accounts. An account whose limits are not known yet has no counter. A network that makes one post of a thread, which is LinkedIn, has no counter under a part of a thread, where its limit is not the part's: three parts of 1,500 characters would each say that all is well while the account refuses the post. It has one counter under the last part, of the parts as the post that the account gets, with the empty lines between them, joined by the same function of `packages/core` as in the adapter and in `validateContent`. A text of one part is that post and is counted under its part. The same holds for a text that is written for such an account. What `validateContent` refuses is said next to the choice of the account, as before, and now names the part.
- **An account can get a text of its own, as a whole.** A target either gets the post's parts or has a list of parts of its own, a thread with its own number of parts. Not an override per part: a text for Bluesky is often three parts where Mastodon has one, and an override that had to keep to the parts of the post could not say so. The parts are rows of `post_part` that name their target, so the same mutators edit both, and a result of a delivery names a part either way. "Customize for" an account begins its text as a copy of the post's, in one mutation, so that an account never has half a text and of two devices that begin one, one wins as a whole. The copy does not follow the post afterwards: what is changed in the post's text later is not changed in the copy, which is what a text of one's own means. "Use the post's text" drops it. The button that was pressed leaves the page either way, so the keyboard goes into the first part of the text that is then shown, and not to the page, where its keys open and delete drafts.
- **The editor shows one text at a time**: the post's, or the one of an account, chosen above the text where the accounts with a text of their own are listed. The counters and the checks of an account go by what that account will get. An empty part of the post's own text is said once, for the draft, and only if a chosen account still gets that text. An empty part of the text of one account is said for that account.
- **Leaving an account out drops the text that was written for it**, and a notice offers to take that back, with the text, for as long as the notice of a deleted draft does. So does disconnecting the account, without the offer. A device that was in the middle of that text meanwhile, offline, is refused what it then sends, and told: a part that was written for one account never goes into the text that the others get.
- **Nothing changes under a delivery.** Every mutator that edits a text refuses a post that is past being a draft, as `part.setText` did: adding, removing and moving a part, and giving an account a text or taking it away. So a delivery reads the same text in every step and in every attempt, which is what an adapter that looks for what an earlier step published compares with. The `WorkspaceDO` hands a delivery the parts of its target, the post's or its own, where it handed it the post's: nothing else in the pipeline changed.
- **The Sent screen shows the text that went to an account with its own**, under that account. The post is listed by its own text, or by the text of an account where its own is empty, which it can be when every account has one. The list of drafts does the same, and such a draft is no empty draft: "New draft" takes up again only a draft without any text that goes nowhere.
- **A deleted draft comes back whole.** Undo makes a new post with the parts of the deleted one in their order, chooses its accounts again and gives each the text that it had, with the mutators that made them. Section 19 had asked whether that would need a mutator of its own, and it does not yet. A text of one account can have grown longer than `target.customize` carries, which is 40,000 characters, so that one mutation fits one message of the sync protocol. Such a text comes back in steps that each fit: the first parts with `target.customize`, and each part after them with `part.add`. `ownTextMutations` in `packages/core` makes them, for this undo and for the one of an account that was left out. The steps are several, and the account may have been given a text anew before they run: by the person, who chose it again and then pressed the Undo of the older notice, or on another device. The two texts must not be mixed. On the device, that Undo does nothing then and says that the account has a text of its own again. On the server, where two devices meet, the `target.customize` does nothing for an account that has a text, and each `part.add` that follows says `mustFollow`: it goes after the part before it, which these steps made, or it is refused, and never to the end of a text that they did not begin. Without `mustFollow`, `part.add` keeps its rule for a part that somebody types into: when the part to go after is gone, it goes to the end with its text. A client never makes a mutation that is longer than a message: it would stay at the front of its outbox, and nothing behind it would reach the server. The schemas of the arguments are what bounds a mutation, and a test holds the longest that each takes to the length of a message.
- **pigy publishes at most 100,000 characters to one account**, all parts together: `maxContentLength`, which `validateContent` checks, so the editor says it next to the account and the server refuses the delivery before anything is sent. No network asks for it. The text of a delivery is what its first step returns, for every later step and attempt to read the same, and the platform keeps what a step returns, up to about a megabyte: see section 19. A hundred parts on a server that takes long posts would be more. The alternative, a step that returns a digest and reads the text again, would change what a step returns for the runs that are under way, and buys nothing that anybody would post.

Phase 4b built the rest of phase 4: link cards, and mentions and hashtags marked up for each network. Section 6, "Links, mentions and hashtags", has what was decided. The pipeline got one step for it, `cards`, between the checks and the first step of the adapter: the names of the steps that were there and what they return are as they were, so a delivery that is under way over a deploy goes on. So does one to Bluesky whose step had chosen a key before the deploy: its progress has no facets noted, and its part goes out as text, as section 6 has it. A post that is scheduled or queued takes the same steps when it is due, section 9: its cards are read and its marks made when it goes out, with what the pages and the handles say then, and nothing of either is kept from when it was written.

Phase 7 gave the pipeline one more step, `media`, between `cards` and the steps of the adapter, for a post that has images: section 10. It asks nothing of a network. For each image of the parts that are still to be published it says which file goes out, the image as the workspace keeps it or a smaller one, which it makes with the Images binding if the bucket does not have it, and what that file is. The step returns the names of the files and their types, sizes and dimensions, a few lines for an image and no bytes, so every step of the adapter in the run is handed the same. It goes by the limits of the account as `prepare` has just stored them. An image whose bytes are gone, and one that cannot be brought within the limits, end the delivery as failed, with words for the person. A failure of the bucket or of what makes images is not the delivery's: the step has changed nothing anywhere, so it is tried again as a step that a network asks to have tried again is, `media-try-2` and on, with the same waits, and if it never works the delivery ends as failed, with words that say that the images could not be made ready. Never as `uncertain`, which would have the person look at an account that was asked nothing, and mark the delivery as one that may have sent. Leaving it to the platform did both: the platform gives up on a step after a few tries, and the object of the workspace ends a delivery whose run stopped without a word as `uncertain`. The names of the steps that were there stay as they were, and a run from before the deploy has no images and skips the step. A post with an image that the server does not have yet, or no longer, does not get this far: `prepare` refuses it, because `validateContent` judges the images of a content. The steps of the adapter read the bytes from the bucket through `mediaSource` of `apps/worker/src/media/variants.ts`, by the names that the step gave, and through nothing else: a name that is no file of an image is no key of the bucket.

## 9. Scheduling and queue

- **Exact time**: the WorkspaceDO keeps one alarm set to the earliest due item. No polling.
- **Queue**: weekly slots (weekday and time) in the user's timezone. "Add to queue" takes the next free slot, "share next" takes the first slot and shifts the rest. Posts can be reordered, and the queue can be paused.
- **Views**: a queue list and a calendar, both reading local data.
- The same alarm also drives token refresh, reconnect reminders and stats refresh.

Phase 5 built this. `SYNC.md` section 4, "Scheduling", is the contract. What was decided, and why:

- **The due time of a queued post is stored, and the mutators compute it.** The alternative was to store only the order and compute the times wherever they are needed. But the alarm of the object needs the next time without a client, the Queue screen and the calendar need all of them, and the API of phase 6 will hand them out: three places that would each compute, and could disagree. So a post of the queue has its place, `queuePosition`, which is what a person arranges, and its time, `dueAt`, which follows from the place, the slots, the time zone and the pause. One function in `packages/core/src/sync/mutators.ts`, `requeue`, gives the posts the slots after the time of the mutation, in their order, and every mutator that changes any of the four runs it. It is deterministic: the same rows and the same `tx.now` give the same times in the browser and in the Worker. Time passing needs no mutation: the first post goes out at its slot, and the others have theirs.
- **What a change of the queue costs is bounded.** `requeue` reads the clocks of a time zone for every post of the queue, which is the one slow thing that a mutator does, and a client runs its unconfirmed mutations again with every change, a keystroke included. Four things keep that small. A mutator runs `requeue` once, however many posts it changes: disconnecting an account with 500 queued posts ran it 500 times, three seconds of CPU, and with 2000 posts past what a Durable Object has. `timeOf` keeps the moments that it has worked out. The places only have to grow along the queue and may leave numbers out, so a post that goes out or leaves the queue changes no other row, and neither does one that enters at either end: a patch of one row to every client, where it was one of every queued post. And a workspace has at most 500 scheduled posts, the queue included, `maxScheduled`: the next is refused with `too_many_scheduled`, and the person is told. 500 is a guess at more than anybody schedules by hand. Phase 6, where an API can schedule, did not raise it: a script that needs more is told so with a 400, and whoever raises it has to show that the cost holds. The one request of the API that changes many slots at once pauses the queue while it does: section 12.
- **Time zones are computed with `Intl.DateTimeFormat`**, which the browser and the Workers runtime both have, in `packages/core/src/schedule/zone.ts`: no library, and no table of zones in the bundle. The two can have time zone data of different ages. That is the ordinary case of a client that computed something for display: the server's rows replace it. The server refuses a zone that it does not know, so it never computes with another zone than the one that is stored. It stores a zone under its own name for it, `canonicalTimeZone`, and not as a client wrote it: names of zones are read without regard to case, so every spelling of every zone was a valid argument, and the first form kept a format for each that it was asked about, some tens of kilobytes, for the life of the isolate. A client that sent a hundred spellings a message took the isolate past its memory, with the objects of other workspaces in it. Now a question about a zone keeps nothing, the formats that are computed with are kept up to 64, and a workspace holds one of the few hundred names that the server has. That name need not be the one that a browser reports for the same zone, Asia/Calcutta and Asia/Kolkata, so the app compares zones with `sameTimeZone`, by what the device makes of both names, wherever it asks whether the zone of the workspace is the device's.
- **A device that cannot compute in the zone of the workspace says its times in UTC, and says so.** Another device, with newer time zone data, can have set a zone that this browser does not have. The times of the posts are in their rows, so they are right. What this device cannot do is read a date and a time in that zone, or show one. It does both in UTC, by name: the dialog that asks for a time says "In UTC", with the reason, and so do the Queue screen and the calendar. It does not read what somebody types as UTC under the name of the other zone: the moment that a dialog makes is all that the server gets, and it cannot correct it. The same goes for a workspace that has slots or scheduled posts and no zone, which is one whose device claimed a zone that the server refused: the server computes its slots in UTC, so the app shows UTC until somebody sets a zone, and does not claim the device's zone again with every post, to be refused again each time.
- **Daylight saving.** A slot is a time on the clocks, so it stays at 09:00 when the clocks change. A time that a day does not have, in the hour that is skipped, is used at the moment the gap ends plus what the time was into it, and not left out: somebody with a slot at 02:30 gets a post that night, an hour later by the clock. A time that a day has twice is used once, the first time. This is what `Temporal` calls "compatible". Two slots that land on one moment that way count once. Where the clocks skip the hour before midnight, as in Nuuk, a slot late on Saturday is carried to after a slot at midnight on Sunday: the slots come out in the order of their moments, also across two days, and neither is lost.
- **The time zone is the workspace's, and is the device's to begin with.** The app sets it with the zone of the device before the first thing that goes by it: a slot, a queued or scheduled post. Not when a workspace is opened: that would be a mutation with every first visit, for something that most visits do not need. Settings show it and change it, and every time in the workspace is shown in it, also on a device that is somewhere else, which the screens say: the Queue screen and the calendar have a line that names the zone wherever it is not the zone of the device, and the dialog that asks for a time always names it. A notice that says when a post was scheduled or queued for names the zone too, wherever it is not the device's or the times are in UTC: it is read on the drafts, which have no such line.
- **A scheduled post is not edited in place.** "Edit" takes it back to the drafts, where it is edited and scheduled again. Editing in place would be kinder to a typo, and the mutators could allow it, since no delivery is under way before the time comes. It was left out because every mutator of a text and of a choice of accounts rests on one guard, "only a draft is edited", and so do the screens: the list of drafts, the undo notices, the notices of refused changes. A second editable status touches all of them. The cost is that a queued post loses its place when it is edited. Section 21 has it as a later addition.
- **A post that is found late.** The object can miss a time: a deploy, an outage, an alarm that the platform delivers late. And a device can schedule a post without a connection and reach the server after the time. Both are one case, and one rule: up to an hour late, the post is sent as soon as the server knows. Later than that, a post with a time that somebody chose is **held**: it stays scheduled, the Queue screen says that it missed its time, and the person sends it, reschedules it or takes it back. A post of the queue takes the next slot. Held or moved, the post says when: `heldAt`, and its `updatedAt` with it, as for everything that is done to a post. An hour is a guess at where "a little late" ends: an announcement that goes out twenty minutes late is still what was meant, and "see you in ten minutes" the next morning is not. The inbox tells the person of a held post, and their devices with it: the mutator that holds the post makes a notification of a kind of its own, `post_held`, which leads to the Queue screen and goes when the post is released, put aside or deleted (section 13). Until phase 9 had notifications, only the Queue screen and the calendar said it.
- **So `post.schedule` takes a time in the past**, and does not refuse it. Refusing would lose the schedule of a post that was a minute late for no fault of the person, with a notice that a change was refused. The mutator takes such a post up itself, the way `post.due` would: sent, or held. So no mutation ever finds a post that is scheduled for the past, on the server or in what a device shows, and what becomes of a post does not depend on what else is in the message that scheduled it.
- **One post of the queue at a time.** When the time of several queued posts has come, because the alarm was late or the server away, the first is sent, if it is at most an hour late, and the others take the slots after now, in their order. Sent one after the other they would be a burst to the same accounts, which the slots are there to prevent. Before, an alarm that came 50 minutes late over slots a quarter of an hour apart sent three.
- **A post never goes out twice because it was deleted and brought back.** Deleting a scheduled post offers Undo, and the Undo is the same post again, never a copy. The delete of the Queue screen only puts the post aside, `post.trash`: a mark on its row, `trashedAt`, with which the post is not due, has no place in the queue, counts for nothing and is on no screen. The Undo takes the mark off, `post.restore`, and the post has its time or its place again. When the notice with the Undo has gone, the device deletes the row, `post.delete` with `ifTrashed`, and the object of the workspace does so a day later for a device that never did: its window was closed. The danger is the order in which a server hears of it: a phone without a connection deletes a post two minutes before its time, Undo, and is back twenty minutes after the time, when the server has sent the post. So each of the three steps is about the one row, and does nothing where it does not apply: only a scheduled post is put aside, and a phone that is behind is refused and told that the post went out, or that somebody took it back to the drafts. Only a post that is put aside is restored or deleted so. The first form made a copy for the Undo and scheduled that, with two guards: the delete only for a scheduled post, the copy only if the post was gone. They did not hold. A post that went out, and whose record another device then deleted from Sent, was gone when the copy asked, and went out a second time as its copy. And two devices that each deleted and undid the same post each had a copy accepted. A copy has to prove afterwards that its original was deleted by this very deletion, which nothing in a row says. Keeping the row needs no proof. It costs a column that every copy holds, and rows that are kept for a day at most. A post that is restored after as many posts were scheduled as a workspace can have, or after its accounts were disconnected, is a draft: it is never lost, and never one too many.
- **The pause holds the queue and nothing else.** A post with a time that somebody chose goes out while the queue is paused: the pause is for "nothing by itself for now" of the stream of queued posts, and a time that was chosen for one post is a promise about that post. While the queue is paused its posts have no time, and when it goes on they take the slots from then: a slot that passed during the pause is not used, and nothing goes out in a burst.
- **An account that is disconnected leaves the scheduled posts**, as it leaves the drafts, and a scheduled post that went nowhere else is a draft again. The alternative, to keep the target and fail it when the time comes, would fail a post hours later for something the person did on purpose, and a target of a channel that is gone cannot be tried again.
- **One path into the pipeline.** A due post is sent by the mutator `post.due`, which does what `post.publish` does: the targets are `queued`, and `startDeliveries` begins them, in the turn of their channel. Nothing else is new in the pipeline: a due post whose account needs reconnecting or whose text no longer passes fails as a published one does, on the Sent screen.
- **The alarm.** The object has one alarm, for everything that it does by itself: the scheduled posts, the deliveries, the accesses of section 6, the images of section 10, the inbox and the push messages of section 13, and the stats of section 14. One function of `object.ts`, `#nextLookIn`, says when it is next needed, and is the one list of what it is for. The alarm is set to the earliest of:
  - at once, after a change that made a delivery ready to begin, or ready but for an access that is overdue for renewal, which the alarm renews first;
  - in a minute, `deliveryWatchMs`, while a delivery is under way, to see that it ends and to create its workflow if that failed;
  - at once, when the alarm stopped renewing accesses because a delivery was ready, for the rest of them. It has renewed one at least, so that ends;
  - at once, when a delivery waits for an access that is overdue for renewal and the alarm did not turn to that access. The delivery came to wait while the alarm was busy: the alarm takes its list of the accesses that are due once, without the channels that are being sent to, and the delivery to such a channel can end, and the next one be queued, while the alarm waits for the network of another. It then waited a minute for nothing. The next alarm turns to that access, which is no longer overdue afterwards, whatever came of it, so that ends too. Not when renewing failed as a whole, before the alarm could say what it turned to;
  - at once, when the alarm stopped waiting for a pass beside it, the push messages to be sent or the stats to be read, because a change asked for a delivery to begin, or queued a push message while the alarm waited for the stats (sections 13 and 14). It takes a change to get there, so that ends. The alarm also stops waiting at the time of the next scheduled post, which comes back at that time by the moment of the post in this list. Not at the time of the renewal of an access, or of what an image is due for, which wait for the pass (sections 10 and 14);
  - a day after a post was put aside, to delete it, and in a minute if that has just failed;
  - the time of the next scheduled post. If that time has passed and the object has looked for due posts since, taking the post up has failed, and it comes back in a minute;
  - at once, after a change that queued a push message, and when the pass over the push messages stopped at its limit with some left, which has sent some, so that ends. In a minute while a message waits otherwise: one that is left could not be sent a moment ago, or a pass is sending it beside the alarm;
  - the time of the next renewal of an access, and a minute from now at the earliest, `renewalPauseMs`;
  - the time at which the inbox next has something to do by the clock, which is to tell of an access that ends in a week or to remove a notification that is 30 days old, and a minute from now at the earliest.
  - the time at which an image is next due for something, section 10: an upload to give up, an image in no part to delete, bytes to let go of, the parts of an upload to drop again. A minute from now at the earliest, `deliveryWatchMs`, which is when the rest is taken where a look has done as many as it does, and when what failed is tried again.
  - the time at which the stats of a delivery are next to be read, which is at once for a delivery that has just been sent, section 14. In a minute at the earliest, `metricsPauseMs`, while a pass over the stats is under way or after one that stopped at its limit with reads left: what a pass gets to, it leaves with a later time or with none, so that ends.
  - a year from now at the latest, `farthestLookMs`, whatever the list says. The runtime refuses an alarm after the year 2189, and a post can be scheduled for a later time, in the app until its field refused one and through `post.schedule` still: the alarm was then not set at all. It comes back, finds nothing to do, and is set anew.

  A frozen workspace, section 6, has no scheduled post and no access in that list: the store says that none is due.
- **Two places set it, both from that function.** The alarm itself, when it has looked, and `#setNextAlarm` after every change, a hello of a client included, which is what gives an alarm to a workspace from before it had something to do by itself. Phase 9 was built beside this phase and had two more: one that set the alarm for a push message or a delivery, and one for the inbox, which remembered the time that it had last set. Both are gone, and what they were for is in the list. The pass over the push messages, which can outlast the alarm that began it, sets nothing itself either: it notes that it stopped at its limit, and asks `#setNextAlarm`. A change brings the alarm forward and never puts it off: scheduling a post for next week leaves the look at a delivery under way where it is. While the alarm runs, a change sets nothing: the alarm sets the next one when it is done, from what it finds then, and a change that the alarm made itself would have it run again at once. The alarm never comes back at once for what it has just tried and left undone, a delivery that could not begin or a due post that was not taken up: that would be a loop for as long as it fails. It comes back in a minute.
- **The runtime is asked what the alarm is set to. The object keeps no record of it.** The first version of this phase kept the time in the `workspace` row and went by that, with this reason: asking the runtime takes a moment in which another call can set the alarm, and the later answer would win. But a record of the object's own can be wrong, and then nothing corrects it: after an alarm that failed, for an object from before the column, and after anything that sets or clears the alarm past the object. Where it said "none" while the runtime had the look at a delivery under way, the next post that was scheduled for later moved that look back to its own time. The runtime cannot be wrong about its own alarm, so it is the one that is asked, and the moment that the answer takes is dealt with where it matters: an alarm that is needed at once is set at once, without asking, in the same turn as the change, so it is stored with it. For a later moment the object asks, and works the moment out anew when the answer is there, with nothing awaited between that and setting the alarm. And the alarm at its end does not ask: nothing else has set one while it ran.
- **The alarm fails only when it could not set the next one.** What goes wrong in a look is logged and tried again the next time. Without a next alarm nothing would look after the workspace until something else woke its object, so then the alarm fails, with an error that holds nothing of the workspace, and the runtime runs it again, some times, with a growing pause. That is the one way of handling it: nothing swallows that failure. After a change the same failure is only logged: the change is committed, and the next change or alarm sets it.
- **Due posts are also taken up around every mutation**, so the order of things does not depend on how punctual the alarm is: before the first mutation of a message and after each, between the mutations of one message exactly as between two messages. A device that was without a connection packs what it sends by how much there is, and that must not decide whether a post is published. A due post that could not be taken up, which would be a bug, is logged once and left to the alarm.

Left for later: drag to reorder (the queue has move up, move down and to the top), slots per channel as Buffer has them (the queue is the workspace's), a week view of the calendar, and notifications for a held post.

## 10. Media

- **Upload**: the browser uploads in chunks through the Worker into an R2 multipart upload. Uploads are resumable, which matters on phones.
- **Images**: up to 4 per part, alt text per image. When a network's size limit is smaller than the original, the Cloudflare Images binding produces a smaller variant at publish time.
- **Video**: one per part. Workers cannot transcode, so the client reads the file's format, size and duration and blocks a video that a selected network would reject, naming the network and the reason.
- **Limits** for every network live in one module in `packages/core`, `src/network/limits.ts`, filled from each network's docs when the phase starts. Mastodon's come from the instance. The limits for text are there since phase 3, with the counting. Those for media join them in phase 7.
- **API uploads**: `POST /api/v1/media`, or a public URL in `assets` as in Buffer. Fetched URLs are size-capped and type-checked.
- **Retention**: originals are deleted from R2 a set number of days after every target has been sent. A thumbnail is kept.

Phase 7 built the images of that list. What was decided:

**The rows.** An image is a row of the synced table `media`, and it is in a part of a post by a row of `part_media`, which has its description and its place: `SYNC.md`, "Images". A device makes both rows with mutators, without a connection if it has none: the row of the image says `uploading`, with what the device read in the file, and the draft has the image at once. Only the server says that the bytes are there and what they are: `media.ready` and `media.fail` are its own, as the mutators of a channel are, because the row stands for something that only the server has. Where the bytes are in the bucket is no column: `mediaKeys` of `apps/worker/src/media/keys.ts` makes the names of the IDs of the workspace and of the image, so nothing has to be stored to find or to delete them. `duration` waits for video.

**The upload.** `packages/core/src/media/upload.ts` has the protocol: a request that begins the upload or takes it up again and says which chunks the server has, one request for each chunk of 5 MiB, which is the smallest part that R2 takes, and one that completes it. The Worker talks to the bucket, and the object of the workspace keeps the rows and what is known of an upload, the name of the multipart upload and the parts that the bucket has: no byte of an image crosses into a Durable Object or out of one.
