A chunk is held in memory once, 5 MiB at most, because that is where its length is checked: a chunk is exactly the bytes of its place in a file whose length the row says, so no request is read beyond it, and the file that the chunks make has that length. Everything else is a stream.

- An image is 20 MiB and 64 million pixels at most, a workspace holds 2 GiB of images that the server still has, 40 can be on their way at a time, and a person makes 240 requests of an upload in a minute, counted in D1 as the previews of links are.
- **What "resumable" means.** The server says which chunks it has, so an upload that was cut off goes on with the ones that are missing. For that the device has to have the file still, and a web page cannot take a file from the disk a second time by itself. So the file is kept until its upload has ended: in the memory of the tab, and in IndexedDB, in a database of the person's next to the copies of their workspaces, which whatever deletes those deletes with them. So each such file is a change that has not been synced, in what signing out counts before it asks, what keeps the copies when a session ends by itself, and what the sign-in page says is kept, and a tab that loads the app syncs a workspace whose images wait, so that their uploads go on. A tab that is loaded again finds it there and goes on. A device that has lost it, because the browser cleared its storage, says that the image waits for the device that has the file, and the person attaches it again. Every tab of the person hears of the rows and finds the file, so the tab that uploads an image holds a Web Lock of the upload until the row says that it has ended, and another tab waits for the lock, says that the image is uploaded in another window, and takes the upload over when that window is closed. The file goes before the lock, so a tab that takes the lock afterwards finds no file of an upload that has ended. The files of uploads that ended while no tab ran them are deleted when a tab next looks after its workspace, if no tab holds their lock: of a row that the tab knows, and of one that it does not know and that is more than a day old, whose row is gone. An image that was attached without a connection waits like every change, and its upload asks nothing until the copy says that the server has confirmed the mutation that makes the row: a request before would be answered with a 404, which the browser writes into the console of the page. It asks again after a 404 all the same, for a tab that does not sync the workspace and cannot tell.
- A draft with an image that is not `ready` is not published: the editor says so next to every account, and the server refuses the delivery with the same words, because `validateContent` judges the images of a content.
- An upload that is not finished within a week is given up, and its row says so.
- **An upload is completed in an order that a repeat can take up.** The parts are put together into one file, which is read, its copy and its small picture are written, and only then is the workspace told how it ended. The file as the device sent it is deleted after that, and of an upload that failed everything: a completion whose call to the workspace failed leaves the file for the next one, which the device makes, and which reads it again. Two completions at once, from two tabs or a device that asked again before its answer came, both say how the upload ended as the workspace has it: the first to tell it decides, and what each deletes goes by that and not by what it found itself, which can differ when the small picture could not be made.

**What pigy takes for an image.** `packages/core/src/media/image.ts` reads a file as a stream and says what it is by its bytes: JPEG, PNG, WebP or GIF by how it begins, the dimensions from its header. What a device says of a file, its name and its type, counts for nothing: the server reads the bytes when the upload is complete, and writes what it found into the row. No SVG, which is a program. The same walk makes the copy that pigy keeps: the picture, without what a camera wrote next to it.

- **Metadata is stripped, and nothing is encoded again.** The copy keeps what a decoder draws with, from a list for each type, and nothing that a list does not name: Exif, where a camera writes the place and the time, XMP, IPTC, comments and the text chunks of a PNG are left out, and so is what no format names, where anything can hide, and whatever follows the end of the image, so a file that is an image with something else behind it is only the image afterwards. What is kept for its fields keeps its fields alone: JFIF without its thumbnail, which is the picture before it was cut, Adobe's colour transform without what follows it, the control of a GIF frame and the count of its repeats without blocks behind them, and a chunk of a PNG only where it is as long as its fields, with an end that has nothing in it. And it is kept only where decoders read it, and the first of its kind: the segments of a JPEG before its first scan, a chunk of a PNG before its data, the alpha of a WebP directly before its picture. A WebP with two alphas, or with one before a lossless picture, which has its alpha in itself, is refused, because decoders do not agree on what it is. The ICC profile of a JPEG and a WebP is kept as it is, because it is what the colours mean. The picture is the same bytes: no quality is lost, and no decoder runs on the way in. The way up of a JPEG is in its Exif too, so a copy whose picture is turned gets an Exif segment of pigy's own that says that and nothing else, and the first Exif segment is the one that counts, as for decoders. The top of the file has the lists. A WebP whose way up is only in its Exif loses it, which no camera writes, and a WebP frame that holds anything besides its picture is refused, because it cannot be left out without saying the length of the frame anew before it.
- **A file is decoded once, to be believed.** A header can lie about what follows it. After the copy is made, a small picture is made of it with the Images binding, for the lists of the app, and a file that cannot be drawn is refused there, after two tries: what draws pictures does not say whether it failed for the file or for a reason of its own. A file whose header claims more pixels than pigy takes is refused by its header, before anything decodes it, and so is one with a frame that does not lie within the picture, or a picture of another size than its header says: what a decoder would draw is then not what pigy read.
- **A file is read in bounded work.** A length that a file declares is checked against the 20 MiB before anything is read or made room for, a file has 50,000 parts at most, segments, chunks or extensions, and the copy is written in pieces of 64 KiB, whatever its parts are.
- **An animated GIF is taken.** It goes out as it is where the network shows one that moves, Mastodon and LinkedIn, and as a still picture where it does not, Bluesky and Threads, which the editor says in advance.
- **The description is a reminder, not a refusal.** An image without one goes out, and the editor says under the images that people who use a screen reader hear nothing of it. Its length is checked against each network, like a text.

**Serving.** The bucket has no address of its own. The bytes of an image leave pigy through the Worker and in two ways. To the owner of the workspace, with their session, at `/api/workspaces/<id>/media/<id>/original` and `/thumbnail`: with the type that the server found in the bytes, `nosniff`, as an attachment, with a policy that lets nothing run, and never from a cache. And to a network that fetches an image itself, at a signed link: section 16. The app keeps the pictures that it has fetched in the memory of the page, and the service worker keeps none: section 13.

**Per-network variants.** `limits.ts` has what each network takes of images, `mediaLimitsSchema`: how many in a post, how many bytes and pixels in one, which types, how long a description, how long a shape. Mastodon's are read from the instance, the others are the same for every account. Where an image has more bytes or pixels than the account takes, or is of a type that its network does not take, `variantFor` says what to make of it, and the step `media` of the publishing workflow makes it: section 8. So none of the three keeps a post from going out. What does: more images than the network takes in a post, a description that is too long, a shape that the network refuses.

- A variant is made once for an image and a limit, and kept in the bucket next to the original under a name that is made of the limit: `variantName`. A second delivery to a network with the same limits, and a step that is run again, find it there.
- It is made by the Images binding of Cloudflare, behind a small interface, `ImageTransformer` of `apps/worker/src/media/transform.ts`: the image in the shape that it has, never larger than it is, tried at a lower quality and then at smaller sizes until it is within the bytes, as a JPEG unless it was a drawing and the network takes a PNG. The local Workers runtime has the binding, with another library behind it, so the tests of the Worker and the end-to-end tests make real variants. That the binding of the platform does the same with the same calls is to be confirmed: section 19.
- What goes out is never larger than a network takes, so an adapter holds one image in memory at a time, and the Worker's 128 MB are not at stake.

**Where images and a link meet.** A post of Bluesky has one embed, and so has one of LinkedIn: its images or the card of its link. The images win, on both, because somebody put them there, and the link stays a link in the text. The editor says so under the images of a part that has a link. Mastodon and Threads make their cards themselves, and decide themselves.

**The text of one account** has images of its own. The parts that are written for one account are parts, so they have their own rows of `part_media`. "Customize" begins the text of an account with the text of the post and with its images, and from then on the two are changed apart: an account that takes no image, or fewer, gets a text without them.

**Undo.** Taking an image out of a part, removing a part and deleting a draft remove rows of `part_media` and no image. The Undo of each puts the images back, with the mutator that put them there. The server deletes an image that has been in no part for a day, which is far longer than any Undo is offered, also on a device that was without a connection.

**Retention.** The server lets go of the bytes of an image 30 days after the last post that it is in was sent to every account: the original, what was made of it for the networks, and the file as the device sent it, which a completion deletes and leaves behind only where the bucket failed to delete it. The small picture stays, for the Sent screen, and the row says when the rest went, `originalGoneAt`. A post that did not arrive everywhere keeps its images, because it can be tried again. So a delivery finds its image gone only if somebody removed it by hand, and then it fails with words that say so. The alarm of the workspace does all of it, as one more moment of its one list, section 9: uploads that were not finished, images that are in no part, and bytes whose time is up, a bounded number in each look. It does so before it begins a pass over the stats, section 14, so the images never wait for a pass that the same alarm begins, and what comes due while the alarm waits for a pass beside it waits with it, as the renewal of an access does: it is due hours or days after what it waits for.

- **An image is claimed before its bytes go.** The alarm lists what is due and then goes through it with the bucket, and a device can change an image meanwhile: put one whose bytes were about to go into a draft, finish an upload. So each image is claimed when its turn comes, by the mutator that says what happens to it, in a transaction that looks again at whether it is still due, `claimMedia` of the store, and the bucket is told to delete only what a claim says is gone.
- **The parts of an upload that was given up are dropped.** The bucket bills them while it has them, and nothing but the row names them. So the row keeps the upload until the bucket has dropped it, or says that it has no such upload, and the bucket is asked again an hour after each failure, for eight days. The upload began before it was given up, so by then a lifecycle rule of the bucket that aborts an upload eight days after it began has dropped it, section 19: the row lets go of the upload, and the log says once that it was not dropped. Without that end, a bucket that never says that an upload is gone in the way that pigy reads would be asked every hour for ever. An image in no part whose upload was given up is deleted after that.

- The row is changed first and the bucket after: each of the mutators looks at the image as it is when it runs, and leaves one alone that is something else by then. If the bucket fails after that, bytes are left that no row names. They are served to nobody, and the log says which. Nothing looks for them yet: section 19.

**Publishing.** Section 6 has how each network takes an image, and section 8 the step of the workflow. Phase 7 is built in steps, as phase 3 was: section 17 says which networks publish images so far. A post with an image is not sent to an account of a network that does not yet: `imagesNotPublishedTo` of `limits.ts`, which the editor says next to the account.

**The sandbox.** A simulated network takes images as its network does and keeps the bytes in its world, the latest 48 MiB of them. The feed of a sandbox has the images of its posts, and `/api/sandbox/images/<id>` serves the bytes to the owner of the sandbox as an image, so the Simulator screen shows what a network was sent, which is how a person sees that a smaller image was made.

**Not built in phase 7.** The public API takes no media yet: it was not on `main` when the phase was built. A draft with images that was written in the app is checked with its images when the API shares it, by what a delivery would publish, `contentFor` of `apps/worker/src/workspace/content.ts`, as the editor checks it. Sharing image files into pigy, section 13, is not built either. The card of a link still has no picture: the picture of a page would be one more file that pigy fetches from a host that a person named, and it waits for the API's `assets`, which need the same guards.

## 11. Sign-in and access

- Sign-in with Google is the OpenID Connect authorization code flow with PKCE, state and nonce, done with `oauth4webapi`, which also validates the ID token. State, nonce and the PKCE verifier live in a cookie for the ten minutes a sign-in may take, not in the database. Section 1 says why this is not Better Auth.
- The Google account ID is the identity of a user. The email address is what the allowlist is matched against, trimmed and lowercased, and only if Google has verified it. Only ASCII addresses are accepted, from an admin, from `ADMIN_EMAILS` and from Google, and that is checked before the address is lowercased: lowercasing turns some other characters into ASCII letters, which would let one address pass for another.
- Sessions are our own, on D1 through Drizzle, after the guides at https://lucia-auth.com and https://auth.pilcrowonpaper.com/sessions. The cookie holds a random token, the database a SHA-256 of its secret half, and a session ends when its row is deleted. Sessions last 30 days and renew on use, at most once a day, so the installed PWA stays signed in. The cookie is `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax` and, over HTTPS, `Secure` with the `__Host-` prefix.
- Sign-in is refused unless the email is in `allowed_email` or in the `ADMIN_EMAILS` variable. The check runs for every new session and again on every request, so a change of role applies to the next request. Removing an email also deletes that user's sessions, revokes their API keys and freezes their workspaces, in one batch with the removal (sections 6 and 12). The workspaces and the channels in them stay as they are, and the timer of section 6 does nothing in them until the person has signed in again: no access is renewed and no scheduled post goes out. So does a sign-in that is refused: when Google reports an address for a known account that is not invited or not verified, the sessions that account has on any device end, because they would go on working with the address it had before. The stored address is left as it was, since nothing is stored about an address that is not invited. The API keys of such an account are revoked with its sessions, in one batch, unless Google has verified for it the very address that it is stored with, which is then only no longer invited: section 12 has why. Its workspaces are frozen where its keys are revoked. A sign-in also ends the sessions of any other account stored with the address that Google has verified for the account signing in, revokes their API keys and freezes their workspaces, whether it succeeds or is refused because the address is not invited: Google has just said that the address belongs to the account signing in, so the account that had it before has lost it. A sign-in that succeeds thaws the workspaces of the account that signs in, if the address is still invited when the thaw runs (section 6).
- The first admin comes from `ADMIN_EMAILS`. The addresses in it are always admins and cannot be changed from the settings page, where admins manage the rest of the allowlist. Nobody can change their own entry. An entry that is not an email address is ignored and logged.
- A person who is not invited sees a plain "pigy is invite-only" page. Nothing is stored about them.
- A request that changes something and is authenticated by the cookie must carry the app's own `Origin`. So must the handshake of the sync WebSocket, which is a GET: a browser sends the cookie with a handshake that any site starts.
- The session is checked when a sync socket is opened, not while it is open. A socket therefore lasts five minutes, after which the client has to open a new one. That bounds how long a session that has ended, by sign-out or by removal from the allowlist, can go on using a socket. Telling every workspace about every session that ends would close them sooner, but sessions end in many places, and one that was missed would leave a socket open for good. Where a person loses their access, their workspaces are told, because they are frozen (section 6), and close their sockets then, and open none while they are frozen: the object reads D1 before it opens a socket, after the session was checked, so a handshake that passed the check before the access ended does not get a socket after it either. The five minutes are what is left for a sign-out. A workspace that is frozen rejects the changes of a client, `frozen`, and ends the deliveries that wait their turn: what was asked for before the access ended does not go out, unless it is under way.
- The address Google redirects back to is built from the address the app is reached at, so it works unchanged on `localhost`, on `workers.dev` and on a custom domain. Each has to be registered with the Google client.
- Without Google credentials nobody can sign in: the production build has no other way in. Section 7 covers the simulated Google of development and tests.
- The device remembers who was signed in on it and which workspaces they have, in `localStorage`, and which of the two it showed last: section 7. That lets the app show the local copy before the server has answered, and without a connection. It lets nobody in: the session cookie does that, and the server checks it. The app asks the server when the person goes from one screen to another, in the background and not more often than every two seconds, and the sync socket is refused within five minutes of a session ending. A link that is only hovered, which the router loads ahead, is no reason to ask. When the answer is that the session is over, the app leaves for the sign-in page and the local copy goes: section 16. So somebody whose session has ended can see the data that was already on the device until the device has reached the server once, and never anything newer.

## 12. Public API

REST under `/api/v1`, described by an OpenAPI document generated from the same Zod schemas the app uses, with a docs page.

| Endpoint | Buffer equivalent |
| --- | --- |
| `GET /account` | `account` |
| `GET /channels`, `GET /channels/{id}` | `channels`, `channel` |
| `GET /posts`, `GET /posts/{id}` | `posts`, `post` |
| `GET /posts/{id}/targets/{channelId}` | None. One entry of `targets`, with its text in full |
| `POST /posts` | `createPost` |
| `PATCH /posts/{id}` | `editPost` |
| `DELETE /posts/{id}` | `deletePost` |
| `POST /posts/{id}/move` | `movePostInQueue` |
| `GET /posting-schedule`, `PUT /posting-schedule` | `Channel.postingSchedule` |
| `POST /media` | None. Buffer accepts URLs only |

- **Copied from Buffer**: camelCase fields (`text`, `assets`, `dueAt`, `saveToDraft`, `metadata`), `mode` values (`addToQueue`, `shareNext`, `shareNow`, `customScheduled`), status values, the `assets` shape with `altText`, per-service `metadata` including `thread`, cursor pagination with `pageInfo`, and error names (`InvalidInputError`, `NotFoundError`, `UnauthorizedError`, `LimitReachedError`, `UnexpectedError`).
- **Different from Buffer**: `channelIds` is a list, because a pigy post is cross-network, and the response carries `targets[]` with per-channel status, link, error and metrics. `schedulingType` is dropped, since pigy always publishes automatically.
- **Errors**: every error response is `{ "error": name, "message": text }`. The name follows from the status: `UnauthorizedError` for 401 and 403, `NotFoundError` for 404, `LimitReachedError` for 429, `InvalidInputError` for every other 4xx, `UnexpectedError` for 5xx. An `InvalidInputError` that is about one field of the request also has `"field"`, the name of that field, with dots between the parts of a nested one, so that a form can show the message next to the input. The app's own routes outside `/api/v1` answer the same way.
- **Keys**: created in settings, shown once, stored hashed. Prefix `pigy_live_` or `pigy_test_`. Scopes: `posts:read`, `posts:write`, `channels:read`, `media:write`. Sent as `Authorization: Bearer`.
- **Safety**: `Idempotency-Key` header on writes, and per-key rate limits using the Workers rate limiting binding.

Phase 6 built this. `GET /api/v1/docs` describes it to whoever uses it, and `GET /api/v1/openapi.json` is its document. What was decided, and why:

- **One description of the shapes.** `packages/core/src/api/v1.ts` has the Zod schemas of every request and answer, and `apiOperations`, the list of the routes: method, path, scope, schemas, status. The Worker registers its routes from that list and parses with those schemas, and `openapi.ts` makes the document from the same two with `z.toJSONSchema` of Zod 4, which writes the schemas that have a name under `components.schemas` and refers to them. No library for OpenAPI: the list is some hundred lines, and a second description of a shape, as the decorators of such a library are, is what was to be avoided. The compiler asks for a handler for every entry of the list, a test compares the routes that Hono has with the paths of the document, and another holds the document to the schema of OpenAPI 3.1, with a validator that has the schema with it and asks nobody. The three are an entry point of their own, `@pigy/core/api`, which the web app does not import: it has no use for them.
- **The docs page is the Worker's own.** `/api/v1/docs` is plain HTML that the Worker makes from the document: the routes, their parameters and the shapes, with the introduction that the document has as its description. No script and nothing from another site, which the content security policy would have to allow and the page would wait for. The page has the policy that the Worker gives a page, with its style sheet allowed by its hash (section 16). Swagger UI and its kind load from a CDN or weigh a megabyte in the build.
- **A key is `<prefix><id>_<secret>`.** The prefix is `pigy_live_` or `pigy_test_`, for the sandbox, by the workspace that the key belongs to, which is one and never changes. The ID, 16 hexadecimal characters, names the row in `api_key`, so a key is found without its secret being compared with anything in the database. The secret is 256 bits, of which a SHA-256 is stored and compared in constant time, as for a session. The key is in one answer, the one that made it, and the list shows how it begins. A workspace has 20 keys at most, which are counted and stored in one statement: two requests that arrive together with room for one key do not both find the room. The same statement asks whether the session that the request came with is still one of the owner's, and stores nothing if it is not: a request waits between the check of its session and its insert, for its body if for nothing else, and a sign-in that ended the sessions of the account and revoked its keys meanwhile would otherwise be followed by a new key for the identity that was just revoked. Such a request is answered with a 401. A key ends in the year 9999 at the latest, as every time of the API.
- **The keys are managed with the session, never with a key.** `GET`, `POST` and `DELETE` under `/api/workspaces/{id}/api-keys` take the cookie and the `Origin` check of every other route of the app. A key that leaked cannot make another, list the others or revoke the one that would replace it.
- **The API takes a key and never the session.** Nothing under `/api/v1` reads the cookie, so no page can call it as the person who looks at it. The cookie is for pigy's own pages, whose requests are told from those of other sites by their `Origin`. A script has no origin to check, so what it sends has to be a secret that no browser attaches by itself. No answer has a CORS header and a preflight finds no route, so a page of another site cannot send the header that carries a key. A server or a command line can, which is what a key is for.
- **Every request with a key looks the owner up.** The key is read with the owner of its workspace and the owner's row in the allowlist, in one statement, and `ADMIN_EMAILS` is asked as for a session. So a key stops with the access of its owner whichever way that ends, also where no route hears of it: an address taken out of `ADMIN_EMAILS`, and whatever is added later. It costs nothing: the row of the key has to be read anyway.
- **Removing an invitation also revokes the keys.** `DELETE /api/admin/allowed-emails/{email}` revokes the API keys of the accounts that are stored with the address, in the batch that removes the row and ends the sessions. The lookup stops them anyway. Revoked, they do not work again when the address is invited again, which they first did: the lookup goes by the address that is stored for the owner, and an address that is invited a second time may be somebody else's by then. Until that person has signed in, nothing has told pigy so, and the keys of the account that had the address before would post to that account's channels. The person who is invited again makes new keys. An admin who is taken out of `ADMIN_EMAILS` and put back keeps theirs: no route hears of either, and the operator who edits that variable knows whose the address is.
- **The keys of an account that has lost its address are revoked.** The lookup above goes by the address that is stored for the owner, which is the one of their last sign-in that succeeded, and section 11 leaves that address stored when a sign-in is refused. So when Google says at a sign-in that the address of a known account is no longer verified, that the account has another address which is not invited, or that another Google account now has the address, the keys would go on publishing to live accounts for somebody of whom nothing says any more that the address is theirs, while their sessions end. They are revoked where the sessions are ended, in the same batch of statements, `revokeAccessAtRefusedSignIn` and `revokeAccessOfOtherAccountsWith` of `auth/session.ts`, and do not come back: the person makes new ones once they have signed in again. That another Google account has the address is acted on at every sign-in for which Google has verified it, also one that is refused because the address is not invited: it was first acted on only where the sign-in succeeded, and the keys of the account that had the address worked again when the address was invited for its new owner, until that person first signed in. The alternative was a mark on the user that says whether the stored address still holds, which both ways of authenticating would check: a third thing to keep true, where a revoked key is a state that the keys have already. One case is left to the lookup: an account that Google reports with the address that it is stored with, verified, and that is only not let in. Nothing of its identity is lost, and a refused sign-in of its own revokes nothing.
- **`media:write` is declared and given to no key.** A key with a scope that does nothing would have to be explained twice: now, and when images come and old keys turn out to have it. `POST /media` answers 501 and a post with `assets` is refused with a 400 that names the field, both in the documented shape, and the document says so.
- **When a key was last used is written once a minute at most**, in the request, with a statement that asks again whether a minute has passed: of the requests that arrive together one writes.
- **A write is one change of the workspace, made with the mutators.** `POST /posts` is a `post.create`, a `part.add` for each further part, a `target.add` for each channel, a `target.customize` for each channel with a text of its own, and the mutator of its mode. The Worker hands the parsed request to the object of the workspace in one call, `callWorkspaceApi`, and `apps/worker/src/workspace/api.ts` runs the mutators there, inside the transaction of `once`, in a transaction of its own: what any of them refuses, or what the checks below refuse, undoes all of it, and the clients are sent the whole or nothing, in one patch. The alternative was a composite mutator, a `post.createWhole`, that clients would have to know too: a change of the sync schema for something no client does. This way the API can make nothing that the app could not, because it has no other way to write, and the rule that only mutators write synced tables holds without an exception. Phase 6 changed neither the mutators nor the protocol.
- **What goes out is checked as the editor checks it.** A post that is shared, scheduled or queued has to have a channel, none that needs connecting again, no empty part in a text that an account gets, and has to pass `validateContent` with the limits of every account for the text that the account gets: the rules of `compose` in the web app, run in the object in the same transaction. A refusal is a 400 that names the field that the text came from, `text`, `metadata.thread.1.text`, `metadata.mastodon.text`. A draft, `saveToDraft`, is not checked and may hold what a draft of the app may: no channel, an empty text, a text that is too long for its accounts. The workflow checks again when the post goes out, as for a post of the app, because limits change.
- **`metadata` is per service, as Buffer has it, and a target says what its channel gets.** `metadata.thread` has the further parts of the post's own text. `metadata.mastodon`, and the same for the other three, gives every chosen channel of that service a text of its own, with a `text` and a `thread`: "Customize" of the editor. The app can give two accounts of one service different texts, which a request cannot say. An answer can: an entry of `targets` has the `text` and the `thread` of a channel that has a text of its own, and `customized` says that it has one. For every other channel both are null: it gets the text of the post, which the post says once. They are also null where the answer had no room for the text, which `textOmitted` says: see the next point. Saying it again for each channel made the answer of a thread for fifty channels fifty times its request.
- **An answer carries a million characters of texts at most**, `maxApiAnswerTextLength`, whatever is in the workspace. The app can give each of fifty channels a long text of its own, and so can a script, with one request for each service. A page of `GET /posts` ends before the post that would take it past the budget, and has one post at least, so it can have fewer posts than `first` asked for: `hasNextPage` says whether more follow. One post says the texts of its channels for as long as there is room. Of the rest it says that it left the text out, `textOmitted`, in every answer that has the post: a read, a repeat, a page. It first said null for them and nothing else, as for a channel without a text of its own apart from `customized`, so an answer that lacked two texts read like a whole one, and nothing handed the two out. `GET /posts/{id}/targets/{channelId}` does: one entry of `targets`, with its text whatever the length. That always fits, as the post's own text does: a text is a hundred parts of ten thousand characters at most, for a post and for an account, which is the budget. A page has whole posts: none is cut in two by the budget, and what a post leaves out on a page it leaves out by itself.
- **`PATCH` edits a scheduled post without losing its time.** Only a draft is edited, section 9, so the object takes a scheduled post back, edits it, checks it and schedules it again, in the one transaction: at the time that it had, or behind the post that it stood behind in the queue. A script does not have to know that rule, and nobody sees the draft in between. A request that says the `mode` that the post has, `addToQueue` for a post of the queue or `customScheduled` with the time that the post has, asks for nothing new and is treated as one that leaves the mode out: a script that sends the whole post with every change does not send it to the end of the queue. `shareNext` is another place, and is asked for. A post that has gone out is refused with a 409. The time that is kept is the one of the row, which can be later than the API says it: see "Times" below. The time that a request is compared with is the one that an answer says: a script that sends back the last moment of the year 9999 for a post that is stored for a later year has named the time of the post, and the post stays where it is. It was first compared with the row, and such a request moved the post to the year 9999.
- **`DELETE` deletes at once**, with `post.delete`, also a scheduled post: the two steps of the Queue screen are for an Undo, which a script does not have. The reason for the two steps, section 9, does not reach it either: the row is gone in the workspace itself, not on a device that is behind, so nothing is left that could go out, and an Undo of the app that arrives later finds no post and does nothing. `post.delete` takes what the inbox said of the post with it. A post that somebody put aside in the app is not there for the API: every route answers a 404 for it and `GET /posts` leaves it out, so a script neither changes, which would bring it back, nor deletes what a person may still take back. It is there again when they do.
- **What the app has to tell, it tells of what the API did.** The API writes with the mutators, which is where the inbox is told (section 13), so a post that a script shared and that failed is a notification and a push message like any other, and no route of the API makes one. A key has nothing to do with the devices: `push_subscription` is the sessions', and no request with a key reads or writes it. A scheduled post that is held, section 9, is `scheduled` for the API, with `heldAt`: a status of its own would be one that the app does not have. `PATCH` leaves it held unless it is given another time, shared or taken back. A change that leaves it held, at the time that it had, tells nobody a second time. The mutators that take the post back and schedule it again remove the notification and make a new one, unread and with a push message, because that is what they do for a person who decides about a held post. In the transaction of the request the object then takes back the push message that was queued for it, and runs `notification.read` or `notification.dismiss` on the new notification if the person had read or dismissed the one before: the inbox is as they left it, and a script that corrects typos in held posts does not buzz a phone each time. The notification is a new row all the same, with a new time, because only a mutator writes a synced row and none restores one. A held post that is given another time which is missed as well is told of anew. A target that ended because its account was disconnected says so, `disconnected`, as the synced row does: it is in `error` with no `service`, which a script could otherwise not tell from a delivery that failed and that a person may try again.
- **`PUT /posting-schedule` pauses the queue while it replaces the slots.** Every `slot.add` and `slot.remove` gives each post of the queue its time anew, the one costly thing that a mutator does (section 9), and a request can replace 200 slots under 500 queued posts. A paused queue has no times to compute, so the object pauses it for the changes, in the transaction, and lets it go on at the end, where the posts are given their times once. They get the times that they would have got: the slots after now, in their order. So `maxScheduled` stays at 500. A script can fill that, and is told so with a 400.
- **`Idempotency-Key`.** The key of the call is the API key's ID with a digest of the header, so two keys that send the same header name two calls. The object keeps a small record under it for the 30 days of every keyed call, a few hundred bytes whatever the post: the status of the answer, a digest of the method, the path and the body, and, for a call that was carried out, which post or that the posting schedule is what the answer was about. Of a refusal it keeps the refusal, which is a status and a few words. It does not keep the body of a success. That was the first design, and it did not hold: the answer of a valid draft for fifty channels was past what a row of the object's database takes, so the request failed with the header and worked without it, and a key could fill the storage of its workspace in minutes, for 30 days. And a body that is kept is parsed by the Worker of a later day with the shape of that day, so a deploy that adds a field to the answer would make every kept answer a 500.
- **A repeat is answered with the status of the first answer and with the post as it is now**, read when the repeat comes, and with the header `Idempotent-Replayed: true`. A script that lost the answer of a `POST /posts` gets the ID of the post that it made, which is what it lost, and makes no second post. It does not get the post as it was: one that was `sending` in the first answer may be `sent` in the repeat, and a post that was deleted since is a 404, which still makes no post: the document lists the 404 for `POST /posts` for that reason, the one route that names no post and can answer that there is none. The alternative that keeps the answer as it was has the size and the shape against it, above. A repeat of a refusal is the refusal as it was kept, with the same header, whatever would become of the request now. The same header with another request is refused with a 422, the status that the IETF draft of the header has for it, and changes nothing. A call that failed, which is one that threw in the workspace, has left nothing and is carried out when it is repeated. A 5xx does not say that this is what happened: the workspace can have made the change and its answer be lost on the way back, or the Worker can fail after the call. So the document tells a script that a request which failed with a 5xx may or may not have been carried out, and to repeat it with the same `Idempotency-Key`, which is carried out if it was not and answered as a repeat if it was. It first said that such a request had not happened. A call that threw includes one more case: that includes a bug of a mutator, which the store answers a client with as `internal_error` and which the API throws on, so that a 500 is never what is kept for 30 days. A refusal that the Worker makes before it asks the workspace, of a body that does not parse, is not kept: it follows from the request alone and comes out the same again. Without the header a request is carried out every time.
- **Rate limits.** One limiter, `API_RATE_LIMITER` in `wrangler.jsonc`, 120 requests a minute for each key, counted by the ID of the key once the key was found good. Beyond it a 429 with `Retry-After: 60`, the length of the period: the binding does not say when a count ends. The platform counts per location and catches up with a delay, so the limit is a brake and not a quota. A request without a good key is not counted, and costs one read of D1: section 19.
- **Reading.** The object answers a read in one call, as plain data. `GET /posts` is paged in the object: by `createdAt` and `id`, newest first, with a cursor that says where the next page begins. That order does not change when a post is edited, so a script that pages through sees no post twice. A page is a walk down an index from the cursor on, which stops at the end of the page: over `(created_at, id)` for all posts, and over `(status, created_at, id)` for the posts of one status, which phase 6 puts in the place of the index on the status alone. The posts of two statuses are not in one order in an index, so each status is asked for by itself and the newest of the answers are the page. The cursor is compared as one row value, `(created_at, id) < (?, ?)`: written with `or`, SQLite reads every post before the cursor to get to it. A test reads the plans. A read changes nothing, so the object does nothing after it of what it does after a change: a script that asks every few seconds costs the read.
- **Errors.** The mutators refuse with codes: `not_found` is a 404, a post that is not in a state for what was asked, `not_a_draft`, `not_scheduled`, `sending`, is a 409, everything else a 400. A body is at most 256 KiB and is read no further than that.
- **Times.** An answer says a time as ISO 8601 with a year of four digits, so the API takes none after the year 9999, for `dueAt` and for the end of a key, and the field of the app for the time of a post takes none either, which says so. The mutator `post.schedule` still takes every time that a date can hold: narrowing it would be a change of the sync schema, and a row with such a time may exist. So an answer says a later time as the last moment of the year 9999, `apiTime`, and never fails for a time that is in the data: one post that a slip scheduled for the year 20266 would otherwise make every page that has it a 500. A `PATCH` that keeps the time of such a post keeps the time of the row.
- **Stats (phase 10).** `metrics` of a target is what people did with the post on its network, section 14: `status`, the five counts, each null where the network has no such count, `countedAt`, and `reason`. It is null where nothing has been read, which is before the first read, a moment after the delivery was sent, and for a delivery that is not `sent`. Where pigy has no stats, and will have none or none before something changes, it is not null but `unavailable`, with the reason: where the network has none for pigy, as LinkedIn, where the account has to be connected again or was disconnected, where pigy gave the read up, and where it has no record of the post. A null that also meant "not yet" would leave a script to wait for what never comes, which is the rule of `textOmitted` too. The one null that stays is that of a delivery that was sent before phase 10, which nothing reads: pigy had not been deployed then, and the API says so. The object reads them from the synced row, in the same read as the post. They add a fixed number of fields to a target, and a reason of a sentence, as `error` does: nothing that grows with the texts of the workspace, so `maxApiAnswerTextLength` does not count them.
- **Not in phase 6**: images, `assets` and `POST /media`, phase 7. `metrics`, which was null, came with phase 10. Trying a failed delivery again, which the app does with `target.retry` and Buffer has no route for. Webhooks, and a key that reaches both workspaces: section 21.

## 13. PWA, notifications and share target

- **Install**: manifest, icons and a service worker that caches the app shell. With local data, the app opens and is usable offline. The rest of this section, up to "Push", says how.
- **Manifest and icons**: `apps/web/public/manifest.webmanifest` has what Chrome asks for before it offers to install: a name, where the app starts, a window of its own (`standalone`) and icons of 192 and 512 pixels, plus one that Android may cut to its own shape. iOS takes its icon from a link in `index.html`. All of them are made from `assets/icon.svg` by `pnpm icons`, with the letter in the font of the app, and committed. A manifest has one theme colour and one background colour, so it has those of the light theme, which is what the splash screen of Android is drawn with. The colour that a browser or a phone draws around the page follows the theme that is on screen, through the `theme-color` tag.
- **Offering to install**: where the browser says that pigy can be installed (Chrome, Edge and others on Chromium), Settings and the command palette offer it, and the browser's own banner is held back. An offer is good for once. Safari has no such offer: on an iPhone or iPad, Settings say where the browser has "Add to Home Screen". Installed, pigy shows neither. Phase 9 added the two moments at which pigy brings installing up by itself, below, under "Install prompts": nothing else in the app does.
- **The service worker** is `apps/web/src/sw`, built into `/sw.js`. It keeps the app shell and every file of its build in a cache named after the build, and answers from it: a file of the build with that file, and any other navigation with the app shell, which shows the screen. Everything else goes to the network as if there were no service worker. That includes the paths of the Worker, above all: a navigation to one of them, such as the return from a sign-in, must not get the app shell. The service worker knows them from `assets.run_worker_first` in `wrangler.jsonc`, which the build writes into `sw.js`, so a build has one list. A device goes by the list of the service worker that it runs, and that is the one of the release before until the app is next started there: a navigation to a prefix that a deploy has just added gets the app shell on such a device. So a new route that a browser navigates to goes under a prefix that exists, `/api/`, `/connect/`, `/sim/` or `/sync/`. A new prefix takes a changeover: the service worker that knows it is released, and taken over by the devices, before anything sends a browser there. The list has a fifth pattern since phase 11a, `/assets/*`, which is no such path: the Worker stands before the files there only to say that one is missing, "Serving" below. So a file of its build is the service worker's to answer for wherever it is, before the list is asked, and nothing sends a browser to a path under `/assets/`. A device that still runs the service worker of the release before does not know the pattern and needs nothing of it: it answers for the files that it keeps, and leaves a file that it does not keep to the network, where the Worker now answers.
- **What is on a device**: the files of the app in that cache, which are the same for everybody. The service worker keeps no answer of the API and no synced data: the local copy in IndexedDB (section 4) is the only place where the data of a workspace is on a device, and `pigy-session` in `localStorage` the only one that says whose. Signing out deletes both, as before. It leaves the cache, the service worker and the chosen theme, none of which says anything about anybody: a device that somebody has signed out of still starts the app without a connection, on a page that says that signing in takes one.
- **A build is kept whole or not at all.** The service worker is installed only if it has got every file of its build. Cloudflare answers a path that it has no file for with the app shell and the status 200, so the service worker looks at what it gets: a page in place of a file is refused, and so is an app shell that loads a file of another build. Both happen when the next version is deployed while this one is being installed. The app shell is not compared byte for byte, because Cloudflare can be set up to add to the pages that pass through it. A file that is named after its content is copied from the cache of the build before if that has it, so an update downloads what has changed. An install that fails deletes what it has written, so that releases which could not be installed do not pile up on a device. Unless the cache was there before the install began: a build is named after the files of the app, so a release that changes only the service worker has the cache of the worker that is running.
- **A version** is a digest of the files of a build and of the rules that Cloudflare serves them by, `_headers` with the content security policy among them. The service worker keeps the app shell with its headers, and a page that it answers for goes by them: a release that changed only the policy and made no new version would leave every device with the policy that it has, because a browser installs a new service worker only when `sw.js` has changed.
- **A new version** is found by the browser after every page load, and when the app asks: when a window comes back to the front, and when the sync server has turned the window away as too old. It is installed next to the running one and waits. It does not take over by itself, because the windows that are open run the old build and somebody may be typing in one. It takes over when the last window has been closed, which is the browser's rule, when the app is started while it waits, or when the person says so. The page that starts says so before it has put anything on screen, and starts again as the new version: that costs about a second in Chromium, once per version. It does not say so while a window holds changes that it could not store: see "Several windows". The person says so with "Reload to update", which Settings and the command palette offer while a version waits, and the sync indicator when the window is too old: that button first asks the server for the new version, because a reload alone would bring back the old one from the device. None of the three offers it in a window that holds changes which it could not store, in either of the workspaces that it syncs (section 7). While another window holds such changes, a reload would start as the old version again, with the same offer: the button then leaves the window as it is, and a notice says that the other window is in the way. The offer is not hidden for that, because nothing tells a window when the other one has stored its changes.
- **Several windows** share one service worker, and a worker that takes over deletes the files of the build before it. So one rule stands behind the others: no window goes on under a worker of another build, where the next screen whose code it has not loaded would fail. When a new worker takes over, every open window loads itself again, where it was. Nothing is lost by that: what is typed is stored with every keystroke, and a window waits with the reload until it holds no change that is not stored, in either workspace, one that is still being written included (section 4). A window whose device fails to store a change would have to wait under the new worker, so it is not let come to that: it holds a Web Lock for as long as it holds such changes, in either workspace, and a page that starts while the lock is held does not ask the version that waits to take over. It starts as the version it is, whose files are there, and the new version waits on: for the last window to be closed, or for a start after the changes are stored. The page has no screen before the browser has said who holds the lock, so the browser is given three seconds to say so, the time that the take-over itself is given: a page that is not told starts as the version it is as well. Chromium puts the question off for a page that it renders ahead of time. A browser that fails to answer is taken for one without Web Locks, where no window can hold the lock, and the page goes ahead. What remains possible is a write that fails in the instant after a page that starts has looked. A page that no service worker answered for, because it was loaded before the first one was installed or with a reload that goes past the worker, is taken on by the next worker that is activated, which can be of another build: the page asks whether that worker keeps the script it was started from, and loads itself again if not. Two rare cases are not found out, and stay that way: a worker that takes the page on before the code of the page has begun to listen, and a newer build that is being installed at that moment, whose cache answers the question because it is put to all caches. Such a page goes on under a worker of another build until it is loaded again. Nothing fails while the server can be reached, and without a connection a screen whose code was not loaded yet may not open. The cure, if it ever matters, is to ask the worker that answers for the page, and not the caches. The other windows are loaded again without having been asked. That is seldom where the person is: a new version takes over because a window starts or a button is pressed, and that is where they are. It can be all the same, with the installed app and a browser tab of pigy side by side, or with a link that opens pigy in a new window next to one that is in use. The window comes back where it was, with what was typed into a draft, which is stored with every keystroke. What was on screen only is lost: a form that is half filled in, such as the one that connects an account, or a dialog that is open. Section 19 lists it. The message that asks a worker to take over, the prefix of the names of the caches and the name of the lock are contracts between the versions of the app: a page of one version says them to the worker and the windows of another, so they do not change.
- **Starting without a connection**: the app shell and its files come from the service worker, who is signed in from `localStorage`, the workspace from IndexedDB, and the server is asked in the background (section 11). A device that does not know who is signed in cannot start the app: it says that it cannot reach the server, and goes on by itself when the connection is back. So does the sign-in page, which then offers no way to Google that would end on an error page of the browser. That is for a request that got no answer, and for one that is not answered within ten seconds, both questions of a check together: on a network that leads nowhere a request is taken and never answered, and the two pages show nothing before they know who is signed in. A device that holds a session which is over asks on its way to the sign-in page, because somebody may be signed in again, and the sign-in page does not put the same question once more when that one got no answer: it is one wait and not two. In the background, on a device that knows who is signed in, a question that is given up after that time is one that got no answer too: nothing changes, and the next navigation asks again, where a question that stayed open would have been the last one of its window. A server that answers with an error is not a missing connection, and nothing that the device could wait for cures it: both pages say that something went wrong and offer to try again, and the sign-in page keeps its way to Google. Unless the device still holds a session that the server has not ended: the server could not be asked about it, so nothing has looked for changes of that session that were never synced, and whoever signed in as somebody else would have them deleted without the page having said that they are there, as it does when a session has ended (section 16). The page then offers only to try again. A copy that has never been filled, and cannot reach the server, does not say "No drafts yet". It says that the drafts come when the connection is back only where they do: a window that another one has taken the socket from, or that is too old for the server, does not try again, and says that it has stopped syncing.
- **Keeping what is stored.** A browser may delete what a site has stored when the device runs low on space, unless it has promised not to: the local copies with the changes that were never synced, and the files of the app. pigy asks for the promise with `navigator.storage.persist()`, with the first change that the person makes after the app is opened, and never before the first screen or on the sign-in page. Chromium decides by itself, by how much the app is used and whether it is installed, so the answer can change, and the question costs nothing: it is asked again after every page load until the promise is given. Firefox asks the person, which is why the moment is one at which they are at work in the app and the device is about to hold something of theirs, and not the start. Settings say under "App" whether the browser has promised, in plain words, and offer to ask again where it has not. A browser that decides by itself refuses without showing anything, so Settings say after such an answer that the browser was asked and decided not to, and what makes it decide otherwise: an app that is installed or used a lot. There is nothing for the person to press. Where a browser has no such promise to give, they say that it does not say.
- **A version that changes the synced schema** empties the local copy when it first starts, and fills it again from the server (SYNC.md section 6). If that start is without a connection, the drafts are not on the device until it is back: the screen says so, and what was written and not synced is kept. A new version usually takes over at a start, and a start can be offline, so this will happen to somebody. The cure is not in the service worker: a client that kept the rows which the new schema still reads would not have to ask for them again. To decide with the first change of the schema that people have to live through.
- **Serving**: `apps/web/public/_headers` lets browsers keep what is under `/assets/` for a year, because Vite names those files after their content, and has them ask the server again every time for `sw.js` and the manifest. It also has what a browser may do with a page of the app, the content security policy above all: section 16.
- **A path under `/assets/` that has no file is a 404**, since phase 11a. Cloudflare answers every path that it has no file for with the app shell and the status 200, which is what makes the address of a screen work, and `_headers` gave such an answer under `/assets/` a year in the cache of a browser: a browser that asked for a script in the moment in which a deploy replaced it would have kept a page under the name of the script. Neither `not_found_handling` nor `_headers` can tell a file from the fallback: the first has one answer for every path, and a rule of the second goes by the path and not by the status. So `/assets/*` is in `assets.run_worker_first`, and the Worker asks the binding `ASSETS` for the path: `apps/worker/src/assets.ts`. A file is passed on as Cloudflare answers it, with the headers of `_headers`. An answer that is HTML is the app shell, because no build has a page under `/assets/`, and becomes a 404 with `no-store`. What it costs: a request for such a file is a request to the Worker, which is billed where a static asset is not. Each device asks for each file once per release, because the browser keeps it for a year and the service worker keeps it on the device, so that is some dozens of requests per device and release. Everything else is still a static asset that does not reach the Worker.
- **In development** there is no service worker: `pnpm dev` serves modules one at a time and replaces them under hot reload, so there is no list of files to keep, and a cache would be in the way. The production build and the build of the end-to-end tests have it.
- **Not verified on a phone.** Everything above is tested in Chromium, on a desktop and with the screen of a phone. How iOS and Android show the installed app, the insets around the status bar and the bar for gestures, the sign-in with Google inside the installed app and a new version taking over in WebKit need the deployed address and the devices, in phase 11 (section 19).
- **The inbox, push messages, the share target and the install prompts** are phase 9. The rest of this section is about them.

### The inbox

Built in phase 9: the synced table `notification`, the mutators that fill it (`SYNC.md` section 4), and the Inbox screen. What was decided, and why:

- **Four things are told of**: a post that has ended and has not arrived everywhere, because a delivery of it failed or nobody knows how one ended. A scheduled post that pigy holds because it found its time more than an hour late (section 9), since phase 5: that is told by the mutator that holds the post, with a kind of its own, and goes when somebody sends the post, gives it another time, takes it back to the drafts or deletes it, the first step of deleting included. A post that was held before it was put aside and comes back with the Undo is held still and is not told of a second time: whoever undoes has it before them. A channel whose network no longer takes its access. And an access that ends by its date, a week before: LinkedIn's 60 days, and an access that pigy renews, as that of Threads, when pigy could not renew it for so long that its end is a week away.
- **One notification per post, when the post has ended.** A post that went to three accounts and failed at two is one notification, made when the last of its deliveries has ended, and not three. The post is what the person wrote and what they deal with, on one screen, and three deliveries that fail in the same second would be three notices of one thing. The price is that a delivery which fails at once is told of only when the slowest one of the post has ended, which a network that asks to be tried again can put off by half an hour (section 8). A channel that needs connecting again is told of at once, in a notification of its own: that is another thing to do, on another screen, and it is what such a wait usually ends with.
- **A notification is under the ID of what it is about.** So there is at most one about a post and one about a channel, by the key of the table and not by a rule that code has to keep. What is said of a channel later takes the place of what was said before. It also lets the mutators make it without an ID from outside: the mutator that ends a delivery, which runs it, is a call of the workflow that is carried out once (section 8), and nothing had to be added to what it is called with.
- **It goes when the thing is dealt with.** The inbox is what needs attention, not a log. A post that is tried again loses its notification while it is on its way, and gets a new, unread one if it ends badly again. One that arrives loses it for good. A channel loses its notification when the account is connected again or disconnected. A post that fails because the person disconnected its account gets none: they did that themselves, a moment before. The Sent screen is the record.
- **Whether a post is told of is decided in one place, when the post leaves `sending`**: `announce` in `packages/core/src/sync/mutators.ts`, which `settle` calls, whichever mutator ended the last delivery. There is a notification if a delivery ended badly by itself since the post last began to be sent. Not for a delivery that a disconnect ended, which its target says with `disconnected`, a synced column that phase 9 added for this. The migration that adds it marks the targets that a disconnect had ended before, which it finds by the text of their error, for that once: a post that is being sent at the deploy, to an account next to one that was disconnected, ends after it. So a post whose other account the person disconnected while it was sent gets none when the rest arrives, and one whose delivery failed by itself is told of also when a disconnect ends the last one. And not for a delivery that had failed before the post last began to be sent: somebody who tries one of two failed deliveries again, and sees it arrive, is not told that the post was not sent, though it is in `error` again for the other. A post that is `sending` is changed by nothing, so its `updatedAt` is when it began to be sent, and `queuedAt` of a target when its delivery was asked for: no column had to be added for that.
- **Read and dismissed are the person's, on every device.** Both are mutators, so they work without a connection and sync like everything else. Opening a notification reads it. "Mark all read" names the ones that it marks: one that arrives meanwhile on the server stays unread. It marks all of them, in as many mutations as that takes, a hundred to each. Dismissing removes the row, and leaves the post or the channel as it is. Both name a notification with the `createdAt` that the device saw, and do nothing to one that has another by then: what a notification tells of can happen again, which puts a new, unread one under the same ID, and a device that was offline meanwhile would otherwise dismiss news that nobody has seen.
- **Kept for 30 days.** A notification that nobody dismisses and whose thing nobody deals with is removed after that, by the alarm of the workspace. The post is still on the Sent screen, and the channel still says what it needs.
- **A reminder is given once for each end that an access has.** The channel keeps, where no client sees it, which end the person was told of: the `expiresAt` that the reminder was for. An account that is connected again is told of the end of its new access when that comes. An access that pigy renews by its timer, as that of Threads (section 6), has an end that moves with every renewal, weeks before it comes: nothing is said of it while the renewals work, as the Channels screen says nothing. Its end is a week away only when pigy could not renew it for more than seven weeks, and then the inbox says that, in the words of the Channels screen, and again if it happens another time after a renewal that worked. A channel that needs connecting again is not reminded: the inbox has said that. A reminder that is dismissed does not come back. An access whose end has passed unsaid, because nothing woke the workspace in that week, is not told of afterwards: the Channels screen says that it has ended. The alarm of the `WorkspaceDO` is set for the time, as section 9 has it for everything that a workspace does by the clock.
- **Where it is on screen.** In the sidebar of a desktop, as the first item of the navigation, with the number of unread ones. On a phone the bar at the bottom has room for six screens and has six in the sandbox, so the inbox is not among them: it is a bell in the header of every screen, with the same number. A count is what an inbox is looked at for, and the header is seen on every screen. The bell could have opened a panel over the screen. It leads to a screen instead: one more thing that has an address, that the command palette goes to, and that a notification can open.
- **Each leads to where the thing is dealt with**: the post on the Sent screen, brought into view, the Queue screen for a post that is held, which marks it and has what can be done, or the dialog of the Channels screen that connects the account again.

### Push

Built in phase 9. `apps/worker/src/push` is the Worker's side: `web-push.ts` sends, `keys.ts` has the key, `subscriptions.ts` the devices, `routes.ts` what Settings call, `notify.ts` the message to every device of a person. `apps/web/src/sw/push.ts` is what the service worker does with a message. What was decided, and why:

- **Web Push is written on the Web Crypto API, without a library**, as the client of atproto OAuth is (section 6), and for the same reason: `fetch` and the clock are parameters, and the libraries for Web Push are written for Node. It is three standards: RFC 8030 for the request, RFC 8292 (VAPID) for who sends, and RFC 8291 with the encoding `aes128gcm` of RFC 8188 for the encryption. The example of RFC 8291 is a test: with the keys and the salt of the RFC, the sender makes the bytes of the RFC.
- **A push message is sent for what the inbox is told, and for nothing else.** For every notification that a mutator makes or makes anew, the `WorkspaceDO` queues a message, in the transaction of the change: `push_outbox`. Its alarm, which is set for at once, reads the devices of the owner from D1 and hands the message to their push services. So a notification never is without its message having been queued, and none is queued for a change that did not happen. A message is taken off the queue when every device was tried, whatever each push service said, and also when a subscription that is gone could not be deleted afterwards: a message that stayed for that would go to the devices that have it a second time. It stays while the devices cannot be read from D1, for an hour at most: told later, it would be news of something that the person may have dealt with.
- **Sending never keeps a delivery waiting.** A push service can be slow, and one that does not answer is waited for ten seconds. The runtime runs one alarm of an object at a time, so an alarm that waited for every message would keep a post that is published meanwhile from going out. The sending is a pass of its own, which the alarm begins as the last thing that it does, after the deliveries and the accesses, and waits for only until a change asks for a delivery to begin: then the alarm ends, runs again at once for the delivery, and the pass goes on beside it. Since phase 10 the alarm also stops waiting by itself at the time of the next scheduled post, which time that passes does not tell it of, and comes back for it: `#waitAside`, with a timer that it calls off. The renewal of an access waits for the sending, section 14. A pass sends 20 messages at most and takes up no new one after half a minute. What is left goes with the next alarm, which is asked for at once and looks after the deliveries first. All of that is in the one list of what the alarm is for, in section 9: since phase 5 nothing sets the alarm but the end of the alarm and `#setNextAlarm`. One message goes to four devices at a time.
- **Nothing is tried again for one device.** A push service that does not answer, or answers with an error, costs that device that message, which is logged. The inbox has it. Trying again would take a queue per device with times of its own, for a message whose whole point is to be timely.
- **A subscription that is gone is deleted**: when its push service answers 404 or 410, which is what a browser that was uninstalled, or whose person turned the site off, leaves behind. A browser can go on handing such a subscription out. So "Turn on" asks the server first whether it sends to the subscription that the browser has, and has the browser end that one and make another where it does not: saved again as it was, it would be on and never get a message.
- **A subscription that the browser replaces by itself** is told to the service worker alone, with `pushsubscriptionchange`, where a browser tells anybody. The service worker has no say in who gets notifications, so it tells the windows of the app that are open, and the app looks when it starts: the device remembers in which session notifications were turned on and with which subscription, and where the browser has another by now, or none, the app hands the server the new one, for that session and no other. Section 19 has what is lost in between.
- **What a message holds: IDs, and no words.** It is JSON with the kind of the event, the workspace and whether that is the sandbox, and the ID of the post, or of the channel with its network: `pushMessageSchema` in `packages/core`. No text of a post and no name or handle of an account. The message is encrypted for the browser and its push service cannot read it, but it waits there, and what is made of it shows on a lock screen. The service worker makes the words: "A post was not sent", "pigy can no longer post to your LinkedIn account". The network is named and the account is not.
- **The service worker always shows something.** A browser counts a push message that shows nothing against the site, and takes the subscription away after a few. The service worker of a device can be older than the Worker, so a message of a kind that it does not know, or that it cannot read at all, is shown in general words and leads to the inbox.
- **Pressing a notification leads to the thing, in its workspace.** The notification holds an address of the app: the post on the Sent screen, or the dialog that connects the account again. A window of the app that is open is told to go there and is brought to the front. It goes there itself and is not loaded again, because it may hold what it has not stored (section 4). Otherwise a window is opened. A window counts only if it shows the app: one at a path of the Worker, such as the consent page of a simulated network, has the address of the app and nothing in it that listens. And the window says that it has heard, on a port that comes with the message: where nothing comes back in time, as from a page that is still starting or a window of a version of the app that does not know the message, a window is opened, so that pressing a notification leads somewhere. In time is 400 milliseconds from the press, listing the windows included: a browser lets a service worker bring a window to the front and open one only for a while after the press, Firefox for about a second, and refuses what comes later. A window that is hidden is brought to the front before it is told, because the browser may have put it aside, as the app of a phone that is not on screen, and it hears nothing until then. A window that the person sees is told first and brought to the front when it has answered. A window that the browser refuses to open is left unopened: the window that was brought to the front is what the press led to then, and it goes where it was told when it hears. What the worker opens and where a window lets itself be led is decided by one rule, `appPath` of `packages/core/src/return-to.ts`: a path of the app, and nothing that a browser reads as another site. The address names the workspace, `inWorkspace`, and the tab then shows that one: a post of the sandbox is not in the live workspace. That is the second place where something other than the switch changes what a tab shows, after the return from a network (section 6), and like that one it changes the tab alone: the device goes on remembering what the person chose. The name is taken out of the address at once. A workspace that is not the person's counts as none named.
- **The sandbox sends too, and says that it is the sandbox.** A push message is not something of a simulated network: it is pigy reaching its person, and the failures of the sandbox are the one way to see what a notification looks like without breaking something real. So an event of the sandbox goes to the real devices, through the real push services, and its title begins with "Sandbox:". The other way, nothing from the sandbox, would leave notifications untried until the first real failure.
- **A device belongs to a session.** A subscription is kept with the session in which notifications were turned on, and goes when that session ends, however it ends: by signing out, by an invitation that is withdrawn, or by running out after 30 days without use (section 11). A device that nobody is signed in on is told nothing. So is a device whose session has run out, which the reminder of an access that ends can meet: it is in the inbox for whoever signs in again. A browser has one subscription, which it hands out again to whoever turns notifications on in it next. The same person, signed in anew, takes the row over. Somebody else does only when the session that the row is of is over: while it is good, the request is refused with a 409, or anybody who learned the address of a subscription could take the device from its person. "Turn on" does not get there, because it makes a new subscription where the server does not send to the one at hand.
- **What one person can have the Worker send is bounded.** A session has one subscription: turning notifications on replaces what the session had. A person has ten at most, and the eleventh takes the place of the oldest. So a notification is at most ten requests, whoever is signed in.
- **A renewal never undoes what the person chose.** The app hands the server the subscription that the browser has replaced, when it starts and when the service worker says so, without asking anybody: `renewPush`. It waits for the server meanwhile, and "Turn off" does not wait for it. So what the device remembers of the choice, which every tab reads, decides: the renewal looks at it after every wait, and stops when notifications were turned off, taking back a subscription that it made and what it saved, or when they were turned on anew, which it leaves as it is. One renewal runs at a time in a tab.
- **Settings ask the server how things stand.** "Notifications on this device" says on only when the browser is subscribed, with the key that this pigy has, and the server sends to that subscription for this session. So it needs a connection to say, and says so without one. The states, in plain words: on, off, blocked in the settings of the browser, not shown by this browser, on an iPhone only once pigy is on the Home Screen, not set up by whoever runs this pigy.
- **The browser is asked for its permission at the click on "Turn on", and never before**: not when the app starts, and not when Settings are shown. It is the first thing that the click does, because a browser lets a page ask only while the click still counts.
- **The key is one, and replacing it turns every device off.** `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY` is a private key of P-256, as `openssl` makes it, and its public half is what a browser subscribes with. A push service takes messages for a subscription from that key alone, and a subscription cannot be moved to another. So there is no list of versions as for the keys that seal: after a new key, the devices that subscribed with the old one are dropped, Settings say off, and each is turned on again. Without the key no device can be turned on and nothing is sent. The inbox works all the same.
- **Who sends is the address of this pigy.** A push service is told who to turn to about a sender, `sub` of the token, and wants an HTTPS address or a `mailto:`. It is the address at which the device subscribed, which is kept with the subscription: the object that sends has no request to read it from. A pigy that is reached without HTTPS is one on somebody's machine and says `mailto:pigy@localhost`, which the push service of Chrome takes. Those of Firefox and Safari may not (section 19).
