- **The address of a push service is the word of a browser.** It is checked like every host that somebody else names (section 16), when a subscription is taken and again before a message goes out: HTTPS, a public name, no port, no credentials. And its host has to be one of a push service that pigy knows, `pushServiceHosts` in `apps/worker/src/push/subscriptions.ts`: those of Chrome and what is built on it, Firefox, Safari and Edge. Anything else is an address that somebody made up to have the Worker send requests there, and is refused with a 400 about `endpoint`. The price is that a browser with a push service of its own has to be added to the list before its people can turn notifications on. pigy's own host never passes, whatever the list says. The keys and the endpoint of a subscription are in no log: what is logged of a push service that fails is its host.
- **In development and in the tests.** `pnpm dev` has no service worker and so no notifications: Settings say so. A build for development and tests has a key from the repository, under the rules of the other development keys (section 16). The build that the end-to-end tests run against reaches no real host, so its push messages go to the simulated push service, inside the Worker, which that build alone has: `apps/worker/src/simulated-push.ts`, kept in memory like the codes of the simulated Google. A test has it make up a device, under `/api/e2e/push`, and reads what the device received, decrypted. The production build is checked for all of it. The browser of those tests has no push service and refuses notifications to every site, so no test shows a notification: section 19 lists what a deployed pigy and a phone have to show.

### Share target

Built in phase 9, for text and links. Files are not shared to pigy yet: they come with media, phases 7 and 8.

- **The manifest registers pigy** with `share_target`: a GET to `/share`, with `title`, `text` and `url` in the address. Android offers an installed app with a share target in its share sheet. iOS does not, for any web app, and neither do the desktop systems as far as pigy is concerned: Settings say that it is Android's.
- **`/share` is an address of the app, not of the Worker.** The service worker answers it with the app shell like any screen, so sharing works without a connection, and no prefix had to be added to the paths of the Worker, which would have taken a changeover (above).
- **What is shared is shown, and becomes a draft when the person says so.** The address of a share is one that any website can send a browser to, with a text of its choosing. A draft that the visit alone made would be written into the workspace of whoever is signed in, and synced to all their devices, by whoever made the link. So the screen shows what came, as text in which nothing can be pressed, with "Save as draft", which has the keyboard, so that Enter saves, and "Discard". Nothing is stored or synced before. Saving makes the draft with `post.create`, a change like any other, and takes the place of the share in the history, so that going back offers nothing a second time. A share of nothing leads to the drafts.
- **What was shared is read from the address as the browser has it**, not from what the router makes of it: the router reads every value of a query as JSON where it can, so a shared "null" or "[1,2]" was nothing, a long number another number, and a text in quotation marks lost them. The address has to arrive as it was written for that, so the way back from signing in is the address of the browser and not the router's writing of it, and what a tab keeps of a share through a sign-in is the query as it came.
- **In the workspace that the device showed last, unless the person changes it.** The window that the system opens for a share has no choice of its own, and takes what the device remembers (section 7). The screen says which workspace the draft is saved in, and the switch is there: what was shared is of no workspace, so it stays when the person changes to the other one, which is the one exception to the rule that a change of workspace drops what the address says (`keepsAddress` in `lib/workspace.ts`).
- **The text and the link are put together once.** Every app fills the three fields in its own way. A browser shares a page as its title and its address, many apps put the address at the end of the text and leave `url` empty. The draft is the text, or the title where no text came or the text is only an address, and then the address, unless the text has it already.
- **Signed out, the person signs in first, and what was shared waits in the tab.** The route is one that needs a session, so the app keeps where they were going, and what was shared is shown after the sign-in, to be saved or discarded like any other. It does not travel with the address to come back to, as it first did: the Worker keeps no such address of more than 1,000 characters through a sign-in (section 11), which a share of 1,200 letters is, or one of 120 Chinese characters, each of which is nine characters of an address, and the person arrived at their drafts without their text. The tab keeps the query of the share in `sessionStorage`, under `pigy-share`, and the way back is `/share` alone: `keepShared` of `lib/share.ts`. The share screen shows what the address holds, or else what was kept, and what was kept goes when it is saved or discarded, when another share comes, and when the person goes to another screen without deciding. Until then a reload and a change of workspace show it again, as they do for a share in the address. It is nobody's data: it came with an address that the tab was opened at before anybody was signed in, so it is shown to whoever signs in in that tab next, as it was when the address carried it, and it is not dropped for who that is. It is still text that another site may have supplied, so nothing is a draft before "Save as draft". A tab that can keep nothing sends the share with the address, as before.

### Install prompts

Section 13 so far had Settings and the command palette offer to install where the browser says that it can, and nothing else. Phase 9 adds two moments at which pigy says so itself, and no more:

- **Where somebody turns notifications on in a tab of an iPhone or iPad.** They cannot work there: iOS has push messages only for an app on the Home Screen. The row of Settings says that, and how to add pigy to the Home Screen, in the place of the button.
- **After the first post that is published from a phone, in a browser.** That is when somebody has found a use for pigy, and when the installed app serves them better: it opens without a connection, and on an iPhone it is the way to notifications. A notice offers to install, where the browser lets a page ask, or leads to where Settings explain it. It is made once on a device and never again, whatever the person does with it, which the device remembers. On a desktop nothing brings installing up.

Nothing else nags: no banner at the start, nothing on the sign-in page, nothing that comes back.

## 14. Engagement stats

- Fetched per target for Mastodon, Bluesky and Threads: likes, reposts, replies, plus quotes and views where the network reports them.
- Refresh slows down as a post ages, for example at 1 hour, 6 hours, 1 day, 3 days, 7 days and 30 days.
- LinkedIn stats show as unavailable.

Phase 10 built this. `SYNC.md` section 4, "Stats", is the contract with the clients. What was decided, and why:

- **An optional method of the contract, `metrics(credentials, id)`.** It returns the counts of the one post that `id` names, likes, reposts, replies, quotes and views, each null where the network has no such count, or null for a post that the network no longer has. What an adapter reads is checked with its own schema, as every answer is, and what it returns with `postMetricsSchema` of `src/provider.ts`, which the Worker checks it with again. Failures are `ProviderError`s as everywhere: `retry` for a limit or a network out of reach, `reconnect` for an access that is refused, `fail` for an access that reaches the post and not its stats. It never fails with `refresh` or `uncertain`: it renews nothing and publishes nothing. LinkedIn's adapter has no method, as for `readPost`: the type says what a caller can count on, and a caller knows without asking that there are none. Section 6 has what each network does.
- **The stats of a thread are those of its first part, as Buffer has them.** The first part is what people see in a feed, and what they like and share: the parts after it are replies, seen by whoever opens the thread. Adding the parts up would count a person who likes every part once for each, and count the thread's own parts among its replies. And it is one request per delivery, whatever the length of the thread, which is what bounds a pass, below. The counts are what the network shows under that post, and nothing is taken off: Mastodon and Bluesky count a reply of the account itself, so the replies of the first part of a thread count its second, and Threads counts every reply in a thread under the post that begins it, and a reply's own replies under a reply, so there they count every part after the first. pigy cannot take that one off reliably: Mastodon counts it under a public post and not under one for followers, and a person can delete it. So the number is the one that the person sees on the network, and the API says so.
- **Only a delivery that is `sent` has stats.** A delivery that failed halfway has a first part on the network all the same, but it is offered "Retry", which takes it up again, and a target is `sent` for good once it is. So a row of stats has one thing to go with, its target, which goes with its post. A delivery that ended `error` or `uncertain` has none, and the Sent screen shows none.
- **Kept: the latest values, synced, with when they were read.** The synced table `metric` has a row per delivery, under the ID of its target: a status, the five counts, `countedAt` and a `reason`. No history: nothing shows one, and a row per read would grow every workspace by eight rows a post for nothing that anybody sees. A later phase that draws a curve keeps one in a table of its own.
- **Three statuses, so that nothing means two things.** `counted`, `unavailable` with a reason for the person, and `gone` for a post that was deleted on the network. No row at all is "not read yet". A row that is `unavailable` or `gone` keeps the counts of the read before, with their time, which are the last that are known: the person still sees how the post did before it was deleted.
- **When: at once, then at the moments of `metricMoments`.** The first read comes as soon as the delivery has ended, by the alarm that its end brings: it says at once whether the network gives stats for this channel, which for LinkedIn and for an old Threads channel is the one thing worth saying, and it is what an end-to-end test can see, which cannot move the clock of the server. Then ten minutes, an hour, six hours, a day, three days, a week and 30 days after `sentAt`, and never after the last: a month-old post has had what it gets. The time of the next read is a column of `post_target` that clients do not see, `metrics_at`, with an index of the status and that time, `post_target_metrics_at_idx`: every change asks for the next read among all the deliveries that were ever sent, most of which have none left, and by the time alone or the status alone SQLite would go through all of them. The end of a delivery sets it, and every read sets it to the next moment that is ahead, or to null. A read that comes late, because the object was not woken, takes the moment that is still ahead and leaves out the ones that passed: nothing catches up in a burst.
- **A read at ten minutes, for every network.** The first people see a post within minutes, and what they do in that time says whether it takes: that is when a person who has just published looks, on a real network as in a sandbox. So a read comes ten minutes after the delivery was sent, between the read at once and the one of an hour. It is also what a person who tries pigy in a sandbox needs: the read at once comes before anybody has seen the post, and with the hour as the next read, the counts of a sandbox stood at 0 for an hour, which looked as if nothing worked. The audience of a sandbox, below, begins within the first minutes, so that every post has a like by the read at ten minutes. Not a moment of the sandbox's own: the Worker reads both environments at the same moments, and nothing in it knows of the audience, which is the simulated world's. It is one read more for each delivery, which the passes bound as they bound the rest.
- **The alarm reads them, in a bounded pass beside itself.** The time of the next read is one more moment of `#nextLookIn`, section 9, and the alarm begins a pass, `#metricsPass` of `src/workspace/object.ts`, after it has looked after the deliveries, the accesses, the inbox and the images. A pass reads 20 at most, `metricsPerPass`, and takes up no new read after half a minute: a workspace that published 500 posts at once reads their stats over 25 minutes, a pass a minute, `metricsPauseMs`. It runs beside the alarm, as the pass over the push messages does, section 13: the alarm waits for it, and leaves it to itself as soon as a change asks for a delivery to begin or queues a push message, and when the time of a scheduled post comes, so that a slow network never keeps a post or a push message waiting. A pass can wait for its networks for a minute and a half: half a minute of taking up reads, and a read that began then and waits for Bluesky's app view and PDS. The renewal of an access whose time comes meanwhile waits for it, and for the push messages, which the alarm waits for first: the alarm that renews it comes a minute after the one that waited, `renewalPauseMs`, or sooner for something else, which renews it all the same. An access is good for weeks when its renewal comes due, and is renewed within a few minutes of its time. What an image comes due for meanwhile waits in the same way, section 10. The alarm stopped waiting at the time of a renewal at first, which held only for the first of its two waits: a time that had passed in the first was not one that the second stopped for, so the renewal waited for the stats all the same. Where it did stop, the alarm came back for the renewal a minute later all the same: a minute and a half at most to win, on an access that is good for weeks, for a second record of what the alarm has looked for, next to the one of the scheduled posts. An alarm that comes while a pass is under way begins no second one. Every read that a pass gets to leaves its delivery with a later time or with none, whatever comes of it, so the alarm never comes back at once for what has just failed. The one that stays due, because its account was connected again meanwhile, below, is read by the next pass, a minute later.
- **What a read does with each outcome.** Counts are stored, `counted`, and the next moment is set. A post that the network no longer has is `gone`, and nothing is read again. A network without stats for pigy is `unavailable`, with words that say so, and is never asked again. An access that reaches the post and not its stats, which is a `fail` of the adapter, is `unavailable` with the adapter's message, and is asked again at the next moment: a Threads account that is connected again has the permission from then on. At the last moment there is no next one, and the reason is pigy's, that the network did not give them when pigy last asked: the adapter's message can promise stats once the account is connected again, and a new connection brings forward only the reads that are ahead. Connecting an account again also brings the reads of its posts whose stats are `unavailable` forward to that moment, `connectChannel` of the store: the next moment can be weeks away, and the person who has just connected the account expects to see them. A read that the alarm is making with the access before when the account is connected again is due already, which the connection leaves as it is: it stores nothing of what it found, which is of the access before, and stays due for the next pass, `metricsPauseMs` later, which reads with the new one. So do the other reads of the channel on the list of its pass. Stored, the refusal of an old Threads access would have put the post off to its next moment, which can be weeks away, a moment after the person granted the permission. A disconnect meanwhile does the same, and the next pass says that the account was disconnected. A network that asks to be asked later, or that is out of reach, is asked an hour later or when it says, whichever is later, and so are the other reads of its channel that are due: the pass asks it nothing more. The read that it refused comes a moment after them, a millisecond: a pass reads what was due first first, and a network that fails for that post alone, as a server that cannot build the page of one status, would refuse it first every time, and put the others off with it, for a month. A read that is put off past its last moment by more than a day, `metricsGraceMs`, is given up: a network that keeps asking to be asked later would be asked once an hour for good. The other reads of its channel wait all the same, until the time that the network named: the pass does not come back for them at once. A network that answers and does not show the one post, a `retry` of the adapter with `notShown`, rests nothing: that post alone waits, for its next moment, and the other reads of its channel go on in the same pass. Bluesky's app view does so for a post that it has yet to see, and for good for one that its moderators took down, which would otherwise keep the reads of its channel waiting with it, the oldest of them, for a month. Such a post that is still not shown at the last moment is given up. A pigy without the keys that open the credentials, and a failure of pigy's own, try again a day later. Whatever is given up, or cannot be read at all, stores `unavailable` with a reason, keeping the counts of before: a delivery whose channel was disconnected, one whose post the workspace has no record of, and one that was given up. Nothing that is `sent` is left without a row for good, which the API counts on, section 12.
- **A refused access marks no channel.** Publishing marks a channel `needs_reconnect` when the network refuses its access, and the timer of section 6 does when a renewal is refused. A read of stats does not, and stores the refusal as `unavailable`, with the adapter's message. It reads beside the alarm, which renews the accesses of Threads and Bluesky, and a refusal of credentials that a renewal replaced a moment before would mark a channel that is fine, with a notification that the person acts on for nothing. What is wrong with an access is said by what needs it: the next post, or the renewal. A channel that is marked already is not asked: its stats are `unavailable` and say that the account has to be connected again, and are read at the next moment. At the last moment they say that it had to be then, and are read no more. LinkedIn says that it has none before that: connecting it again would change nothing.
- **Credentials and adapters as everywhere.** A read opens the credentials of its channel with `openCredentials`, in the call and for the call, and makes the adapter with `providerOf`, which gives a sandbox its simulated world through `reachOf`. Bluesky's counts are public, and the read of them sends no token at all. A channel that was disconnected takes its access with it: its deliveries keep their counts, and the next read of each says that the account was disconnected and is the last.
- **The sandbox has an audience.** A person who tries pigy should see stats move without a second account to like their posts. The simulated world of a sandbox has an audience, `audienceOf` of `@pigy/simulators/world`, which sees, likes, shares, answers and quotes a post as time goes by, from the post and the clock alone: from the first minutes, with the first like within one to seven minutes, half of it in the first hour, and less and less after a day, between 10 and 59 likes in the end. The first read, at once, finds nothing, the read at ten minutes finds the first likes, and the reads after it find more. Worked out from the clock, it needs no screen and no timer, and is the same whoever asks. A test sets counts of its own, `engage` of the world, in a world without an audience, where nothing happens by itself, and every simulator counts the replies of its world among the replies of a post, also those of the account itself, as the networks do.
- **On the Sent screen**, a delivery that is sent has a line under its account: an icon and a short number for each count that its network has, 1.2K for a large one, with the words in a label for whoever does not see the icons, and when they were counted. It fits a phone on one line. LinkedIn, and an old Threads channel, say "Stats unavailable" and why, and a post that was deleted on its network says so under its last counts. Nothing is shown before the first read. `selectSent` of `apps/web/src/lib/sent.ts` reads them from the copy, with `statsOf`, which says the words: Mastodon calls a repost a boost.
- **In the public API**, `metrics` of a target, section 12.

## 15. Testing and CI

| Layer | Tool | What it proves |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Unit | Vitest | Grapheme counting, link and mention detection, LinkedIn escaping, thread splitting, queue slot maths, validation rules |
| Adapter conformance | Vitest in Node | One spec for all networks, run once per network: connect, validate, publish text, images, video and threads, read back, delete, refresh tokens, map every error class, publish nothing twice. Runs against the simulator |
| Pipeline | Vitest in the Workers runtime with local D1, Durable Objects, R2 and Workflows | API call to alarm to workflow to simulator. Asserts final statuses, sync messages and the push notification. Injects faults, including the lost-response case, and asserts no duplicate post |
| Sync | Vitest in the Workers runtime | Two clients converge, the offline outbox replays once, snapshot fallback works, one user cannot reach another's workspace, credentials never appear in sync messages. The server is tested with a client written for the tests, and the client of the app is tested against the real workspace object |
| Sync client | Vitest | The rules of SYNC.md for a client, against a server that the tests play: pending mutations on top of patches, confirmation and rejection, the outbox and what it merges, starting over, every close code, several tabs |
| Service worker | Vitest | What it keeps of a build and what it refuses to keep, which requests it answers and which it leaves to the network, the paths of the Worker among them |
| End to end | Playwright against the local Worker, built as for production except that sign-in is simulated | Simulated Google sign-in, allowlist refusal, sandbox onboarding, connecting four simulated accounts, cross-post with override, thread and image, schedule and fast-forward, offline draft then sync, a start without a connection, a new version taking over, several tabs and devices, installability checks, phone viewports |

**No real network in CI.** In test runs, any outbound request to a host that is not simulated throws, so a test that reaches for a real service fails. The build that the end-to-end tests run against has no `fetch` that leads outside: sign-in and the sandboxes are answered inside the Worker, and what a live workspace asks of a network is answered by a guard, `apps/worker/src/outbound.ts`, with an error.

**The end-to-end tests test their own build.** Several checkouts on one machine run them at the same time, each on a port of its own, which the server of a run chooses as it starts, after the build, and says in its output, where the run reads it. Between choosing the port and listening on it lie some milliseconds, in which another process can take it: the server then fails to start, and the run with it, before it has tested anything. The server listens at 127.0.0.1 alone, and the browser and Playwright's own requests take `localhost` for that address alone, so a server at ::1 on the same port is never asked. A run can still find another server on its port: one that took it after the run's server had stopped, which Playwright does not notice. And another run in the same checkout builds over the build that it tests. So every e2e build has a random ID, which the build says in its output, and a run compares the ID that its build said with what answers on its port and with the build in the checkout: once its server has started, and before and after every test. A run that finds anything else fails and says what it found, also when that is a build of the same code, and also when the test that found it passed when it was tried again. The production build has no such ID.

**Keeping the simulators honest.** The adapter conformance spec can also run against the real services with dedicated test accounts. That run is manual or nightly, never part of pull request CI, and it deletes what it posts. If the real service and the simulator disagree, the simulator is fixed.

The run is started by `pnpm --filter @pigy/providers conformance:live <network>`, with the credentials of the test account in the environment. The script is the only way to start it, and refuses in CI. It posts with the least visibility the network has: on Mastodon, posts that only the account itself sees. In CI the same specification also runs against the simulator with the harness of the live run, which has one account and no way to look into the network, so that the live run does not break unnoticed between the times somebody starts it.

The conformance suite runs in Node, like the other tests of the packages, and not in the Workers runtime as an earlier draft of this plan had it. The adapters use nothing but web-standard APIs, the live run needs Node to reach the network, and the pipeline tests run the same adapters in the Workers runtime.

**CI (GitHub Actions)**: lint, typecheck, unit and Workers-runtime tests, Playwright with traces on failure, build. Playwright runs in three jobs, each a third of the tests with a build of its own (`--shard`), since the whole suite took longer than one job may. Every end-to-end test is also a test of the content security policy: the `test` of `e2e/fixtures.ts` fails one whose page did what the policy refuses.

**Deployment from CI**, since phase 11a: a push to `main` that passes every check is deployed by the same workflow, in a job of its own, and so is a run that is started by hand on `main`. What was decided:

- **Off until the owner switches it on**, with the repository variable `DEPLOY`. Without it a job says that nothing was deployed, and fails nothing: the workflow was merged before anybody had a Cloudflare account. `SETUP.md` has the steps.
- **The secrets of the deploy belong to an environment, `production`, that only `main` may use.** A pull request runs the workflow as the pull request has changed it, so a secret of the repository is one that a pull request can read.
- **It deploys the build that it has just made**, with `pnpm build`, and checks that this is what `wrangler deploy` is pointed at: never a build of the tests, which has the simulated sign-in.
- **The migrations of D1 come first, then the Worker.** The new code needs the new schema from its first request. In between, and for good if the deploy fails, the code of the release before runs on the new schema, so a migration only adds what that code can ignore, and what a release no longer needs is removed by a later one.
- **One deploy at a time, and never an older commit over a newer one.** The job waits for the one before it, and deploys nothing when `main` has moved on since its checks began: the run of the newer commit deploys. It asks GitHub's API where `main` is, and fails when it gets no answer, rather than deploy on a guess.
- **The secrets of the deploy go to the two steps that call Cloudflare**, the migrations and the deploy, and to no other. The install runs code of the dependencies, and the build and the checks have no use for them.
- **A change that must not be deployed while posts are being sent**, one that renames the steps of the publishing workflow (section 8), says `[no deploy]` in the message of its merge commit and is deployed by hand. CI cannot see whether a delivery is under way. The marker holds only until the next push to `main`, which deploys everything up to its own commit, the change that waited among it: such a change is deployed by hand before anything else is merged. Only the newest commit of a push is read for it.
- **The secrets of the Worker are not the workflow's.** The owner sets them, once, with `wrangler secret put`.

## 16. Security

- Channel tokens are encrypted at rest with a key held as a Worker secret, with a key version for rotation. The same goes for what else pigy keeps of secrets that are not its own: its registrations with Mastodon instances, and what a connection that has begun needs to finish. The details follow this list.
- OAuth uses state everywhere, and PKCE wherever the network has it for a client of pigy's kind: not on LinkedIn, which has it for native applications only, and where the secret of the application stands in its place. The state of a connection is kept on the server, is good once, and only in the session that began. Cookie-authenticated writes and the WebSocket handshake check the origin.
- A workspace is reached only through the Worker, which looks it up by its ID and the signed-in user together. The Durable Object never sees the request of the client: the Worker makes a new one. The object also remembers which workspace and owner it was first used for and refuses any other, so a mistake in the Worker's routing cannot hand one person's data to another.
- Mastodon instance domains, Bluesky handles and asset URLs come from users, and so do the servers that the DID document of a Bluesky account and the metadata of its servers name: whoever controls an account writes them. So they are validated, HTTPS only, and size-capped. A host that somebody else names is asked only if it is a public name, with no port: no IP address, and no name of a private network such as `localhost`, `.local`, `.internal`, `.lan`, `.home` or `.corp`. `packages/providers/src/host.ts` has the one check that every adapter uses, and that the Worker uses for the address of a push service, which a browser names, and which has to be at the host of a push service that pigy knows besides (section 13). An adapter follows no redirects, with one exception, gives up on a host that does not answer, and reads a bounded amount of every answer. It takes for a network out of reach only what a `fetch` fails with when no answer comes, a `TypeError` or a request whose time is up. Anything else that the `fetch` it was given throws, such as the refusal of a simulated world that belongs to another workspace, is passed on and logged as a failure of pigy's, not reported as a server to try again later. What a network says in an error may reach a message, a log or the screen only without the secrets of the request.
- The exception is the file in which the host of a Bluesky handle says the DID of the handle, `/.well-known/atproto-did`. The specification "Handle" of atproto lets a host send that request on, "up to a reasonable number of redirect hops", so a host that does is no mistake of the person's. The request carries nothing secret, so a redirect cannot carry a token anywhere. It is followed five times at most, each time only to an address that pigy may ask, as above, and the whole way has the time of one request: a host that sends pigy on gets no more time for it. `sendFollowing` in `packages/providers/src/http.ts` is the only code that follows one. A request with credentials never is: a redirect could take them to another host.
- The page that a link of a post leads to is read for the card of the link, which is a request to a host that a person named and an answer that anybody wrote. It goes only to an HTTPS address at a public host, follows a redirect three times at most and only to such an address, has five seconds for the whole way, carries no cookie and no credentials, and reads the first 256 kilobytes of an answer that says that it is HTML in UTF-8. Nothing of the page is run. Its title and its description are taken as text, without control characters and cut to a length, and are shown as text: section 6, "Links, mentions and hashtags". The routes that the editor asks through are for whoever is signed in, about a workspace of their own, and counted per person. The picture of a page is not taken, and the browser of a person asks the page of a link for nothing.
- A host of the Cloudflare zone that pigy is deployed on is public by its name and is still a case of its own: by default a `fetch` of a Worker to such a host goes straight to its origin, past the firewall of the zone and past Cloudflare Access. With pigy at `pigy.example.com`, somebody who is invited could have had pigy read them the title and the description of a page at `wiki.example.com` that only Access protects, as the card of a link, in the editor or in a post. No name tells such a host from another, so `publicUrl` cannot refuse it. The Worker is deployed with the compatibility flag `global_fetch_strictly_public`, in `wrangler.jsonc`, which sends every `fetch` to a public host through the front door of its zone, as a request from anywhere. Every host that pigy asks is a public one, so nothing else changes by it. The route that reads a card for the editor also refuses the host that it was reached at. The step of the workflow does not: no request stands behind it, so it does not know that host, and what it would read there is the app itself, which is public. That the flag routes as described for the way pigy is deployed is to be confirmed on the deployment (section 19).
- The addresses of avatars and profiles come from the networks. Only HTTPS addresses are passed on. The address that a page sends the browser to for a connection comes from an adapter: it has to be HTTPS, or an address of the app itself.
- The simulated networks are part of the production build. Section 7 says what keeps them to their simulated world.
- Media links for Threads are signed and expire. Threads takes no bytes: it is told an address and fetches an image itself, without a session. `apps/worker/src/media/links.ts` makes such a link and checks it, and `/api/media-links/…` serves it. A link names one file of one image of one workspace and the time at which it ends, a quarter of an hour on, and is signed over exactly those with HMAC-SHA-256: another image, another file of the same image, another workspace or a later time is another signature, and the comparison is the platform's, in a time that does not depend on how much of a signature is right. Only what is published can be named, the image itself or one that was made of it for a network: not the upload, not the small picture. Whatever is wrong with a link, the answer is the same 404, and nothing lists anything. A link is a capability, so it is made when a step is about to hand it to the network and is put nowhere: not into the progress of a delivery, not into the result of a step, not into a log. The key is made of the keys that seal credentials, so a pigy that can publish can sign, with nothing more to set up, and replacing those keys ends at most the links of the deliveries that are under way. It is built and tested in phase 7 and not used yet: the adapter of Threads publishes no images so far, section 10.
- The bytes of an image are untrusted, and served as nothing but an image: the type is the one that the server read in the bytes when it took the file, with `nosniff`, as an attachment, under a content security policy that lets nothing run, from the origin of the app only to the owner of the workspace. A file is laid out as one of four types or refused, and what follows the end of an image is left out of the copy that pigy keeps, so a file that is an image and a script is an image afterwards: section 10.
- Signing out deletes what the device holds of the person: the local copies of both workspaces in IndexedDB, what it remembers of the session, and the choice between live and sandbox, in every tab. If there are changes that were not synced, in either workspace, the app says so first and asks. What the service worker keeps stays, because it is the app and nothing of the person: section 13.
- So does learning that the session has ended, by expiry or because an invitation was withdrawn. There is one exception. A session ends without the person's doing, and a copy can hold changes that were made offline and never synced: deleting it would lose work that nobody chose to lose. Such a copy is kept. It is not shown to anybody, and the sign-in page says that it is there, whose it is and how many changes it holds. They are synced when the same person signs in again. They are deleted when somebody else signs in on the device, or when whoever is at the device says so on the sign-in page. What is kept is what a copy holds. A change that a tab could not store, on a device that is out of space, is in no copy. A tab whose session ends by itself therefore stores what it holds before it leaves, and stays, with a dialog that says so, while it cannot: section 4. For as long as a window waits like that, the copies are kept also when they hold nothing that was not synced, so that the window has something to store into: the window holds the Web Lock `pigy/unsaved` from the moment it hears of the end until it leaves, also while a write is only under way, and whoever is about to delete the copies looks for the lock. The device records that the session is over at once, and the copies go later, when nothing needs them: the sign-in page looks every time it is loaded, so copies that were kept for a window are deleted the next time somebody is there after the window has stored its changes and they were synced, has been told to go without them, or was closed. The sign-in page says what other windows could not store, next to what the copies hold, and counts both when it is asked to delete: deleting starts those windows again. A window that holds the lock and does not say how many changes it holds, because the browser has put it aside or because its write is still under way, is not taken to hold none: the page then warns of changes without a number, or of at least as many as the other windows counted, and asks about them in those words before it deletes. A session is taken on and what is left of one is deleted under one Web Lock, `pigy/session`, so that the two never interleave: a deletion that had begun would otherwise take the copies of a session that began in its middle, with what that session had stored by then. Whoever deletes looks at what the device holds once it has the lock. That is not done for somebody else who signs in: their sign-in deletes what the device holds of the person before, what a tab could not store included.
- A content security policy, since phase 11a: below. An audit trail of publishes per workspace is phase 11b.

**Sealing.** `apps/worker/src/vault.ts` seals a value before it is stored and opens it when it is needed, with AES-256-GCM and a new random nonce each time.

- The keys are the Worker secret `CREDENTIAL_KEYS`: a list of `<version>:<key>`. The key with the highest version seals, and each key opens what it sealed. A sealed value begins with the version of its key.
- A key is replaced by adding one with a higher version and keeping the old one. What the old key sealed is sealed again only when it is next written, so the old key stays for as long as anything is left that it sealed. The sealed values are the credentials of the channels, the registrations with Mastodon instances and the connections that have begun, and for some minutes the credentials that a connection replaced, in `keyed_call` of a workspace: section 6. Dropping it earlier costs a new registration with each Mastodon instance, which happens by itself, and a new connection of each channel that it sealed. Sealing the credentials of a channel again when they are read, so that an old key runs out of use, belongs to the step of the publishing workflow that opens them, `accessOf` in `apps/worker/src/publish/workflow.ts`. It is not built: until it is, an old key is dropped only when no channel is left that it sealed, and a channel whose key is gone fails its next post and asks to be connected again.
- A value is sealed for the place it is stored in: the workspace, network and account of a channel, the scope and domain of a registration, and for a connection that has begun its state together with the session, the workspace and the network that its row names. It does not open in another place, so somebody who can write to a database and has no key cannot give one channel the credentials of another, or hand the connection that one session began to another session, workspace or network.
- The Worker seals and opens, where it calls an adapter: in the routes that connect and disconnect, and in the steps of the publishing workflow. For the credentials of a channel that is one small module, `apps/worker/src/channels/credentials.ts`, and no other code calls the vault for them. The `WorkspaceDO` stores the credentials of a channel as it gets them and has no part in it. Credentials in the clear exist in memory only, between an adapter and the vault, and are in no log, no error, no sync message, no answer and no state of a workflow.
- A sealed value is kept out of the logs like the secret in it, and so is the hash of a session. The one way that they got there is an error of the database: Drizzle puts the values that a statement was run with into the error of a statement that fails. So the error handling of the app logs such an error as the statement and the reason, without the values, wherever in the causes of an error it is, for every route: `loggable` in `apps/worker/src/errors.ts`.
- Without keys nothing can be sealed, and then nothing is stored instead: no account can be connected, and the request to begin is refused before anything is asked of a network. A build for development and tests has a key from the repository for that case, so that a fresh clone needs no setup. It is not part of a production build, which is checked for it. A route uses it only where the app is reached locally. A step of the publishing workflow is reached at no address, so in a build that has the key it opens with it what a route sealed: `vaultWithoutRequest`.

**The keys of pigy's client of Bluesky** are a secret of the Worker too, `BLUESKY_CLIENT_KEYS`, and are not sealed: they are pigy's own, like the keys that seal. Their public halves are in pigy's client metadata, section 6. They follow the rules of the keys above: a build for development and tests has a key from the repository, which a production build is checked for, a route uses it only where the app is reached locally, and work that no request stands behind, such as a refresh in a step of the publishing workflow, uses it wherever the build has it. A key that leaks is removed from the list at once, which ends the sessions that began with it, at the latest when Bluesky reads the metadata again.

**The key that pigy signs push messages with**, `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY`, is a secret of the Worker and pigy's own, like the keys of its client of Bluesky, and follows their rules: a build for development and tests has a key from the repository, which a production build is checked for, a route uses it only where the app is reached locally, and the sending, which no request stands behind, uses it wherever the build has it. Section 13 says why it is one key and not a list. What a browser hands out with a subscription, its endpoint and its two keys, is kept in D1 as it is: the keys open nothing, and nobody can send that browser a message as pigy without the key of the Worker. They are kept out of the logs all the same, with the endpoint, which is an address that only the browser and pigy should know.

**The application of pigy at LinkedIn**, `LINKEDIN_CLIENT_ID` and `LINKEDIN_CLIENT_SECRET`, is a secret of the Worker as well, and pigy's own. It is used for real only. A sandbox has an application of its simulated world, which is no secret: section 6. So no build has a stand-in for it, and a production build is checked for none.

**The app of pigy at Threads**, `THREADS_APP_ID` and `THREADS_APP_SECRET`, is the same in all of that. One thing is its own: Meta documents the secret of the app, and the tokens of an account, as parameters in the address of a request. They go nowhere but to Threads in that place: pigy logs no address of a request to a network, and an adapter takes the secrets of a request out of whatever a network repeats of it before a message is made of the answer. The token of an account is in the address of every request that reads, which the platform would write down if it were asked to: the traces and the logs of outbound requests that Cloudflare offers for a Worker must stay off for as long as that is so. `wrangler.jsonc` turns Workers Logs on for what pigy writes itself and says of the traces that they are off: "The log" below. Section 19 has what would end it.

**The object of a workspace opens credentials too**, since phase 3e, where it renews an access by its timer: with `openCredentials`, in the call to the adapter and for it, as a step of the workflow does. It is built with the keys that the workflow is built with, and seals what the refresh returned before it stores it.

**The content security policy and the other headers**, since phase 11a. Every answer says what a browser may do with it, in one of two places, because pigy is served from two: Cloudflare serves the files of the web app without the Worker, and the Worker answers the rest.

- **The app shell and every other file**: `apps/web/public/_headers`, the rule for `/*`. `default-src 'none'`, and then only what the app loads: its own scripts and its own style sheet, its fonts, the manifest and the service worker, `connect-src 'self'` for the API and the sync socket, and pictures from the app, from `blob:` and from any HTTPS address. `blob:` is there since phase 7: every picture of an image is shown from an address that the page makes of bytes that it holds. The file that a person has chosen is shown so before and while it is uploaded, and the small pictures of a workspace and the images of the feeds of a sandbox are fetched with the API, which serves them with `no-store` to the owner alone, and are kept in the memory of the page, which shows them from there: an image is somebody's, and no cache of the browser or of the service worker is to keep it, section 13. A `blob:` address names bytes that the page itself holds, so it lets nothing in from elsewhere. Nothing of an image needs more: no canvas, no worker and no request to another host. HTTPS is as wide as it is because the pictures of the connected accounts, their avatars, are at the networks, and every Mastodon server and every server of a Bluesky account is a host of its own that nobody can list. A picture can run nothing, and the page sends no referrer with it, `Referrer-Policy: same-origin`. What a network learns is that its avatar was loaded, from which address, and when: that the account is open in pigy somewhere. pigy does not fetch the avatars itself for that, which would make it a proxy for pictures that anybody names. `form-action 'none'`: no form of the app is sent by the browser, and signing in and connecting an account are links and redirects. `frame-ancestors 'none'` and `base-uri 'none'`.
- **No `'unsafe-inline'` and no `'unsafe-eval'`**, for scripts or for styles. Three things needed something done:
  - The script in `index.html` that sets the theme before the first paint is allowed by the hash of its text. The build computes the hash and writes it into `_headers`, `apps/web/security-headers-plugin.ts`, so nobody keeps it by hand, and fails if `index.html` has a second inline script, an inline style or an inline event handler, or if `_headers` has lost the place for the hash.
  - The notice library, sonner, adds a `<style>` element to the page as it runs: first empty, then with its style sheet. Both are allowed by their hashes, which the build computes from the installed library. An update of the library that adds its styles in another way fails the build, and one that changes them changes the hash with it. What React sets of a style it sets through the style object of an element, which a policy is not asked about, so no `style` attribute needs allowing.
  - Zod compiles a schema with `new Function` where a browser lets it, and finds out by trying, which the policy refuses and reports. The builds of the app tell Zod not to, `apps/web/zod-without-eval.ts`, as it does not in the Worker either.
- **What the Worker answers**: `apps/worker/src/security-headers.ts`, a middleware over every route, the errors included. An answer that is no page, which is nearly all of them, gets `default-src 'none'` and nothing else. A page gets a policy that is made of the page: no script at all, and the style sheets in its head by their hashes. That is what the pages of the simulated networks and of the simulated Google need, which are HTML with one style sheet and a form: `form-action 'self'`, which a browser also asks of the redirect that answers the form, back to a callback of this pigy. A style sheet further down in a page, where it shows what others have written, is not allowed. A route that needs more sets a policy of its own, which is left alone. So a page that is added later starts from a policy that allows it nothing, and not from none. The bytes of an image, which the routes of images serve, have a policy of their own that is narrower still: the one of what is no page, and `sandbox`, so that a browser that is sent to their address and shows them as a document of their own gives it no origin and runs nothing in it, `imagePolicy`.
- **With them**: `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`. `Referrer-Policy: same-origin`, so that no other site learns an address of pigy, which can name a draft. Not `no-referrer`: a browser would then send `Origin: null` with a form of a simulated consent page, which the Worker refuses. `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin`, which does no harm to the OAuth flows because they leave the page and come back to it, and open no window. A `Permissions-Policy` that turns off what pigy does not use. And `Strict-Transport-Security`, which the Worker sends with its own answers over HTTPS at a deployed address, never at `localhost`. `_headers` does not have it: a rule there cannot tell a deployed address from a local one, and a rule for HTTPS addresses alone applied over HTTP in the local runtime too. A browser goes by the header for the whole host once it has seen it, and the app asks the API who is signed in every time it starts.
- **Not in `pnpm dev`**, which has no `_headers`: the dev server adds scripts of its own to the page. The policy is tested against the built app, by every end-to-end test: section 15.
- **The page that describes the public API**, `/api/v1/docs` of phase 6, needs no policy of its own: it is plain HTML with one style sheet in its head and no script, which is what the policy of a page of the Worker allows, the style sheet by its hash. It loads nothing, and the policy would refuse a request of its own: what reads the document, `openapi.json`, is somebody else's code.
- **What the next phases add to the policy**, with the code that needs it and not before. A page of the Worker that needs more than the policy of a page, such as one with a script, comes with a policy of its own, which it sets on its answer. None does today.
- **Reports of what the policy refuses**, `report-to`, are phase 11b. Today a refusal shows in the console of the browser and in the end-to-end tests, and nobody hears of one on a device.
- **What it relies on**: that Cloudflare serves `index.html` byte for byte. On `workers.dev` it does. A zone of the owner's can be set to change pages on their way, which would break the hash: `SETUP.md` says what to leave off.

**The log**, since phase 11a. Everything that goes wrong in the Worker is one line of JSON, written by `log` of `apps/worker/src/log.ts`, which is the only code of the Worker that writes to the console: Biome refuses `console` everywhere else in its source. A line has a level, a kind to search by, a message in words, the IDs of what it is about, and the error with its stack and its causes. Every error passes through `loggable` on its way, so nobody who logs has to think of the values of a failed query. Of an error a line has what it says of itself, by a short list of names, `toldOf`: its kind, its status, its code. What else it carries, and a cause that is no error, is what a library compared or a server answered: the state and the parameters of a callback with its code, the claims of a token with the address of a person, an answer with its headers. A line says what kind of thing that is and nothing of what it holds. A sign-in that failed is logged by the name, the code and the message of its error alone, and by the code of the OAuth error where Google refused pigy's request for a token. A line names a request by its method and the pattern of its route, `/api/admin/allowed-emails/:email`, and never by its path, which holds the address of a person there, or by its address, whose query can hold the code of a callback. It holds no text of a post and no name or address of a person or an account.

`wrangler.jsonc` turns Workers Logs on for these lines and for nothing else:

- **No traces.** A trace records the requests that the Worker makes, and the token of a Threads account is in the address of the ones that read. `observability.enabled` for all of it is not set, because it would turn the traces on with the logs: the logs are turned on by themselves, and the traces are said to be off.
- **No invocation logs**, which record every request that the Worker is called with, with its address: the callback of a sign-in or a connection has its code there. What is lost is a list of the requests with their statuses, which the dashboard of the Worker counts all the same.
- **Query strings are taken out** of whatever is recorded, `redact_query_string`, for the day on which somebody turns one of the two on. That is a second line and not the first: nobody has seen what a trace of a deployed pigy holds.

No alerting service is set up. `SETUP.md` says what to look for.

## 17. Phases

Each phase ends with something that works and is covered by tests. Sizes are relative: S, M, L.

| # | Phase | Size | Done when |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Foundations | S | Monorepo, a hello-world Worker with the React shell running in the local Workers runtime, CI green on lint, typecheck, tests, build and a Playwright smoke test |
| 1 | Sign-in and invite-only access | M | Google sign-in works, a non-invited email is refused, admins manage the allowlist, simulated Google sign-in drives the tests |
| 2 | Local-first core | L | The client store is chosen by a spike. Drafts can be created and edited offline, sync across tabs and devices, and the app installs as a PWA |
| 3 | Networks, simulators, sandbox | L | All four simulators and adapters exist. A text post publishes now to all four, in the sandbox and for real. Conformance and pipeline tests pass |
| 4 | Per-network text and threads | M | Live character counters, per-network overrides, thread editor, link cards, mentions and hashtags |
| 5 | Scheduling and queue | M | Exact-time scheduling, weekly slots, add to queue, reorder, pause, queue and calendar views |
| 6 | API keys and REST API | M | Keys can be created and revoked, the API passes its tests in both environments, OpenAPI docs are served |
| 7 | Images | M | Upload, alt text, per-network variants, published to all four |
| 8 | Video | L | Chunked upload, client-side checks, each network's upload and processing flow |
| 9 | Phone features | M | Push notifications, in-app inbox, share target, install prompts |
| 10 | Engagement stats | S | Stats collected and shown on sent posts |
| 11 | Hardening and launch | M | Deployment from CI, security review, reconnect flows, logging and alerts, backups checked, setup guide, friends invited. In two steps, below |

Phase 2 is built in three steps. 2a is the server side: workspaces, the `WorkspaceDO`, the mutators, and the sync protocol with its tests. 2b is the client: the sync store, IndexedDB, the offline outbox, the tabs of a browser, and the drafts screen. 2c is the PWA shell: the service worker, the manifest, starting the app without a network, and installing it. After 2b the data was there without a network and the app itself was not: loading it took the server. Section 13 has what 2c did about that.

Phase 3 is built in steps too. 3a is in the packages only: the provider interface, what the simulators share, and Mastodon as the first network, with its adapter, its simulator and the conformance suite. The steps after it put that to use in the Worker:

- 3b-1, the server side of channels: the synced table `channel` with its sealed credentials, the routes that connect and disconnect an account, the simulated world of each sandbox, and the guard that keeps the end-to-end build off the network. Section 6 has the routes and what was decided.
- 3b-2, the publishing pipeline, server side: targets and their results as synced tables, the mutators that choose channels, publish and retry, the workflow, and the queue of each channel in the `WorkspaceDO`. It adds the limits of an account to its channel, sets `needs_reconnect`, reads the credentials of a channel inside each step, makes the time of the server move forward only, and gives `mutateWorkspace` a key for callers that try again. It also has the routes that the sandbox screens read: the feed of a simulated world and its faults. Sections 6, 7 and 8 have what was decided. 3b-3b added the screens.
- 3b-3, the web side, in two steps. 3b-3a: the Live/Sandbox switch and the Channels screen, with connecting, reconnecting and disconnecting. Sections 6 and 7 have what was decided. 3b-3b, after the publishing pipeline: publishing from the composer, the Sent screen, the simulated feeds and the failure panel, and with them the decision whether a new sandbox comes with accounts and drafts, which it does not. Sections 7 and 8 have what was decided.

Phase 9 was built on what phases 3 and 4 have, without waiting for the phases between: section 13 has what was decided. Its push messages and its inbox tell of what the pipeline of phase 3 does, and will tell of scheduled posts when phase 5 has them without a change. Sharing files into pigy waits for media.

Then Bluesky, LinkedIn and Threads follow, each the way Mastodon was built. 3c is Bluesky, in the packages and the Worker, and offered on the Channels screen: section 6 has what was decided. Its live run is still to be made, section 19. 3d is LinkedIn, for members and for text, the same way, and with it what the pipeline needed for a network on which a step must not be run twice: sections 6 and 8. Its live run is still to be made too. 3e is Threads, for text, the same way, and with it the timer that renews an access when its time has come, for Bluesky too: section 6. Its live run is still to be made as well, and takes a pigy that is deployed: Meta sends a browser back to HTTPS addresses only.

Phase 4 is built in two steps. 4a is the editor and the data: the mutators that make a thread, the counters per network under each part, and the text that one account gets in place of the post's, which the pipeline publishes. Sections 6 and 8 have what was decided. 4b is what the adapters do with a text: link cards, and mentions and hashtags marked up for each network, with the editor marking them as they are typed and the feeds of the sandbox showing what a network made of them. Section 6 has what was decided.

Phase 7 is built in steps. 7a is everything that is not one network's: the synced tables and their mutators, the upload in chunks into R2, what pigy takes for an image and the copy that it keeps, the bytes served to their owner and at signed links, the limits per network, the step of the workflow that makes a smaller image, the source of images in the contract with a network and what every adapter does with one in the conformance suite, retention by the alarm, the editor, the Sent screen and the feeds of the sandbox. And two networks, each with its simulator and its adapter: Mastodon and Bluesky. Section 10 has what was decided, and section 6 how each network takes an image. 7b is LinkedIn and Threads, which are left: until then a post with an image is not sent to an account of either, and the editor says so. With them come the API's media, once phase 6 is on `main`, and sharing image files into pigy.

Phase 5 is one step: the mutators and tables of scheduling, the alarm of the object, and the screens. Section 9 has what was decided. It was finished after phase 9 and put on top of it: the alarm of the two is the one alarm of section 9, and a post that is held is told of in the inbox of section 13.

Phase 6 is one step too: the keys in D1 with their screen in the settings, the routes under `/api/v1` with what they do in the object of a workspace, and the OpenAPI document with its page. Section 12 has what was decided.

Phase 10 is one step, built on phase 9 before phases 7 and 8: the method of the contract that reads the stats of a post and its answers in the simulators, the synced table `metric` with the pass of the alarm that fills it, the Sent screen and `metrics` of the API. Section 14 has what was decided. Its stats are of text posts, and need nothing of media.

Phase 11 is built in two steps, because its first half needs no Cloudflare account and its second half needs a pigy that is deployed.

- 11a is what can be built and tested locally: the content security policy and the other headers, and the 404 for a file under `/assets/` that is not there (sections 13 and 16), deployment from CI, which is off until the owner switches it on (section 15), the log (section 16), and the setup guide, `SETUP.md`, which has everything that the owner does by hand, backups and logs included.
- 11b is what is left, and most of it takes the deployed address: the security review, reports of what the content security policy refuses (section 16), the audit trail of publishes per workspace (section 16), what happens to the channels of a person who is removed from the allowlist (section 6, "Renewing an access by the timer"), the reconnect flows on real networks, alerting, and the checks on real devices and against real networks that section 19 lists. It ends with friends invited.

Until the owner has deployed pigy, with `SETUP.md`, every phase is built and verified locally against the simulated networks. Connecting real networks and installing on a phone need the deployed address.

Usable milestones:

- After phase 3: cross-posting text from your phone.
- After phase 6: a scheduler with an API.
- After phase 8: full media.
- After phase 11: everything in this plan.

Each network in phase 3 is built in the same order: conformance spec, simulator, adapter passing against the simulator, then one live run to confirm that both match reality. Order: Mastodon, Bluesky, LinkedIn, Threads.

## 18. What you need to set up

`SETUP.md` has these as steps, in order, with a check after each. This section stays the reference for what is registered where.

| What | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| Cloudflare account | Workers, D1, R2, Durable Objects, Workflows, Images. R2 needs a payment method on file. Budget for the Workers Paid plan. The bucket of the images is made once, before the first deploy: `wrangler r2 bucket create pigy-media`. It stays private: no public address, no custom domain. The Images binding makes the smaller images and the small pictures, and is billed by the transformation |
| Address | `https://pigy.<your-subdomain>.workers.dev`, free and with HTTPS. The subdomain is set once per Cloudflare account, under Workers in the dashboard. pigy takes its address from each request (section 11). Work that no request stands behind takes it from `APP_URL`, which only the link at which Threads fetches an image needs, phase 7b (section 6), and which can wait until then |
| Google Cloud OAuth client | Set the consent screen to "In production". In "Testing", only listed test users can sign in. Basic scopes need no review. The redirect address is `<address>/api/auth/google/callback`. Client ID and secret become the Worker secrets `GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID` and `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` |
| First admin | Your Google email address, as the Worker secret `ADMIN_EMAILS` |
| LinkedIn developer app | At https://www.linkedin.com/developers/apps. Must be linked to a LinkedIn Page. A placeholder page is enough. Add the products "Share on LinkedIn" and "Sign In with LinkedIn using OpenID Connect", and the redirect address `<address>/connect/linkedin/callback`. Client ID and secret become the Worker secrets `LINKEDIN_CLIENT_ID` and `LINKEDIN_CLIENT_SECRET`. Without them only the sandbox connects LinkedIn accounts |
| Meta developer app | At https://developers.facebook.com/apps, with the use case "Access the Threads API". Add the permissions `threads_basic`, `threads_content_publish`, `threads_manage_replies`, `threads_read_replies`, `threads_delete` and, since phase 10, `threads_manage_insights`, which reads the stats of a post, and the redirect callback address `https://<host>/connect/threads/callback`: Meta takes HTTPS addresses only. The Threads app ID and the Threads app secret of the use case, which are not those of the Meta app, become the Worker secrets `THREADS_APP_ID` and `THREADS_APP_SECRET`. Without them only the sandbox connects Threads accounts. The settings of the use case also have fields for an uninstall callback URL and a delete callback URL, which Meta may require before it saves the settings (a guess, section 19): enter `https://<host>/connect/threads/uninstall` and `https://<host>/connect/threads/delete`. Nothing answers at them yet: Meta gets pigy's error for a path that it does not have, and a person who removes pigy in their Threads settings is heard of when the next post or renewal is refused. Add each friend as a Threads tester. They accept the invite in their Threads settings |
| Test accounts | One per network for the live conformance run |

Mastodon and Bluesky need no developer registration. VAPID keys, the Bluesky client key and the token encryption key are generated during setup and stored as Worker secrets. The token encryption key is `CREDENTIAL_KEYS`, in the form `1:<key>`, with the output of `openssl rand -base64 32` as the key. The Bluesky client key is `BLUESKY_CLIENT_KEYS`, in the same form, with the output of `openssl genpkey -algorithm EC -pkeyopt ec_paramgen_curve:P-256 | openssl pkcs8 -topk8 -nocrypt -outform DER | openssl base64 -A` as the key. The VAPID key is `VAPID_PRIVATE_KEY`: the output of the same command, by itself, without a version.

## 19. Risks and things to verify

| Risk | Mitigation |
| --- | --- |
| The client store is our own code, where a library would have had other users to find its bugs | It is small, and its rules are those of SYNC.md, which are tested one by one in `packages/core` and against the real workspace object. Section 4 says why it is not TanStack DB |
| A deleted post is brought back by making a new one with the same text, which is to go to the accounts that the deleted one was to go to, where they are still connected | Enough while a draft is its parts, its targets and the texts of single accounts, which the client puts back with the mutators that made them: phase 4a does so for a thread. The new post has other IDs. Media and a place in the queue are more than a client can put back by hand: undo then needs a mutator of its own, or deletion that waits |
| atproto OAuth libraries are written for Node and may not run on Workers | Settled in phase 3c: pigy's client of atproto OAuth is written on the Web Crypto API, section 6. What it gets wrong only a live run shows, and none has been made: see below |
| Threads is limited to testers | Fine for friends. Opening it up needs Meta's app review |
| LinkedIn tokens die after 60 days | The Channels screen says the day. A week before, the inbox and the devices are told, once for that end, and the notification leads to connecting the account again: section 13 |
| A browser with a push service that pigy does not know cannot turn notifications on | The hosts of the push services are a list, `pushServiceHosts`, because an address at any other host is one that somebody made up (section 13). Settings say that pigy cannot send notifications to this browser yet, and that the inbox has what they would say, in the words of the Worker that refused the address, and the browser's subscription is ended again: "Turn on" leaves no browser subscribed whose subscription the server did not take. The host has to be added, in the code |
| No real push service and no phone has met what pigy sends | The sender is tested against the example of RFC 8291 and against a simulated push service that is written apart from it, from the same standards. What only a deployed pigy and a phone show is listed below |
| On LinkedIn pigy cannot find out whether a post arrived when the answer is lost | The delivery says that it is unknown and asks the person to look, and nothing is sent a second time by itself: sections 6 and 8. For some minutes LinkedIn itself refuses a second post of the same text |
| No LinkedIn stats | Shown as unavailable. Revisit together with company pages, which need the same approval |
| Video rejected by one network after upload | Client-side checks against the limits of the selected networks before upload |
| Simulators drift from the real APIs | Live conformance run, nightly or before releases |
| The first version is large | Phases are ordered so pigy is useful after phase 3 |
| A session that ends by itself can still lose a change that is being written in another window | The window that finds out waits for its own changes, and keeps the copies while any window holds a change that it could not store or waits to store one (sections 4 and 16). Another window says that it waits only once it has heard of the end, a moment later. A write of such a window that is under way in that moment, and has not failed, is not known to the window that found out: if that one has nothing to wait for and the copies hold nothing else that was not synced, they are deleted, and that change with them. It takes a keystroke in one window within milliseconds of the end of the session being found out in another |
| A new version of the app does not take over while a window waits after its session has ended | The window holds the lock `pigy/unsaved` for its whole wait, which a page that starts takes for a window with changes that it could not store (section 13). That is what it is, for as long as it matters: the wait is over in a moment unless the device cannot store |
| A window whose session has ended stays open over a workspace that it may no longer show | Only while it holds changes that it could not store, under a dialog that cannot be closed, and until the person lets them go (section 4). What is behind the dialog is what the device showed a moment before, and the window is off the network. Somebody else who signs in on the device ends it at once. If the same person signs in again elsewhere, the window still says that they were signed out until it has stored its changes, and then starts again |
| Firefox may ask the person to keep the data more often than they like | pigy asks for the promise with the first change after every page load until it is given (section 13). To verify in Firefox, where it was not tried: that an answer of the person is remembered, and how often a prompt that was only dismissed comes again. The tests run in Chromium, which never asks the person. If it is too often, remember on the device that the question was put, and leave the rest to the button in Settings |
| A service worker that goes wrong keeps a device on a broken app, and the server cannot reach in to mend it | It does one thing, keeping the files of a build, and takes one only whole (section 13). Its rules are tested without a browser, and offline starts and updates in one. A fixed version reaches a device like any other: the browser fetches `sw.js` anew after every page load |
| A new version that takes over loads every other window of the app again, also one that somebody is using | It takes a window that starts while another one is in use, such as the installed app next to a tab, or a link that opens pigy in a new window. Drafts are stored with every keystroke, so what is lost is what is on screen only: a form that is half filled in, a dialog that is open (section 13). To address if it matters: a window with such input could hold the new version back, as one with changes that it could not store does, or ask first |
| A new version that changes the synced schema leaves a device without its drafts if it first starts offline | The screen says so, and nothing that was not synced is lost. Section 13 has the cure, for whoever first changes the schema under people |
| A person who was removed from the allowlist keeps an access to their accounts that pigy holds and does not use | Their workspaces are frozen and renew nothing, so the access ends at the network: in 60 days on Threads and LinkedIn, when the session ends on Bluesky. A token of Mastodon does not end, and stays sealed in the workspace until the person disconnects the account or ends the access at the instance. Section 6 has why the channels are not disconnected: a removal can be a mistake |
| A browser that kept the app shell under the name of a file of `/assets/`, from a pigy before phase 11a, keeps it for the year that it was given | Nothing was deployed before phase 11a, so no browser has. Since then such a path is a 404 that nothing keeps: section 13, "Serving" |

To confirm when the relevant phase starts:

- For Threads, what was open here is decided in phase 3e, section 6, "Threads", and what of it rests on something that Meta does not document is in the list of phase 3e below.
- Whether Bluesky video upload works with the OAuth scopes pigy requests: `blob:video/*`, and nothing for the token of the video service.
- Decided in phase 3d, see the end of section 8: a step of LinkedIn's adapter that was cut off is not run again. The workspace notes that a step begins before the adapter is called, and a second run of the step ends the delivery as `uncertain`. LinkedIn's behaviour on duplicate content and its version string are in the list of phase 3d below.
- Decided in phase 3b-2, see section 8: the deliveries to one channel are kept to one at a time by the `WorkspaceDO`, whose targets are the queue. Later deliveries pass a failed target, and a "Retry" of one that had published no part then starts over, on a network whose adapter does not tell the deliveries to an account apart: Mastodon, and not Bluesky. An account that is connected as two channels in two workspaces gets nothing new: nothing keeps the deliveries of the two apart.
- Current media size and duration limits per network.
- Workflows in the Workers Vitest integration were confirmed in phase 3b-2: they work, with the engine of the local runtime, and `reset()` clears their runs between tests. A run can be paused, resumed and restarted from a test, its waits can be skipped, and its steps and their results read. One caveat: a test and a step of a workflow must not wake each other with a promise, or the test run hangs without a timeout. AGENTS.md has the rule. WebSockets to Durable Objects were confirmed in phase 2a: they work, hibernation included.
- Not confirmed, because nothing is deployed yet: what the Workflows service answers for a run that does not exist. The watch of the `WorkspaceDO` takes a failure that says "not found" for a run that is gone, as the local runtime says it, and any other failure for a service that is in trouble. If the service words it otherwise, a delivery whose run was deleted by hand stays `sending`: `#isOver` in `apps/worker/src/workspace/object.ts`.
- Not confirmed either: that a run whose step is cut off by a deploy is taken up again as the documentation of Workflows says, with the results of the steps before. The tests pause, resume and restart runs, which is as close as the local runtime gets.
- GUESS, not confirmed: that a step of a workflow may return 1 MiB, which is what the documentation of Workflows gave as the most that is kept of one step when phase 4a was built. `stepResultBytes` in `apps/worker/src/publish/workflow.ts` has the number, and `maxContentLength` keeps the text of a delivery to about half of it at worst. Nothing local enforces the limit, so a test cannot show what the platform does with a larger result. Confirm the number before the first deploy.
- Whether usage fits the Cloudflare free plan.
- Decided in phase 11a without waiting to see how a deploy spreads: a path under `/assets/` that has no file is a 404 that nothing keeps, whoever asks and however, section 13, "Serving". If a browser can get the app shell of the new version from one of Cloudflare's servers and be told by another that a file of it is not there, the page fails to load that file once, and the next load finds it. To confirm in phase 11b: whether that happens at all, and whether the app should then load itself again.
- In phase 11b, on real devices, everything that section 13 could not test: installing on Android and from Safari on iOS, the icon and the colours that each shows, the insets around the status bar and the bar for gestures, signing in with Google inside the installed app, and a new version taking over in WebKit. An app that is added to the Home Screen of iOS has storage of its own: the person signs in again there, and its local copy starts empty.

Open since phase 9, none of it confirmed: nothing is deployed, so no real push service has been sent anything, and no phone has shown a notification or shared to pigy. The browser of the tests has no push service and refuses notifications to every site. Where the simulated push service had to assume an answer, the place in `packages/simulators/src/push` is marked GUESS, and every such mark is in this list. What a deployed pigy and a real phone have to show:

- That the push services of Chrome, Firefox and Safari take what pigy sends: the token, the headers and the encrypted message. The first thing to try is turning notifications on in each, in the sandbox, and making a post fail there with the failure panel.
- What each answers a token that does not pass, and one without a contact: 403 in the simulator (GUESS). The Worker goes by 404 and 410 alone to drop a device, and logs every other refusal, so a guess that is wrong costs a message and no subscription.
- Whether the push services of Firefox and Safari take `mailto:pigy@localhost` as the contact of a sender, for a `pnpm preview` on somebody's machine. A deployed pigy says its own HTTPS address, which the standard has.
- That a push service answers 415 for another encoding than `aes128gcm` (GUESS), which the Worker never sends.
