- That the service worker of the build shows a message as a notification, with its icon and its badge, also on a lock screen and while the app is closed, and what an Android and an iPhone make of the title and the two lines.
- That pressing a notification brings an open window of pigy to the front and has it go to the post or the account, and opens the app when none is open. On iOS in particular: whether a service worker may focus a window, and what happens when it may not.
- That a notification about the sandbox opens the sandbox on a device whose last choice was live, in the installed app as in a tab.
- On an iPhone: that "Add to Home Screen" makes an app in which Settings can turn notifications on, that the hint in a tab of Safari is shown there and is right, and that a subscription survives the app being closed. An app on the Home Screen of iOS has storage of its own, so the person signs in again there.
- That Android offers the installed pigy in its share sheet, what Chrome, a notes app and a social app put into `title`, `text` and `url`, and that what the share screen shows, and the draft that it saves, reads as it should for each. `apps/web/src/lib/share.ts` has the rules, which were written from what such apps are known to send.
- What the browsers do when a push subscription ends or is replaced: whether Chrome, Firefox and Safari send `pushsubscriptionchange` at all, and whether a subscription that its push service has dropped is still handed out by `getSubscription`. pigy does not depend on either: the app compares the subscription of the browser with the one that was turned on when it starts, and "Turn on" makes a new one where the server does not send to the one at hand. What is lost: the notifications between the change and the next start of the app, for that device, which the inbox has. And a device whose subscription was dropped by its push service without the browser changing it stays off until the person turns it on again, which Settings say.
- Whether the list of the hosts of the push services is complete: `fcm.googleapis.com`, `*.push.services.mozilla.com`, `*.push.apple.com` and `*.notify.windows.com`. A subscription of a real Edge, Firefox and Safari says.
- That a window of the app answers the service worker within 400 milliseconds of the press of a notification, also on a slow phone and when the phone had put the app aside: where it does not, a second window is opened, if the browser still opens one. The time cannot be much longer: Firefox lets a service worker call `clients.openWindow()` and `focus()` only for about a second after the press, and fails both with `InvalidAccessError` after that.
- Whether a browser opens a window for a service worker that has brought another to the front for the same press. Chrome is believed to allow one of the two per press (from its code, not tried), which is why a window that the person sees is asked before it is brought to the front. For a hidden window that does not answer, as one of a version of the app that does not know the message, the press then ends in pigy and not at the post or the account.
- That the notice which offers to install after the first post is shown on a phone at the width that phones have, and that "Install" opens Chrome's own question.
- How long a push service keeps a message: pigy asks for a day (`TTL`), and a push service may keep it for less.

Open since phase 3a, all of it about Mastodon and none of it confirmed against a real server, because the live conformance run has not been made yet. The first run settles most of it. Where the simulator had to assume an answer, the place in `packages/simulators/src/mastodon` is marked GUESS, and every such mark is in this list. The rest of the list is about the adapter and the run itself:

- That the live run works at all: it is written and has never been started.
- That a status with the visibility `direct` and no mention is accepted and seen by its author alone, which the live run relies on.
- What counts as a link. pigy and the simulator take any top-level domain of two or more ASCII letters, Mastodon only the ones that exist. Mastodon's development branch has also replaced the pattern that 4.5 uses. A text that pigy counts as shorter than Mastodon does is refused by Mastodon, with a message that says so.
- That grapheme clusters are counted alike by the browser, the Workers runtime and Mastodon's Ruby. They follow the same standard in versions that differ, which matters for the newest emoji only.
- How Mastodon answers a token request of a client without a scope when the client has not registered `read`, and whether servers that speak Mastodon's API without being Mastodon (GoToSocial, Pleroma, Akkoma) answer the check of a registration in a way the adapter understands. The adapter asks for a scope by name and takes any refusal other than `invalid_client` to mean that the registration is still known.
- That a wrong authorization request ends on a page of the instance and not in a redirect, and that an authorization code lasts ten minutes.
- That a revocation without a token is refused, and that revoking a token which the instance does not know is not an error.
- That a repeated idempotency key for a status that was deleted since is answered with 404.
- What Mastodon answers for an address it does not have. The simulator answers 404, with JSON under `/api/` and with a page elsewhere.
- Mastodon gives out the same token again for the same client, account and scopes. If one Mastodon account is connected to two workspaces, disconnecting one revokes the token of the other, which then has to be connected again. Phase 3b-1 has decided to accept that: section 6 says why. What is left to confirm is that Mastodon does give out the same token, which the simulator assumes. The same assumption is why a connection to Mastodon that could not be stored does not give its access back, section 6: `accessPerConnection` of the adapter. If Mastodon turns out to give every connection a token of its own, the adapter says yes there, and such a token is revoked like a session of Bluesky.
- That the adapter finds a status which it has posted. It goes by the list of the account's own statuses, walked forward with `min_id`, and by the source of a status, which has to be the text that was sent, without the whitespace around it and with nothing changed but the case of a mention and the domain of one that names the instance itself. Both are as the documentation and Mastodon's code have them. Three cases are known in which Mastodon changes more, and such a part is posted again when its answer is lost: a mention under a domain that stands for another, which Mastodon replaces by that one, a mention of an account whose server answers for it under another name, and a text like `@a@b.example@c`, which can be saved as `@a@c`. A live run does not show whether they hold: inside the hour Mastodon answers a repeated request by its key as well, and a live run cannot wait an hour. To confirm by hand: lose an answer, wait, and run the step again.
- That the account's own setting for the visibility of its statuses is `source.privacy` in the answer to `verify_credentials`, also for a token with the scope `profile` alone, and that a limited account says `limited` there. Both are from Mastodon's code. An account that is limited in the middle of a delivery posts the parts that follow as "unlisted", where the step looks for the visibility it recorded: such a part is posted again when its answer is lost.
- That every server pigy posts to lists a status with `visibility`, `spoiler_text` and `media_attachments`. A step fails, and posts nothing, at a server that leaves one of them out.
- That a request to post which Mastodon is still working on shows neither in the list nor under its key. This is why the workflow waits before it runs a step again.
- The limits that Mastodon keeps per IP address are not simulated. Registrations are limited to 5 in 10 minutes per address, and Cloudflare Workers share theirs with others. The adapter reports it as a failure to try again later.

Open since phase 3c, all of it about Bluesky, and none of it confirmed against Bluesky: there is no test account, so the live run of step 5 of "Add a network" in AGENTS.md has not been made. `test/live/bluesky.live.ts` is written and has never run. Where the simulator had to assume an answer, the place in `packages/simulators/src/bluesky` is marked GUESS, and every such mark is in this list. So are those of the adapter and the app. What the first live run has to settle:

- How to get credentials for it at all. The run needs a client that Bluesky can read, which is a pigy that is deployed, with its key, and the credentials of a session of that client with the test account. Nothing in pigy hands those out, because they are sealed in the workspace. The run has to add a way, such as a script that begins a connection with the client of the deployed pigy and takes the address of the callback from the person. A run spends the credentials that it is given: a refresh replaces the refresh token.
- That Bluesky takes pigy's client metadata as it is: the scopes of atproto's permissions, `repo:app.bsky.feed.post?action=create&action=delete`, `blob:image/*` and `blob:video/*`, the keys in `jwks`, a client name and a `client_uri`. And that the token endpoint answers with the scopes as they were asked for, which the adapter checks one by one (GUESS in the adapter): if it writes them otherwise, every connection fails.
- That a Worker can ask `cloudflare-dns.com` over HTTPS with JSON, and that the resolver wants `Accept: application/dns-json` (GUESS in the simulator, which refuses a query without it).
- That the PDS hands out nonces of its own, refuses a proof without one with 401, `WWW-Authenticate: DPoP error="use_dpop_nonce"` and a `DPoP-Nonce` header, and refuses an access token that has ended with 401 and `invalid_token` in the same header (GUESS: the body of that refusal).
- How long a refresh token lasts while nobody uses it, and a session however often it is refreshed: three months and two years in the simulator, from the code of Bluesky's server (GUESS). The adapter renews after a week, which only the timer that is not built yet would act on: section 6.
- That a refresh token that comes a second time ends the session, with no grace for an answer that was lost (GUESS, from the code of Bluesky's server).
- How long a pushed request waits for a person, five minutes, and a code for its exchange, ten minutes (GUESS). How far the time of a proof may be from the clock of the server: three minutes in the simulator (GUESS).
- That the revocation endpoint ends the whole session for a refresh token, takes a token only from the client and the key of its session, and answers 200 also when it revokes nothing (GUESS, from the code of Bluesky's server). And where a person ends the access of an app themselves, which the Channels screen names after a disconnect that could not give the access back: `bsky.social/account` (GUESS in `apps/web/src/lib/networks.ts`).
- What a PDS answers for a record that the scopes of the session do not allow, 403 with `ScopeMissingError` in the simulator (GUESS), which the adapter takes for a new connection. And for `createRecord` under a key that has a record: an error of the server in the simulator (GUESS, from the code of Bluesky's server). The adapter never sends that knowingly.
- What the PDS counts against its limits, and how it says so: the headers `RateLimit-*`, with `RateLimit-Reset` as a time in seconds since the epoch where the draft that they follow has the seconds that are left (GUESS in the simulator and the adapter, which takes both), and whether the span of a limit begins with the first write in it (GUESS). The words of the errors of a refused record (GUESS).
- That grapheme clusters are counted alike by the browser, the Workers runtime and Bluesky's servers, as for Mastodon.
- That a post written with `createRecord` under a key chosen by pigy shows in Bluesky's app with its replies, and that `getRecord` finds it at once on the PDS, which is what a step that is run again relies on.

Open since phase 3e, all of it about Threads, and none of it confirmed against Threads: there is no app at Meta and no test account, so the live run of step 5 of "Add a network" in AGENTS.md has not been made, and the adapter and the simulator were written without access to Meta's documentation, from what their author knew of it. `test/live/threads.live.ts` is written and has never run. The run takes a pigy that is deployed, because Meta sends a browser back to HTTPS addresses only, and a way to come by the token of a connection, as for Bluesky. Where the simulator had to assume an answer, the place in `packages/simulators/src/threads` is marked GUESS, and every such mark is in this list. So are those of the adapter, of `packages/core` and of the app. What the first live run has to settle:

- The permissions. pigy asks for `threads_basic`, `threads_content_publish`, `threads_manage_replies`, `threads_read_replies` and `threads_delete`. GUESS, in the adapter and the simulator: that a reply to a post of the account's own takes `threads_manage_replies`, and that reading such a reply, and listing the replies to a post, takes `threads_read_replies`. If publishing alone covers a thread of one's own, two permissions less are asked of every person and of Meta's review. And whether a list of scopes may be separated by spaces as well as by commas, which the simulator takes (GUESS).
- What a character is to the limit of 500, and what an emoji, which Meta says is counted as its bytes in UTF-8: a code point, and every code point that a pictograph is made of, with joiners, variation selectors, keycaps and the tags of flags, in `packages/core/src/network/threads.ts` and in the simulator (GUESS). Whether a post may hold at most five links, which Meta has been said to enforce and which nothing here checks. And that Threads keeps the text of a post as it was sent (GUESS), which is what a step that looks for a post compares with, but for the whitespace around it.
- What a step that is run again relies on, section 8:
  - That a container says `PUBLISHED` from the moment Threads has taken the request to publish it, and not `FINISHED` while it is still working on that. The workflow waits a minute before it runs a step again.
  - That a container still says `PUBLISHED` when it is asked days later. The adapter takes a container that Threads no longer knows for one that says nothing, and looks for the post (GUESS that Threads forgets containers at all, and that it answers for one as for any ID that it has nothing under: the code 100 with the subcode 33).
  - What publishing a published container answers. The simulator refuses it, and one that is not ready, with the code 24 (GUESS). The adapter never does it knowingly, and reads the status once more after every refusal of a request to publish, whatever its code.
  - Whether a container that was published says `PUBLISHED` for good, or `EXPIRED` a day later. The adapter looks for the post of an expired container before it makes a new one, so it is right either way.
  - Whether the lists have a post from the moment that it is published. The adapter looks three times more, ten seconds apart, before it says that a published part is not there. If the lists are further behind than that, raise `maxLooks`.
  - The fields that tell a post of plain text from another: `media_type` with `TEXT_POST`, and `is_quote_post`, as documented, and `poll_attachment` (GUESS, in the adapter and the simulator: that a post says a poll with it, and that Threads leaves a field out that a post has nothing for and does not refuse the request). If Threads refuses the field, every look for a post fails, and a delivery whose answer was lost ends as failed where it should have found its post. Whether a post with a GIF or with a link that the person attached says something else than `TEXT_POST`.
  - That the times of the posts are said to the second, and that the posts of one second are listed in an order that the adapter does not rely on: it notes the IDs of the posts of the newest second when a delivery begins.
  - That the error with the code 1 is never the answer to something that Threads refuses for good.
  - How the post of a published container is found. GUESS: `GET /me/threads` lists the posts of the account without its replies, newest first, in pages with a cursor, and `GET /{id}/replies` lists the replies to a post, also to a post that is itself a reply, with `is_reply_owned_by_me`. And that a post is in those lists as soon as it is published. If Threads names the post of a container after all, the adapter need not look, and can say yes with `tellsDeliveriesApart`.
  - That a container of text is `FINISHED` at once, or soon. The adapter waits a minute in steps of ten seconds and then leaves it to the retries of the workflow.
- The tokens. That the answer to `th_exchange_token` and to `th_refresh_token` is as documented, and whether a renewal returns a new token and leaves the old one good until its own end, as the simulator has it (GUESS), or the same token with a later end. The adapter stores what comes. That only a short-lived token is exchanged for one that lasts, that the short-lived one stays good for what is left of its hour, and that every exchange makes a token of its own (GUESS in the simulator). That a token is renewed from its second day on, which the adapter's week relies on. That the token of a request is taken in the parameter `access_token` of a form as well as of an address, and in the header `Authorization` too (GUESS in the simulator, which takes all three: the adapter uses the first two). That the parameters of a `POST` are taken as a form: the Graph API takes JSON and multipart forms too (GUESS), which the simulator refuses.
- The errors. Their form is documented, few of them are. GUESS, in the simulator and in what the adapter reads: the code 190 for every token that Threads does not take, with the subcodes 463 for one that has ended and 458 for one that was taken back, and the status 400 with it. The codes 4, 17, 32 and 613 for a limit, and the status 403. The code 2 and `is_transient` for trouble of Threads' own. The type `THApiException` and the code 100 for a parameter that it does not take, and the code 10 for a permission that the token lacks. The form of an error of `POST /oauth/access_token`, which is taken from the same address of Instagram's API. What Threads answers when the 250 posts or 1000 replies of 24 hours are used up, and whether a reply to one's own post counts as a reply. The adapter names no time to come back at for a limit: `threads_publishing_limit` would tell, and is not asked.
- The callback. That Threads says `error=access_denied` with `error_reason=user_denied` when the person declines, which is all that the adapter takes for a change of mind. That the `#_` which Threads puts at the end of the address harms nothing: a browser carries a fragment over a redirect, so the Channels screen is opened with it. What the page at Threads says of a request that it refuses, which the simulator answers with a page of its own (GUESS).
- Deleting. That `DELETE /{id}` is there for an app with `threads_delete`, answers for a post that is gone as for an ID that it has nothing under, which the adapter takes for done, and leaves the replies to a post that it deletes. The limit of 100 deletions in 24 hours is in the simulator, and a conformance run deletes about two dozen posts.
- The token in the address. Meta documents the token of a request as its parameter `access_token`, and the adapter sends it so: in the form of a `POST`, and in the address of a `GET` and a `DELETE`. The simulator also takes it in the header `Authorization` (GUESS, as the Graph API of Facebook does). If Threads does, send it there in every request and keep it out of the addresses: section 16 says what has to stay off until then. The secret of the app in the address of the exchange for a long-lived token is documented so too, and has no other place.
- The uninstall and the delete callback of the use case, section 18. GUESS: that the settings ask for both addresses, and that they may be addresses at which nothing answers. If Meta calls the uninstall callback when a person removes pigy in their Threads settings, a route for it could mark the channel as needing a new connection at once, with `channel.lapse`, where it now takes the next post or the next renewal to find out. The request is signed with the secret of the app, which the route has to check, and it goes under `/connect/`, a prefix that the service worker knows.
- A reply under a post of somebody else. The simulator refuses a reply in a thread that the account did not begin (documented, as its author knew it: `threads_manage_replies` is for the replies to the account's own threads. GUESS: the code 10 and the words of the refusal). pigy never sends one.
- Disconnecting. That there is no way for an app to end a token or to remove itself from an account. If there is one, `disconnect` calls it, and `accessPerConnection` can be yes if every connection has a token of its own, which the simulator has (GUESS). And where a person ends the access of an app in the settings of Threads, which the app names as "Settings → Account → Website permissions" (GUESS, in `apps/web/src/lib/networks.ts`).
- The address of a profile, `https://www.threads.com/@<username>`, which the adapter makes (GUESS: Threads moved from threads.net to threads.com, and both have been seen), and that the `permalink` of a post is an HTTPS address at one of them. The address to send a person to is `https://threads.net/oauth/authorize`.
- That an app in development is refused for an account that is no tester of it on Meta's own page, with nothing that reaches pigy's callback. It is not simulated.

Open since phase 3d, all of it about LinkedIn, and none of it confirmed against LinkedIn: there is no application and no test account, so the live run of step 5 of "Add a network" in AGENTS.md has not been made, and the adapter and the simulator were written without access to LinkedIn's documentation, from what their author knew of it. `test/live/linkedin.live.ts` is written and has never run. Where the simulator had to assume an answer, the place in `packages/simulators/src/linkedin` is marked GUESS, and every such mark is in this list. So are those of the adapter, of `packages/core` and of the app. What the first live run has to settle:

- The version of the API. The adapter sends `LinkedIn-Version: 202610` (GUESS that it exists), and the simulator has the three months around it (GUESS). The first thing to read before a run is which versions LinkedIn has, and what changed in the Posts API since the one that this was written against.
- What a character is to LinkedIn's limit of 3000: a UTF-16 code unit in pigy and in the simulator (GUESS, in `packages/core/src/network/linkedin.ts`), so that an emoji outside the basic plane counts as two. And that the limit is on the text as it shows, without the backslashes of its escapes (GUESS). If LinkedIn counts the escaped text, a post with many reserved characters that pigy takes is refused by LinkedIn, with a message that says so.
- That every reserved character of the little text format can be escaped with a backslash and then shows as itself, also `#`, `@`, `_` and `*` in the middle of a word, and that nothing else needs escaping. The simulator refuses a reserved character that is not escaped, which LinkedIn does not: it publishes something else.
- That LinkedIn refuses a post that repeats one with a 422 whose message is "Content is a duplicate of urn:li:share:…", for about ten minutes (GUESS: the window, and the words, which the adapter reads the ID from), and that a post which was deleted is not counted. If the message does not name the post, or says more than that, the delivery fails with what LinkedIn said instead of being taken for sent. No test of the live run shows the refusal: the conformance suite runs no step of an adapter twice that does not repeat safely. Whoever makes the run posts the same text twice within a minute and reads what LinkedIn answers. What section 8 leaves open about whose post a refusal names stays open whatever the run finds.
- Whether `POST /rest/posts` works for an application that has only the self-serve product "Share on LinkedIn". LinkedIn's documentation of that product shows the older `POST /v2/ugcPosts`, and the Posts API is documented with the marketing products. If the run is refused there, the adapter posts through `/v2/ugcPosts`, whose body, headers and errors differ, and the simulator follows.
- Whether a link whose address has reserved characters still works after escaping: a Wikipedia address ends in `_(…)`, and the adapter writes a backslash before `_`, `(` and `)` wherever they stand. If LinkedIn does not take the backslashes out of an address before it makes the link, addresses have to be left as they are.
- That every exchange of a code makes a token of its own and leaves the others good (GUESS), and what the token endpoint answers: the scopes with commas between them (GUESS, the adapter takes commas and spaces), `token_type`, which the adapter takes as optional. That a request for authorization without a scope, and one with a wrong `response_type`, end the way the simulator has them (GUESS).
- Everything about `/oauth/v2/revoke` beyond its address and its three parameters (GUESS, all of it): that it ends the one token and no other of the member, that a token which LinkedIn does not know is no error, and that the token of another application is refused. `accessPerConnection` of the adapter follows from the first and from the item before: it is no until both are confirmed, section 6. And what `/oauth/v2/introspectToken` says of a token that is not the application's (GUESS).
- The refusals of the API: their statuses, their numbers and their words (GUESS, in `api.ts` and `index.ts` of the simulator). The adapter goes by the status alone, except for a repetition: 401 for a new connection, 426 for a retired version, 429 for a limit, 5xx for `uncertain` where it asked to post. That a status from 400 to 499 always means that nothing was posted is what the adapter counts on, also for an answer that it does not read to its end (GUESS). What LinkedIn answers when it fails (GUESS: 500 with JSON), for a post that never was when it is deleted (GUESS: 404, which the adapter takes like the 204 of a post that is gone), and for a request to the versioned API without `X-Restli-Protocol-Version` (GUESS: taken in the first version of the protocol, which the simulator does not have and refuses).
- The limits: LinkedIn shows them per application in its developer portal and documents no number. The simulator has 150 posts per member and day (GUESS, the limit of the API before the Posts API), and a day that begins at midnight UTC, which is documented. The adapter names the next midnight as the time to try again. The limits per application are not simulated.
- That a post made with `visibility: PUBLIC`, `distribution.feedDistribution: MAIN_FEED` and `lifecycleState: PUBLISHED` shows as an ordinary post of the member, that its ID comes in the header `x-restli-id` as a `urn:li:share:…` or `urn:li:ugcPost:…`, and that `https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/<ID>/` is its address.
- That `sub` of `/v2/userinfo` is the ID in `urn:li:person:<sub>`, as the documentation of the sign-in says, and that `picture` is an HTTPS address that a browser can show for as long as the channel lasts. If it expires, the channel shows initials until the account is connected again.
- Where a member ends the access of an application themselves, which the Channels screen names after a disconnect that could not give the access back: Settings & Privacy → Data privacy → Permitted services (GUESS in `apps/web/src/lib/networks.ts`). And the address at which LinkedIn shows the member who is signed in their own posts, which the Sent screen links to for a delivery that is unknown: `https://www.linkedin.com/in/me/recent-activity/all/` (GUESS, same file).
- That the live run works at all. It needs an application, and the token of a test member with the scopes `openid`, `profile` and `w_member_social`, which the token generator of the developer portal makes, and it posts for the connections of that member: LinkedIn has no posts that only their author sees. Its test of an access that ends does not run live.

Open since phase 4b, about what is in a text, and none of it confirmed against a network: the simulators were written, like the adapters, by somebody without access to the pages of the documentation while writing. The first live runs settle these too. The conformance suite publishes a text with a link, a mention and hashtags, and a live run shows whether the post reads as it was written.

- **Bluesky.** That a PDS takes the facets as pigy writes them, and that Bluesky's app shows them: `#link`, `#mention` with a DID, `#tag` without its sign, over byte offsets. That an `app.bsky.embed.external` without `thumb` shows as a card. That Bluesky's app takes for a link without a scheme what pigy's list of top-level domains takes, which is shorter than the list of every one that exists: a name that is missing is text in pigy's post where Bluesky's own app would have linked it. The simulated PDS is stricter than a real one on purpose: it refuses facets that overlap, that break a character, and a mention of an account that is not there.
- **Mastodon.** The pattern of a hashtag, written down from memory of Mastodon's code (GUESS in the simulator): pigy's own takes no middle dot inside a tag, and no tag after an underscore. That the card of a status is there when the status is read again later: the simulator has it at once (GUESS).
- **LinkedIn.** That a hashtag is written `{hashtag|\#|tag}`, with a reserved character in its name escaped (GUESS), and what LinkedIn does with a tag in a script other than Latin. That an article with `source`, `title` and `description` and no `thumbnail` is taken and shows as a preview, and what a post looks like whose text also has the address. What LinkedIn takes for a link in a text, and whether an address without a scheme is one: pigy takes it for text there (GUESS). The simulator links what the simulated Mastodon links.
- **Threads.** That the first address in the text of a post becomes its link preview where no `link_attachment` is named, that the first hashtag becomes its topic tag where no `topic_tag` is named, and that both show in `link_attachment_url` and `topic_tag` of a post (GUESS, from the documentation as its author knew it). What Threads takes for a tag, for a mention and for a link: the simulator and pigy's detection are both guesses, and agree with each other only. That a text post with a preview still says `TEXT_POST`, which the look for a published post relies on.
- **Pages.** That five seconds and 256 kilobytes are enough for the pages that people link to, and how many of them refuse a request that names itself "pigy (link preview)". A page in another encoding than UTF-8 gets no card, whether its answer or a `<meta>` tag of it says so. One that is in another encoding and says so nowhere is read as UTF-8, and gets a card with wrong letters where it has letters beyond ASCII. A site that shows a page only to a browser that runs its scripts gets none either.
- **The zone that pigy is deployed on.** That `global_fetch_strictly_public` sends a `fetch` of the Worker to another host of its own zone through the front door, with the firewall and Cloudflare Access in the way, on a `workers.dev` address and on a custom domain: section 16. To confirm on the real deployment, with a host of the zone that Access protects and a link to it in the editor: the preview has to say that the page could not be read. And what a `fetch` to pigy's own host does there, which the step of the workflow does not refuse.
- **A run that is stopped while `cards` runs.** The workflow catches nothing around the step `cards`, section 6, so what the platform throws out of it to pause, restart or terminate a run is thrown on whatever its words are, and nothing rests on them. What rests on the platform is that it throws there at all: that a `step.do` which is under way when a run is paused does not return as if nothing had happened, with the run going on to the next step. The local engine throws after the step, and then runs nothing more. To confirm on the platform, with a LinkedIn channel of a test member, where the step after `cards` posts: publish a post with a link to a page that takes some seconds to answer, pause the run in the dashboard or with `wrangler workflows instances pause` while `cards` is running, and see that the run says `paused`, that nothing is posted to LinkedIn while it does, that the target stays `sending`, and that the post goes out once, with its card, after `resume`. Then the same with `terminate` in place of the pause: nothing is posted, and the delivery ends as `uncertain` within a minute. And that a step which never fails is not given up on by the platform for a reason of its own: look through the runs of a week for one whose `cards` step failed, which the log of the Worker shows as a delivery that lost track with nothing sent.
- **The length of a hashtag.** pigy holds a tag to 64 characters and 640 bytes on every network, which is Bluesky's rule. What Mastodon, LinkedIn and Threads take is not documented.

**Stats (phase 10).** None of it has met a network: the adapters and the simulators of section 14 were written without access to the documentation while writing, from what their author knew of it, and the live runs of the conformance suite have their cases for stats. Marked GUESS where it is assumed:

- **Mastodon.** That `quotes_count` is in a status from Mastodon 4.5 on, and left out by an instance from before rather than null: the adapter takes both, and says null. That `replies_count` counts a reply of the account itself under a public post and not under one for followers, as its code read, which is why pigy takes nothing off. That a status for followers is shown with its counts to the token of its author.
- **Bluesky.** That the public app view at `public.api.bsky.app` answers `app.bsky.feed.getPosts` for anybody without a limit that a workspace reaches: it is limited per address, and every pigy reads from the addresses of Cloudflare. That it shows a post some seconds after its PDS has written it, which the first read, at once, can meet: the adapter takes a post that its PDS has and the app view does not show for a `retry` of that post alone, and the read is made at the next moment. That it leaves out a post whose author is taken down by moderation, which a read gives up at its last moment. That an invalid AT URI is refused, as the simulator does, and not left out.
- **Threads.** The form of the insights of a media object: `data` with a `name` and the value as the one entry of `values`, which is what Meta documents for a media object, where the insights of an account have `total_value`. The names `views`, `likes`, `replies`, `reposts` and `quotes`. That a token without `threads_manage_insights` is refused with the code 10, which the adapter takes for an account to connect again, and nothing else for that code. That the insights of a post are there at once after it was published, with zeros. That `replies` counts the replies of the account itself, as the simulator has it: of a post that begins a thread, every reply in the thread, as Meta documents it, and of a reply, the replies to it. What reading insights counts against the limits of the app, which Meta keeps per app and not per account: a pass reads 20 at most a minute, of one workspace.

**The public API (phase 6).** To confirm on the deployed Worker, phase 11b:

- That the rate limiting binding does what the local runtime does: the tests reach the limit of the limiter that Miniflare has, which counts exactly and in one place. The platform counts per location and with a delay. How far over the limit a key gets there is to be seen, and that `namespace_id` 1001 is free in the account.
- That requests without a good key cannot keep D1 busy: each costs one read. Cloudflare's own protections are in front of the Worker, and a rule of the zone can limit `/api/v1` by address if it comes to that.
- The shapes against Buffer's, for somebody who ports a script: they were written from this plan and not from Buffer's documentation, which was not read again in phase 6. Where `thread` is under `metadata`, the names in `pageInfo`, and what Buffer's `dueAt` takes are the ones to look at.

**Scheduling (phase 5).** To confirm on the deployed Worker, phase 11b:

- How punctual the alarm of a Durable Object is in production, and what it does over a deploy. The tests run the alarm by hand, and one end-to-end test waits for it in the local runtime. A post goes out when the alarm runs, and one that is found more than an hour late is held or moved (section 9), so an alarm that is minutes late is seen and nothing worse.
- That the time zone data of the Workers runtime keeps up with that of browsers. Where it is behind, the server computes a slot by the old rule until the runtime is updated, and refuses a zone that it does not know yet with a notice.

**Launch (phase 11a).** Built without a Cloudflare account, so none of it has met the platform. To confirm with the first deploy, `SETUP.md`:

- That the Worker sees an address with `https:` at the deployed host, which is what it sends `Strict-Transport-Security` by.
- That the binding `ASSETS` answers the Worker as the local runtime does: a file with the headers of `_headers`, and the app shell with the status 200 for a path without a file. `apps/worker/src/assets.ts` tells the two apart by the content type.
- That a page loads no slower for its scripts going through the Worker. If it does, the cure is on the side of the cache: Cloudflare keeps what the Worker answers only where it is told to.
- That Workers Logs lets the fields of a line of JSON be filtered by when the line is logged as text, and what it keeps of a request with invocation logs off. GUESS: that a line which is a string of JSON is read as JSON.
- That `wrangler d1 migrations apply DB --remote` finds the database by its name, which the configuration has in place of an ID, and applies without asking where nobody can answer.
- That an API token from the template "Edit Cloudflare Workers" with "D1: Edit" deploys the workflow as well.
- That the hashes of the policy hold on the deployed address: Cloudflare serves `index.html` as the build wrote it.
- What Cloudflare keeps of a workspace and for how long, and whether anything can be restored without code in the object: `SETUP.md`, "Backups".
- Safari and Firefox under the policy. It is tested in Chromium alone. `'self'` in `connect-src` covers the sync socket in the browsers of the last years, and did not in Safari before 15.4.

**Images (phase 7).** Built against the simulators and the local Workers runtime, with no account at Cloudflare and no test account at a network. To confirm, and the guesses that the code marks with `GUESS`:

- **The Images binding.** The tests make real variants and small pictures with the binding of the local runtime, which has another library behind it than the platform. To confirm on the deployment: that `input(stream).transform({ width, height, fit: "scale-down" }).output({ format, quality, anim: false })` gives an image that is never larger than the original, turned as its Exif says, without metadata, and still for a GIF that moves; what a WebP with transparency becomes as a JPEG; how large an input the binding takes, which the documentation has as 70 MB and 100 megapixels, against pigy's 20 MiB and 64 million pixels; and what it costs, which is one transformation for each small picture and for each try at a variant, seven at most.
- **A file that cannot be drawn** is refused after the small picture could not be made twice, because the local binding fails with one code for everything. The platform has a code for each kind of failure: once they are known, a file should be refused only for the ones that are about the file.
- **R2.** That a multipart upload whose parts are 5 MiB and whose last part is smaller completes as locally, that `resumeMultipartUpload(...).complete` fails for an upload that is completed already, which the intake looks for before it asks, and what an upload that is never completed or aborted costs until the alarm of its workspace gives it up after a week: a lifecycle rule of the bucket that aborts incomplete multipart uploads after eight days would be a second net. That aborting an upload that is completed, aborted already or dropped by such a rule either succeeds, as locally for the first two, or fails with the code 10024, `NoSuchUpload`, at the end of the message of the error, which is what `noSuchUpload` of `apps/worker/src/media/keys.ts` takes for an upload that is gone (GUESS): every other failure is asked again an hour later, for eight days after the upload was given up, `dropUploadForMs`, and then the row lets go of it and the log says so. If the guess is wrong, every upload that was given up is asked for eight days and logged as not dropped, and the lifecycle rule is what drops its parts: a bucket without the rule keeps them.
- **Bytes that no row names.** The alarm changes the row first and deletes from the bucket after. A bucket that fails in between leaves bytes that nothing serves and that the log names. Nothing looks for them: a sweep of the bucket against the rows is for later, section 21.
- **Metadata.** The copy that pigy keeps leaves out what `packages/core/src/media/image.ts` lists at its top, and keeps what a decoder draws with. To confirm with files from real cameras and phones: that no place and no serial number is left in a JPEG, a PNG and a WebP, that the colours are what they were, with the ICC profile kept, and that a photo that was taken on its side still stands upright in each network's app. HEIC is not taken: a phone that shares one has to convert it, which the picker of iOS does for a web page.
- **Mastodon.** GUESS: which files an instance works on after it has answered. Its code does so for video and audio, and the simulated instance does so for a GIF, so that the adapter's wait is tested. GUESS: that a file which is in no status is deleted after a day, from the code. To confirm in a live run: the 202 and the 206, `description_limit` from 4.4 on, that a status is refused while a file is processed and with which words, and that `media_attachments` of a status come in the order of `media_ids`.
- **Bluesky.** GUESS: that a PDS refuses a record whose blob it no longer has with a 400 whose message has the word "blob", from the code of the reference server, which is what makes the adapter upload again, and how long a PDS keeps a blob that no record refers to, an hour in the simulation. GUESS: the 2000 characters of a description, which is the limit of Bluesky's own app and not of the lexicon. GUESS: that a GIF is a still picture in an image embed. To confirm in a live run: that `blob:image/*` among the scopes lets `uploadBlob` through, that the bytes of one request are not read twice when the server asks for a nonce, which the adapter answers by sending the request again, with its bytes, and how the app shows `aspectRatio`.
- **LinkedIn and Threads**, for phase 7b. GUESS, all of it, in `defaultMediaLimits` of `packages/core/src/network/limits.ts`: LinkedIn's 8 MB and 36,152,320 pixels, its types and its 4086 characters of `altText`, and Threads' 8 MB, JPEG and PNG, ten to one, and 1000 characters of `alt_text`. And for Threads: when it fetches `image_url`, at the creation of the container or later, which decides how long a signed link has to last, a quarter of an hour now, and from which addresses, which a firewall in front of pigy must let through.

## 20. Moving to a custom domain later

pigy starts on `https://pigy.<your-subdomain>.workers.dev`. Switching to a custom domain later means adding the domain to the Worker, which is all that pigy itself needs, because it takes its address from each request, and changing `APP_URL` where it is set, and then:

- Updating the redirect addresses in the Google, LinkedIn and Meta apps.
- Reconnecting Bluesky channels, because the Bluesky client ID is the app's address. LinkedIn, Mastodon and Threads channels keep working.
- Reinstalling the PWA and re-enabling push on each phone, because both belong to the address.
- Pointing API clients at the new base URL. Keys stay valid.
- Leaving off, in the zone of the domain, what changes a page on its way to the browser: the content security policy allows the inline script of the app shell by the hash of its exact text (section 16).

Posts, drafts, schedules and stats are unaffected. `SETUP.md` has the steps.

## 21. Possible later additions

- A public "try the demo" link with a temporary anonymous sandbox.
- A posting schedule per account, as in Buffer.
- For the API: webhooks for what became of a post, a route that tries a failed delivery again, a text for one of two accounts of the same service, and a key that reaches both workspaces of a person.
- Editing a scheduled post in place, so that a queued post keeps its place when a typo is fixed (section 9).
- Reordering the queue by dragging, and a week view of the calendar.
- LinkedIn company pages and LinkedIn stats, which need the same LinkedIn approval.
- An export of a workspace, and a way to bring one back from the history that Cloudflare keeps of the storage of a Durable Object: today whoever runs pigy can restore D1 and not a workspace (`SETUP.md`, "Backups").
- A sweep of the bucket of the images against the rows of the workspaces, for the bytes that no row names after the bucket failed in the middle of a deletion (section 10).
